                                    UNITED STATES MARINE CORPS

                                                                                                           12 February 2021

                                                  FOREWORD
Marine Corps Reference Publication 3-10F.2, Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and
Controller, provides the techniques and procedures for requesting, adjusting, and controlling
mortars, artillery, naval gunfire, and close air support. Changes in technology, equipment,
organization, and methodologies have necessitated the update of this publication.
It is intended as a field reference for supporting arms observers, including mortar and artillery
forward observers, naval gunfire spotters, and forward air controllers. Additionally, it serves as a
study guide and field reference for personnel seeking information on supporting arms procedures.
This publication supersedes Marine Corps Reference Publication 3-10F.2, Supporting Arms
Observer, Spotter, and Controller, dated 15 October 1998; and cancels change 1 dated 4 April
2018 and erratum dated 2 May 2016.
Reviewed and approved this date.

                                                C. A. TAVUCHIS
                                          Colonel, U.S. Marine Corps
                           Commanding Officer, Marine Artillery Detachment

Publication Control Number: 144 000261 00
DISTRIBUTION STATEMENT A: Approved for public release; distribution is unlimited.

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 1
      THE COMPANY FIRE SUPPORT TEAM

The Marine Corps provides a maneuver company with observers, spotters, and controllers who are
specifically trained to plan, call for, adjust, and control supporting arms. The organization that
provides maneuver companies with these observers, spotters, and controllers is the company fire
support team (C-FST). The C-FST is the company commander’s primary means of planning and
employing combined arms in support of maneuver.

RESPONSIBILITIES

The primary responsibilities of a C-FST are to––

• Locate targets.
• Plan and coordinate all fire support at the company level.
• Adjust artillery, rockets, mortars, and naval surface fire support (NSFS).
• Report battlefield/battlespace information.
• Operate within a maneuver element or from an artillery observation post (OP). The C-FST

   must occupy the best OP or other position that the tactical situation permits. See appendix A for
   more information about OPs.
• Assist the commander in overall fire support planning and coordination matters.
• Advise the commander on the employment of artillery.
• Plan fire support for the company, including both normal and quick fire planning that has been
   approved by the company commander.
• Be familiar with standing operating procedures (SOPs) of the supported and supporting unit,
   including fire support SOPs and the company’s combat SOP.
• Know the tactical situation, including the scheme of maneuver or plan of defense, and the
   enemy situation.
• Maintain continuous observation of the zone of action of the supported unit.
• Determine and refine target locations, engagement criteria, and trigger points, as required.
• Make a terrain sketch of the area, when possible, to improve accuracy and speed in locating
   targets. When setting into a defensive position, the C-FST should construct a visibility diagram
   to pinpoint those areas where targets could remain unseen from its vantage point. See appendix
   B for more information about constructing a visibility diagram.
• Report all target and intelligence information by using the size, activity, location, unit, time,
   and equipment (SALUTE) format.

                                                                    1-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Report actions of enemy aircraft.
• Report the results of fire.
• Conduct crater analysis (see appendix C).
• Ensure constant communication with either the battery or battalion fire direction center (FDC)

   and the battalion fire support coordination center (FSCC).
• Ensure that the FDC and battalion FSCC are informed of the tactical situation, plans, and

   location of the supported unit.
• Determine the C-FST location to within 100 meters (i.e., a 6-place grid) at all times.
• Transmit periodic position reports (POSREPs), coordinated with the company commander, to

   both the supporting FDC and the battalion FSCC.

             Note: In the absence of a unit SOP or special instructions, apply the
             following: report position changes of every 500 meters on foot or every
             2,000 to 5,000 meters (considered a fast-moving operation).

• Provide security for the team by ensuring the proper use of cover, concealment, observation,
   and fire.

• Observe and direct other types of fire support when necessary (e.g., mortars, NSFS, close air
   support [CAS]).

• Assist in training Marines with nonsupporting arms military occupational specialties (MOSs)
   to request fire support, as required.

TASK ORGANIZATION

Doctrinally, the C-FST contains a C-FST leader, a company fire support officer (FSO), a
forward air controller (FAC), a joint terminal attack controller (JTAC), a joint fires observer
(JFO), a fire support Marine, an 81-mm mortar forward observer (FO), and radio operators. The
C-FST can be collocated with the combat operations center during operations, or its members may
be distributed with forward elements or at an OP.

A C-FST’s composition varies depending on the mode of operation, availability of personnel, and
type of force supported (e.g., a mechanized unit with limited vehicle space, a foot-mobile unit).
See table 1-1 for the personnel comprising a C-FST.

                   Table 1-1. Company Fire Support Team Personnel.

                             Position/MOS        Number

                   C-FST leader/0302          1

                   Company FSO/0802           1

                   JTAC/8002                  1

                   JFO/0861                   1

                   Fire support Marine/0861   1

                   81-mm FO                   1

                   Field radio operator/0621  2

                                      1-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Company Fire Support Team Leader
The C-FST leader is the link between the supporting arms agencies and the company
commander. The C-FST leader is responsible for the planning and execution of fires and receives
advice and guidance from the team members who are considered resident experts in artillery,
NSFS, mortars, and CAS. These individuals are the direct link to their respective supporting
arms agencies and are heavily involved in the planning, coordination, and execution of fires.

During operations, C-FST personnel should be available to provide assistance to the C-FST leader
as needed. The C-FST leader receives guidance for fires from the commander and information
from all elements of the unit, which aids in decision-making. If the C-FST leader becomes
separated from the commander, communication must be re-established and maintained.

It is imperative that the C-FST members understand the complex job of the C-FST leader and are
prepared to assume this position if required. The C-FST leader has two primary responsibilities:
managing fire support, which involves planning, integrating, and executing fire support, and
operating the C-FST.

Managing Fire Support. The C-FST leader ensures that fire support planning is accurate and
integrated into the scheme of maneuver and that fires are executed effectively.

Fire Support Planning. Fire support planning is the continual process of analyzing, allocating, and
scheduling fire support. Once the C-FST leader determines the commander’s intent for fire
support, the fire planning process is begun with the supporting arms agencies. Supporting arms
agencies representatives keep the C-FST leader informed of the capabilities and limitations of
their various fire support assets. Fire support agencies prepare estimates of supportability and
keep the maneuver commander updated on their respective agencies’ current capabilities,
specifically munitions availability (e.g., amount of smoke and illumination, types of fuzes) and
priority of fires.

Integrating and Executing Fire Support. To ensure proper fire support integration and execution, the C-FST
leader works concurrently with the company commander to plan fires in support of the developing
maneuver plan. Once the plan is complete, the C-FST leader determines the task requirements
for all of the fire support assets and ensures that the requests are forwarded to the appropriate fire
support agencies. The C-FST leader must––

• Ensure that all fire support means are integrated into the company commander’s maneuver plan.
• Construct the timeline for the fires to support the maneuver and synchronize those fire support

   elements into the plan.
• Separate the fire support elements by time or space to ensure the safety of friendly forces. This

   is accomplished by studying battlefield geometry (e.g., location of friendly, enemy, adjacent
   units; terrain), attack geometry (i.e., how the various fire support assets will attack their
   targets), and the company scheme of maneuver to attain a clear overall picture of the battlefield
   and efficient coordination of agencies.
• Ensure that the various supporting arms agencies are coordinated with maneuver for maximum
   combat power, mitigating the risks of numerous assets operating in the same battlespace.
• Construct and integrate a fire support plan that will support maneuver.

                                                                    1-3


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Execute the fire support plan. The C-FST leader supervises the agencies involved to ensure that
   their responsibilities are properly executed. If problems arise during execution of the scheme of
   maneuver, the C-FST leader will revise the fire support plan and effectively deconflict fire
   support agencies.

Operating the Company Fire Support Team. To effectively operate the C-FST, the C-FST leader
develops and maintains situational awareness, knows the capabilities and limitations of the team,
synchronizes the efforts of the team, and establishes priorities of work and procedures to ensure
smooth operation by––

• Ensuring that information is received from team members. All members of the C-FST should
   keep the C-FST leader informed of their actions to ensure good situational awareness.

• Positioning the C-FST near the company commander for optimum observation of the
   battlefield, whether centralized or decentralized, in order to––
    Facilitate rapid coordination of fires with the company scheme of maneuver.
    Allow C-FST personnel to observe as much of the battlefield/battlespace and as many
        targets as possible.
    Allow C-FST personnel to see the company’s lead trace elements and facilitate the safe,
        timely clearance of fires.

• Developing a routine for C-FST actions, or an SOP, to facilitate smooth execution. An
   effective C-FST leader will designate personnel and assign specific tasks to be accomplished.
   For example, when there is contact with the enemy, the following procedures may be used:
    The fire support Marine determines grid and elevation to the enemy positions.
    The 81-mm FO determines direction and distance to the enemy positions.
    The JTAC requests air support and develops the 9-line brief.
    The company FSO determines stay above or stay below parameters.
    The 81-mm FO adjusts fire with the 81-mm mortars.

• Developing teamwork for CAS. The development of SOPs for CAS attacks is crucial because
   there are many tasks that must be accomplished simultaneously to facilitate the successful
   attack on a target. To ensure that the C-FST operates smoothly, the C-FST leader will designate
   tasks to each member. For example:
    The company FSO spots and determines corrections from the mark.
    The 81-mm FO acquires the aircraft and talks the rest of the team onto it.
    The JTAC talks to the aircraft over the radio.

• Ensuring proper time management. Time management is all-encompassing and requires the
   C-FST leader to ensure that situational awareness is maintained through effective
   communication with the C-FST members. Accurate information must be passed to the C-FST
   leader quickly and clearly by all team members to ensure that timely decisions can be made.
   This is essential when aircraft check in with a very short time on station or when an emergent
   target must be engaged before it disappears.

                                                                    1-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Company Fire Support Officer
The primary responsibility of the company FSO is to plan and coordinate supporting fires at the
company level. The company FSO, assisted by a JFO/0861 and fire support Marine, performs the
following duties:

• Plans, controls, and adjusts artillery, naval, and mortar fires onto targets. The company FSO
   should plan for priority targets, suppression of enemy air defenses (SEAD), and final protective
   fires (FPFs) when developing any plan.

• Provides the company commander and C-FST leader with all pertinent information regarding
   artillery and NSFS employment. The company FSO must know how much and what type of
   artillery and/or ammunition is available and should be prepared to advise on all types of
   indirect fire support, including naval surface fires, rockets, and mortars.

• Establishes and makes frequent contact with the other observers (i.e., artillery and mortar FOs)
   and any JTACs supporting the company.

Joint Terminal Attack Controller
Although not part of the aviation combat element (ACE), the JTAC is an essential element of the
Marine air command and control system and the tactical air control party (TACP). The JTAC–

• Provides direct air support to task-organized maneuver elements and is the primary terminal
   controller of aircraft.

• Maintains radio communications with assigned support aircraft from a forward ground position.
• Directs each CAS aircraft’s attack on the target, which aids target identification and enhances

   troop safety.
• Employs a laser system for designating targets, as required.
• Controls aircraft during the terminal phase of CAS missions to assist in target identification

   and minimizing the danger to friendly troops.
• Operates with the assault units of the battalion.
• Observes and locates targets of opportunity.
• Directs air strikes against targets.
• Advises the supported company commander on the proper employment of aircraft assets.
• Gathers and reports all information of an intelligence nature, including target damage assessments.
• Stays abreast of the supported unit’s plans, positions, and needs.
• Stays abreast of the enemy situation, location of friendly units, and current target list.
• Requests and controls aircraft in accordance with the Marine air-ground task force (MAGTF)

   master air plan.

Joint Fires Observer
A JFO is a certified and qualified Service member who can—

• Request, adjust, and control surface-to-surface fires.
• Provide targeting information in support of type 2 and type 3 CAS terminal attack controls.
• Perform terminal guidance operations.
• Assist in fire planning.

                                                                    1-5


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Terminal guidance is different from terminal attack control. Terminal guidance operations are
electronic, mechanical, voice, or visual communications that provide approaching aircraft and/or
weapons additional information regarding a specific target location. Enemy targets, such as
mobile, high-payoff targets that are difficult to locate from the air, are often more visible to ground
forces. Small ground elements can sometimes search for, identify, and precisely report the location
of targets and provide a precise battle damage assessment (BDA) of attacks on targets that may
otherwise be obscured or hidden if using Global Positioning System (GPS), laser designators, or a
combination of both systems.

             Note: Terminal guidance operations do not include the authority to clear
             aircraft to release ordnance and should not be confused with terminal
             attack control.

Fire Support Marine
Duties of this position include the following:

• Assist the company FSO in the planning, control, and execution of all fires from indirect fire
   support systems (i.e., NSFS, artillery, mortars, and the multiple launch rocket system,
   [referred to as MLRS]).

• Provide the company FSO with all pertinent information regarding indirect fire support system
   employment.

• Provide targeting information.

Additional duties include operating from within an FSCC at the battalion, regiment, division, or
MAGTF level and employing fire support command and control systems. Fire support Marines in
an FSCC maintain status boards and plot updates on situational maps.

81-Millimeter Mortar Forward Observer
Duties of this position include the following:

• Plan, control, and adjust mortars onto targets.
• Provide the company commander and C-FST leader with all pertinent information regarding

   mortar employment.
• Provide targeting information.

COMMUNICATIONS

Fire support personnel use several means of communication to ensure effectiveness in mission
execution.

Radio
Company Fire Support Team Nets. The C-FST uses radio traffic as its primary means of
communication. All members of the team must be communications experts who are able to

                                                                    1-6


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

maintain the equipment to the highest degree of performance, troubleshoot, and enhance
performance with the use of directional antennas and other expedient methods. Several nets are
employed by the C-FST.

Artillery Conduct of Fire Net. This very high frequency (VHF) net provides a means for observers to
request and adjust artillery fire. The artillery battalion FDC establishes as many as three conduct
of fire (COF) nets and acts as net control of each. The following applies:

• The battalion fire support team located at the battalion FSCC monitors and receives traffic on
   the COF net to coordinate requests for fires.

• The artillery battalion may identify a COF net for each maneuver battalion to facilitate
   continuity of fire support during battery displacements.

• The artillery battalion FDC receives all calls for fire and designates which firing battery or
   batteries will provide fire support to the maneuver battalion. This allows the artillery battalion
   to quickly mass on larger targets and manage the assets of the battalion as a whole.

• Each battery FDC maintains a COF net and acts as net control when conducting autonomous
   operations. Each net is monitored by the battalion FDC when present. Autonomous operations
   are also conducted by batteries attached to a battalion landing team.

• For coordination purposes, the battalion fire support team located at the battalion FSCC
   monitors all traffic on this net.

• The fire support coordinator at the infantry regiment may enter the net, as required.

The stations on this net include the following:

• Direct support artillery battalion FDC.
• Firing battery.
• Battalion FSCC.
• C-FSTs.
• Regimental FSCC, as required.
• Artillery units assigned reinforcing mission, as required.

Division or Ground Combat Element Artillery Air Spot Net. The artillery regiment, which is considered a
battalion when operating independently, establishes this VHF net for aerial observers to adjust
artillery fire. The artillery regiment is the net control; however, for a specific mission, control may
be passed to the battalion or battery FDC conducting the mission. When this net is in use,
company FSOs monitor for targets in their unit’s zone. The company FSO may use this net to
coordinate with an aerial observer for the attack of targets in the company’s zone. The stations on
this net include the following:

• Net control.
• Aerial observer.
• Artillery battalion, as required.
• Firing battery, as required.
• Company FSOs, as required.
• FSCCs, as required.

                                                                    1-7


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Maneuver or Company Tactical Net. Monitoring VHF maneuver tactical nets is not a requirement for the
C-FST. However, the ability to monitor multiple channels with the current communications
equipment enables the C-FST to use company nets to increase situational awareness without
requiring additional radios.

Infantry Battalion Mortar Net. The VHF infantry battalion mortar net provides the means for requesting,
adjusting, controlling, and coordinating the fires of the 81-mm mortar platoon. Stations on this
net include––

• FOs.
• 81-mm mortar section FDC.
• 81-mm mortar representative located in the battalion FSCC.
• Mortar platoon commander (net control).

Tactical Air Control Party Nets. The TACP communicates by the radio nets described in the
following subparagraphs, as well as by other means (such as landlines) when available.

Tactical Air Request/Helicopter Request Net. This high frequency (HF) net provides a means for units to
request immediate air support from an air control agency. The FSCC at each echelon monitors this
net and coordinates with the air control agency to approve, deny, or modify the requests. The
following may be passed over this net:

• Target damage assessments and emergency helicopter requests.
• Preplanned air support requests if other means are not available.

The stations on this net include the following:

• TACPs.
• Direct air support center (DASC).
• FSCCs.
• Navy tactical air control center (TACC).
• Tactical air coordinators (airborne) (TAC[As]).
• Forward air controllers (airborne) (FAC[As]) or other terminal controllers.
• Assault support coordinators (airborne).
• Other MAGTF agencies, as required.

Tactical Air Direction Net. This net is a primary ultrahigh frequency (UHF) net, with a secondary VHF
capability depending upon the aircraft. It provides a means for directing aircraft in CAS missions
by the terminal controller and a means for the air control agency to brief support aircraft on target
information and handoff to the terminal controller or TAC(A).

Tactical Air Control Party Local Net. This VHF net provides a means for coordination between the air
officer in the battalion FSCC and the JTACs.

                                                                    1-8


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Joint Terminal Attack Controller Nets. The JTAC maintains communication with the air officer
located in the battalion FSCC and the air control agency. Additionally, the JTAC establishes and
maintains positive communications with assigned support aircraft from a forward position.

Helicopter Request Net. This HF/VHF net provides a means for TACPs of helicopterborne forces to
request immediate helicopter support from the DASC or the helicopter direction center.

Helicopter Direction Net. This UHF/VHF/HF net provides positive control of inbound and outbound
helicopters in the amphibious objective area.

Related Aviation Nets
Naval Surface Fire Support Ground Spot Net. This primary HF/secondary VHF net provides a means for
requesting and adjusting NSFS and passing vital information between stations. The following
applies to this net:

• The naval gunfire liaison officer (NGLO) usually provides net control and monitors all traffic
   for purposes of coordination.

• Within the infantry, the regiment may monitor the net, as required.
• A frequency is allocated to each battalion that is assigned a direct support ship.
• A general support ship assigned to support the battalion will enter the NSFS ground spot net for

   the duration of the mission.

The stations on this net include the following:

• Infantry battalion FSCC provides net control.
• Separate infantry battalion FSCC, as required.
• Company FSO/NSFS spot team.
• NSFS ships.

Shore Fire Control Party Local Net. This VHF net provides direct communications between the NSFS
spot team and the NGLO in the battalion FSCC. It provides a means for coordinating the
employment of the spot team and for coordinating NSFS matters. It provides communications
between the NSFS spotter and the spot team when separated. The following applies to this net:

• The NGLO exercises net control from the infantry battalion FSCC.
• The stations on this net include the infantry battalion FSCC and the C-FST/NSFS spot team.

Naval Surface Fire Support Air Spot Net. This UHF or VHF net, the frequency of which is dependent on
the aircraft, is used by an aerial observer to request and adjust NSFS. The following applies to
this net:

• The NGLO at the infantry battalion, regiment, and division enters this net. When an aerial
   observer is working in conjunction with ground units, the appropriate fire coordination agency
   will exercise net control.

• The supporting arms coordination center (SACC) is net control prior to control and
   coordination of responsibilities being transferred ashore.

                                                                    1-9


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

The stations on this net include any aerial observers and the supporting ship as follows:

• Division FSCC (net control when an aerial observer is working in conjunction with ground units).
• SACC afloat (net control before control and coordination responsibilities have been

   transferred ashore).
• Navy TACC, as required.
• MAGTF force fires coordination center (FFCC), as required.
• Infantry regiment FSCC, as required.
• Infantry battalion FSCC, as required.
• Separate infantry battalion FSCC, as required.
• Company FSO or NSFS spot team.
• NSFS ships, as required.

Automated Systems
Automated systems use a burst transmission capability, which minimizes vulnerability to enemy
radio direction finding. The automated system is used by the C-FST to transmit calls for fire, fire
plans, and battlespace information.

             Note: Manually-packed devices, such as the Target Location, Designation,
             and HandOff System Version 2 (commonly called THS V2), enable users
             to rapidly prepare, transmit, and receive both text and graphic messages
             over radios or field wires.

Wire
The company FSO will use wire communications—to the extent permitted by the tactical
situation, time, and personnel available—as follows:

• Static defensive posture allows the use of a land-line between the company commander and a
   remote OP.

• Wire may be used between the company FSO and fire support personnel when operating
   separately.

• Wire communications may be limited when acting in the capacity of an NSFS spotter.
   However, when the situation permits, a land-line may be established between the NSFS spotter
   and shore fire control party or between the spotter’s OP and the NGLO.

Messenger
The company FSO may use a messenger if the tactical situation permits. This method is a more
secure method of sending fire plans as it reduces radio traffic and hampers enemy direction finding.

                                                                   1-10


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

EMPLOYMENT

The employment of observers, spotters, and controllers is coordinated by the battalion fire support
coordinator, with the assistance of the battalion FSO, the NGLO, an air officer, and an 81-mm
mortar representative at the supported battalion’s FSCC. During operations, the C-FST should
maintain communication with the company commander to provide assistance as needed because
the commander receives reports from all elements of the unit and must decide where fire support
is most urgently needed. If separated from the commander, communications must be immediately
re-established.

Offensive Operations
In offensive operations, supporting arms personnel should be in a position to maintain
communications with the company commander and be able to observe the battlespace. Depending
on the nature of the mission and the situation, specific supporting arms personnel, including the
company FSO or JTAC, may need to be collocated with the commander to maintain voice
communictions.

Personnel from the C-FST may be located with the company’s lead elements. In a mechanized
unit, special consideration should be given to where C-FST personnel are located in order to best
support operations. Supporting arms personnel assist the supported unit commander in the offense
by performing the following duties:

• Plan fires to support the company’s attack and its reorganization following seizure of the
   objective. Fires are planned on both confirmed and suspected enemy positions and key terrain.
   Refer to Marine Corps Tactical Publication (MCTP) 3-10F, Fire Support Coordination in the
   Ground Combat Element, for a detailed discussion on planning fires.

• Execute the fire support plan.
• Call for fire on targets of opportunity.
• Provide battlespace information regarding the enemy situation.
• Update SALUTE reports.
• Provide additional channels of communication.
• Aid in land navigation.

Movement to Contact. During movement to contact operations, supporting arms personnel perform
the following tasks:

• Plan fires on critical points along the route of march (e.g., potential ambush sites, priority targets).
• Maintain their orientation by using land navigation skills and terrain features for reference

   points that are identifiable on both the ground and the map.
• Conduct immediate action drills on contact.
• Provide periodic POSREPs to the FDC, fire support officers, air officers, and other observers

   during movement, as required.

                                                                   1-11


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Attack, Exploit, and Pursue. When enemy contact has been established, supporting arms personnel
position themselves in the most advantageous location to observe the actions of the supported
unit, conduct fire missions, and coordinate fires for the company commander. Supporting arms
personnel should––

• Be positioned on a terrain feature that offers the most advantageous observation of the
   company’s advance.

• Be familiar with immediate action drills on contact, in accordance with the SOP, operation
   order (OPORD), or situational reporting.

• Ensure that fires support maneuvering elements by enabling elements to close with and destroy
   or bypass the enemy.

• Assess the benefit of fires versus their risk to friendly forces.
• Consider the effects of shell/fuze combinations.

Consolidate. When supporting arms personnel are consolidating on the objective, the following
tasks apply:

• Send reports (e.g., new POSREPs, situation reports) to the battalion FSCC.
• Establish defensive fires and fires to disrupt an enemy counterattack in the fire plan.
• Continue fire planning and make revisions or changes for the tactical situation as necessary.
• Maintain continuous observation and fire support for follow-on operations, as directed by the

   company commander.

Defensive Operations
Supporting arms personnel plan their fires to support the company’s plan of defense. Artillery,
rocket, mortar, NSFS, and air fires are planned to break up the attack, repel the assault, limit or
destroy penetrations, and support counterattacks. During defensive operations, supporting arms
personnel perform the following tasks:

• Plan fires forward of, on, and behind the supported unit’s defensive positions, obstacles, and
   avenues of approach.

• Plan FPFs or priority targets for both artillery and mortars and make adjustments if the
   situation permits.

• Select an OP that provides good visibility of the company’s area of responsibility. Fire support
   personnel may be positioned with the company FSO, NSFS spotter, platoon, or forward
   element.

• Execute the fire plan, including battalion targets, in the area of responsibility as follows:
    Verify that the company fire plan has been received by the battalion FSO.
    Verify the number of targets that were approved.
    Verify establishment of target reference points (TRPs).
    Establish a fire support plan for alternate and supplementary company positions.
    Screen fires.

                                                                   1-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Additionally, the C-FST constructs a visibility diagram of the observed area from an established OP.
The diagram is prepared in duplicate. The C-FST retains one copy and the other copy is forwarded
through the battalion FSCC to the battalion intelligence officer. At the battalion level, coverage is
planned for those areas that are not under ground observation (see appendix B for more
information about visibility diagrams).

Patrol Support
Providing fire support for patrols is an important function for supporting arms personnel,
particularly the artillery and mortar observers. The C-FST keeps the battalion FSCC informed of
friendly patrol plans, including routes and schedules. Supporting arms for the patrol are
coordinated before the patrol begins. Planning by supporting arms personnel should include the
following actions:

• Obtain the size of the patrol, times of departure and return, mission, routes and checkpoints,
   and any special instructions from the company commander and patrol leader.

• Assist the patrol leader in fire planning, following the guidance provided by the company
   commander. Fires are planned along the route of critical areas or key terrain by using priority
   targets when available.

• Process the fire plan. Provide a copy of the fire plan to the patrol leader and secure any special
   communications instructions or signals that may be used.

• Make arrangements to maintain communications, as required.

Retrograde Operations
Supporting arms personnel (e.g., company FSO, JTAC, NSFS spotter) are selected depending on
the situation. These personnel may remain with the company commander throughout retrograde
operations. However, portions of the teams may displace with the withdrawing forces to maintain
continuity of observation and fire. Fire support personnel may be employed in an overwatch
position. Supporting arms personnel must perform the following tasks:

• Plan fires for the period when the company has control of fires.
• Plan fires for rearward passage of lines and handoff of fire plans, target lists, and terrain

   visibility diagrams.
• Acquire and pass control of fires through the battalion FSCC and air officer.
• Maintain observation of the area of responsibility during control of fires.
• Maintain orientation during movement.

                                                                   1-13


---



---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 2
                 TARGET LOCATION

Providing the supported unit with effective fire support depends on the observer’s ability to locate
targets quickly and accurately. The elements of target location are direction, distance, and altitude.
Terrain/map analysis is key to accurate target location. Because terrain/map analysis is applicable
to the mortar FO, artillery observer/NSFS spotter, JFO, and JTAC, the use of the word “observer”
in this chapter applies to all of them.

FUNDAMENTALS OF TARGET LOCATION

Understanding and applying the skills of map reading are essential. Observers need these skills to
perform terrain/map analysis because the map is used to determine what terrain observers expect
to see in the battlespace. Observers—

• Scan the map and terrain continually and attempt to associate features that are observed on the
   ground with those located on the map.

• Use the map to determine the location of targets, friendly positions, and their own position and
   to remain oriented during movement.

• Determine direction, distance, and altitude for target location by using the map and other tools.

For an accurate terrain/map analysis, observers perform the following tasks:

• Locate within 100 meters of their actual location on the ground each time they move.
• Use prominent terrain features to relate potential target areas to grid locations on the map.
• Conduct a thorough study of the terrain by drawing a terrain sketch and associating distances

   and directions to prominent features that are indicated on the sketch.

Observers orient themselves and their maps utilizing a compass and declination diagram. The
information in the following subparagraphs can also be applied to other common laser range
finders (CLRFs).

Direction
Direction is the most important element of target location and is an integral part of terrain/map
association and adjustment of fire. Direction is a term used by observers to indicate the bearing
from the observers’ position to the target.

                                                                    2-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Units of Measurement. Direction can be measured in mils or degrees. Degrees can be converted to
mils when required.
Mil. A mil is a unit of horizontal, clockwise, angular measurement that is equal to 1/6400th of a
circle. The mil unit of measurement is used because of its accuracy and the mil-relation formula’s
ability to convert angular deviation into horizontal distance. Mils may be measured from the map
(grid north) or by using a compass (magnetic north).

             Note: The standard unit of measurement is mils grid and need not be
             specified in the call for fire. Mils are transmitted as four digits to alleviate
             confusion; other units of measurement must be specified.

                                     Example: Mil unit of measurement
                 Direction 1600. (Direction is expressed in mils grid.)
                 Direction 1600 mils magnetic. (Unit of measurement must be specified
                 if different from the standard.)

Degree. A degree is a unit of horizontal, clockwise, angular measurement that is equal to 1/360th of
a circle. Degrees can be measured from grid, magnetic, or true north. An observer using degrees in
the call for fire must specify the type.

                                   Example: Degree unit of measurement
                 Direction 090 degrees magnetic.

Degrees may be converted to mils by multiplying the number of degrees by 17.8.

                                    Example: Degrees converted to mils
                 090 degrees x 17.8 = 1602 mils.

                                                                    2-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Types of Direction. There are several types of direction that can be used by an observer: an
observer-target line (OTL), a gun-target line (GTL), cardinal or intercardinal directions, and an
arbitrary reference feature (a known point).
Observer-Target Line. The OTL is an imaginary straight line from the observer to the target. It is the
most commonly used direction for locating targets and conducting adjustments. The standard unit
of measurement is mils grid. The measurement of the OTL’s direction allows for the observer to
determine observer-target (OT) direction. The OT direction is determined as accurately as
possible and transmitted to the nearest 10 mils or nearest degree.

                                       Example: Observer-target line
                 Direction 0640. (A direction of 0642 mils would be transmitted this
                 way, with mils grid understood.)

Gun-Target Line. The GTL is an imaginary straight line from the guns to the target. The GTL is
frequently used by aerial observers as a spotting line for adjustments of fire. It can also be used by
ground observers, particularly when positioned on or near the GTL. When the GTL is used as a
direction, the standard unit of measurement is mils grid. Observers who desire to use the GTL as a
spotting line transmit direction gun-target line in the call for fire.
Cardinal or Intercardinal Direction. This type of direction involves the use of one of the eight
cardinal/intercardinal directions (i.e., north, northeast, east, southeast, south, southwest, west, and
northwest) and is often used by aerial observers. It can also be used by ground observers, but the
accuracy in adjustment is reduced (see figure 2-1).

                              Example: Cardinal or intercardinal direction
                Direction northeast.

                                                                    2-3


---

                  MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                            0 or 360
                         0 or 6400 mils

315 or 5600 mils                                45 or 800 mils

                         N

                     NW                  NE

270 or 4800 mils  W                          E     90 or 1600 mils

                     SW                  SE

                         S

225 or 4000 mils                                135 or 2400 mils

                             180 or 3200 mils
                  Figure 2-1. Cardinal Direction.

Arbitrary Reference Feature. Observers may use a natural or man-made feature (e.g., the point at which
a bridge crosses the north-south river, the intersection of two roads) to reference target location
and/or adjustment of fire. This type of direction may be used by aerial observers and in situations
where the transmission of the OTL may compromise a ground observer’s location. Observers may
use an arbitrary feature to adjust fire by extracting a new direction from the arbitrary reference
feature to the target.

                                Example: An arbitrary reference feature

                 Direction 1800. The observer then references all adjustments from
                 an imagined line originating at a road intersection and ending at the
                 target. The actual OTL is different.

Direction Determination. Observers can measure direction by using a compass or an instrument
that has been oriented for direction, a known reference point, a map, or the ground using––

• Measuring devices. Measuring devices include—
    The lensatic and M2 compasses, which are graduated in 20-mil increments.
    The CLRF, which is capable of providing both direction and distance to the observer. When
        calibrated, it provides directional readings in 1-mil increments.

                         2-4


---

        MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• A reference point with a known direction. By determining the angular deviation in mils
   between the reference point and the target, a direction can be computed by applying the
   deviation to the known direction as follows (see figure 2-2):

    Add the mils to the known direction if the target is to the right of the reference point and
        subtract if the target is to the left. This is referred to as the right, add, left, subtract (RALS)
        method.

    Use any instrument with a reticle pattern to determine direction by measuring angular
        deviation and applying RALS.

• Map scaling. Observers may scale direction from a map to within an accuracy of 10 mils by
   using a protractor.

• Ground estimating. Estimating on the ground is the least accurate method, but it can be used
   when observers require speed or do not have a map and/or compass. Observers should be able
   to visualize a cardinal/intercardinal direction, realizing that the sun and moon rise in the east
   and set in the west.

        Figure 2-2. Measuring Angular Deviation.

Angular Deviation Determination. Observers must be able to determine direction by using angular
deviation or measurement as described in the following subparagraphs. Angular deviation is used
to determine horizontal and vertical distances to the target and to make corrections in adjustment.

Reticle Patterns. Reticle patterns are found on optical instruments and are the primary means of
measuring angular deviation. Binoculars and laser CLRFs can measure angular deviation to an
accuracy of +/- 5 mils. Any object within the field of view with a known direction can serve as a
reference point.

                                               2-5


---

MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Hand Measurement of Angles. Hands and fingers may be used for angular deviation or measurement in
situations where speed is essential or when there are no other tools available. An observer fully
extends an arm with the palm of the hand pointed toward the target area or reference point. By
using the values contained in figure 2-3, the observer can determine an approximate angular
deviation or measurement.

          A  B

300  180     125

100  70      30

Numbers Represent Mils

Figure 2-3. Hand Measurement of Angular Deviation.

The observer should determine the actual values of the hand and finger measurements to increase
accuracy. The observer does this by comparing a known deviation between two objects with that
derived from the hand or fingers.

Object Measurement of Angles. Observers may also use common objects to measure angles as shown in
figure 2-4. Without a more accurate means available, and knowing the actual size of potential
targets and the measurements shown in figure 2-4, observers can quickly determine range with
reasonable accuracy (see figure 2-5).

Distance
Once the direction has been determined, observers must determine distance to the target. Distance
is the horizontal space between a reference point or a ground observer and a target. It is also used
in adjustment of fire. The meter is the standard unit of measurement for distance. Other units of
measurement may be used if specified in the call for fire. Though it is typically the most difficult
variable to determine in target location, observers can employ CLRF, sound, or distance estimation.

Common Laser Range Finder. The CLRF provides the quickest, most accurate means of
determining distance.

Sound. When determining distance by sound, two methods may be used: flash-to-bang and
audible sounds.

Flash-to-Bang. To determine distance by using the flash-to-bang method for sound ranging, count
the number of seconds between a round detonation (flash) and the sound of the impact (bang);
multiply this time by the speed of sound (350 mps).

                                      Example: Formula to determine
                                 distance using the flash-to-bang method

                Flash-to-bang (seconds) x 350 = distance in meters.

By using the flash-to-bang distance to a round impacting in the battlespace, observers can
associate this distance on the ground with a future target. For example, if the flash-to-bang
distance is estimated at 4,000 meters and a target was seen between the observer and the previous
point of impact used in the flash-to-bang method, the observer knows that the target must be
closer than 4,000 meters. Similarly, the flash-to-bang method can be used to estimate the OT
factor during adjustment.

This method can also be used to determine the distance to the muzzle flash of an enemy weapon.
When combined with a good direction, observers can achieve accurate target location data.

                                                                    2-8


---

                   MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Audible Sounds. There will be occasions when observers will not be able to see the flash of a weapon
or a round detonation. To estimate distance in these situations, observers can use common, audible
sounds. Table 2-1 provides frequently heard noises with estimated distances.

             Table 2-1. Audibility of Various Actions.

    Maximum                           Sound
Distance (meters)

100–200            Human speech

300                Stakes being pounded into the ground by hand

500                Stakes being pounded into the ground mechanically

500–1,000          Trenches being dug (e.g., shovel striking rocks or iron)

800                Trees being cut or trees falling

1,000–2,000        Automatic gunfire

3,000              Single rifle shot

Distance Estimation. Observers must estimate the distance if a more accurate method is not
available. The degree of accuracy depends on several factors, including terrain relief, time
available, and experience of the observer. Normally, the longer an observer remains stationary, the
more accurate this method becomes. The following subparagraphs describe ways to determine
distance by estimation.

Mental Distance. A mental distance estimate is made by using a known unit of measurement.
Distance is estimated to the nearest 100 meters by determining the number of known units of
measurement between the observer’s position and a target. For example, a football field that is 100
yards long can be used as a known unit of measurement for determining the distance between an
observer’s position and a target, and it may be required to progressively estimate for longer
distances. To use this distance estimation accurately, the observer determines the number of units
of measurement to an intermediate point and doubles the value.

Conditions that make objects appear closer are as follows:

• Bright light.
• Clear air at high altitude.
• Background is in contrast with the color of the object.
• Observer is looking down from a height.
• Observer is looking over a depression, most of which is hidden.
• Observer is looking down a straight feature, such as a road.
• Observer is looking over water, snow, or a uniform surface, such as a cultivated field or desert.

Conditions that make objects appear more distant are as follows:

• Poor light or fog.
• Only a small part of the object can be seen.
• Background is similar in color to the object.

                   2-9


---

                      MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Observer is looking over a depression, most of which is visible.
• Observer is kneeling or sitting, especially on a hot day, when the ground is moist.

Good Visibility. When visibility is good, distances can be estimated by using the appearance of tree
trunks, their branches, and foliage as seen by the naked eye in comparison with map data. Tables
2-2 and 2-3 may be used as guides for wooded terrain and urban environments, respectively.

Known Dimensions. Distance can be estimated by using known dimensions of vehicles and the mil-
relation formula: (W = R x m).

By using the width of a vehicle appearing perpendicular to an observer as the lateral distance (W)
and measuring the width in mils (m), the distance can be determined by solving the formula for
range in thousands (R), or (R = W) m. This data, when compared with map data, will assist an
observer in estimating distances. The dimensions of selected equipment are provided in table 2-4.

Table 2-2. Use of Trees and Foliage for Estimating Distance.

  Distance                                 Tree Description
  (meters)    Trunk and main branches are visible

1,000

              Foliage appears in a cluster-like shape

2,000         Daylight may be seen through the foliage
              Trunk is visible

              Main branches are distinguishable

              Foliage appears as a smooth surface

3,000         Outlines of the foliage of separate trees are distinguishable
              Lower half of trunk is visible

              Branches blend with foliage

4,000         Foliage blends with adjoining trees

              Trunk and branches blend with foliage and appear as a
              continuous cluster, smooth in appearance

5,000 plus    Movement of foliage caused by wind cannot be detected
              The entire area covered by trees appears smooth and dark

Table 2-3. Use of Various Objects for Estimating Distance.

       Distance (meters) Object Identified by the Unaided Eye

       1,000          Lone tree trunk

       1,500          Individuals and horsemen

       3,000          Chimneys on rooftops

       4,000          Windows in houses

       4,000–5,000    Individual houses in a populated area

       8,000–9,000    Villages and individual houses

       15,000–18,000  Large houses, towers, and steeples

    Table 2-4. Example of Estimating
Distance by Using Known Dimensions.

                                     Dimensions (Meters)

Equipment                      Side View Front View

Tank (T-80) chassis            7.4   3.4

Tank (T-62) chassis            6.5   3.4

Reconnaissance vehicle (BRDM-2) 5.7  2.4

Terrain Study. The observer should always use a terrain/map analysis when estimating distances.
When the observer is looking in a specific direction, the estimation of distance can be enhanced by
studying the terrain to associate what is actually seen on the ground with what is seen on the map.
The use of an observed fire (OF) fan helps the observer to associate map and compass direction,
maintain orientation for direction, and refine distances. Particular emphasis should be given to
color contrasts of terrain features seen along the OTL.

For example, the distance across successive ridgelines or depressions in the distance may only be
identified by slight changes of color to the eye. Different colors of grass might reveal a hidden
terrain feature, such as a stream.

                   Example: How to estimate distance by using known dimensions

                  An observer sees an armored personnel carrier (BMP) and measures
                  its width as seen from a side view as 2 mils.

                  By using the following formula, the observer determines the distance:

                  R = W ÷ m where: m = 2 mils (measured by binoculars).

                  W = 6.8 meters (known dimension of side view).

                  R = 3,400 meters (6.8 ÷ 2 = 3.4).

Elevation
The third element of target location is elevation. Elevation is the vertical distance of a level, a
point, or an object considered to be a point, measured from mean sea level (MSL). The meter is
the standard unit of measurement for elevation. There are three methods of measuring elevation:

• Map spot. If measured from a map spot, the elevation of a target is determined by use of
   contour lines and the contour interval of the map.

                                                                   2-11


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Vertical shift. Elevation may be determined as a vertical shift in meters from the elevation of
   the observer or from a known point to the target. The vertical shift, presented as either up or
   down, uses the same principle of angular deviation in the mil-relation formula.

• Vertical angle. Laser polar missions specify the vertical angle in mils. This allows the FDC to
   incorporate the vertical shift with the associated distance for accurate target location.

                   Example: Measuring elevation to determine a target location

                Elevation 480, up 50, and vertical angle 3.

TOOLS TO ASSIST THE OBSERVER

The following tools are used by the observer in target location and terrain and map analysis: the M2
compass, binoculars, GPS, OF fan, terrain sketch, and visibility diagram.

M2 Compass
The M2 compass is the most accurate compass for determining direction (+/- 10 mils). It is a
multipurpose instrument used to obtain azimuth readings and vertical angles. The magnetic
needle (i.e., the white end of the needle) shows magnetic north. The reading is taken from the
black end when the bubble is centered in the circular level. The azimuth scale adjuster assembly
rotates the azimuth scale to introduce the declination constant.

Declinating the M2 Compass. The steps for declinating the M2 compass from a surveyed
declination station that is free from magnetic attractions are as follows:

             Step 1. Set the M2 compass on a tripod over the orienting station and center the
             circular level.
             Step 2. Sight on the known, surveyed azimuth marker.
             Step 3. Use the azimuth adjuster scale and rotate the azimuth scale until it indicates
             the same as the known, surveyed azimuth.
             Step 4. Recheck the sight picture and azimuth to the known point. Once the sight
             picture is correct and the azimuth reading is the same as the surveyed data, the M2
             is declinated.

To establish a field-expedient declination of the M2 compass, use the azimuth adjuster to set off
the grid-magnetic angle (this is shown on the bottom of all military maps). Once the grid-magnetic
angle has been set off on the azimuth scale, the M2 compass is declinated.

             Note: Once declinated, the M2 compass measures grid azimuths.

                                                                   2-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Measuring Azimuths. The steps for measuring an azimuth are as follows:

             Step 1. Hold the compass in both hands and at eye level. Brace arms against the body
             and the rear sight closest to your eyes to read the azimuth scale by reflection. Place the
             cover at an angle of approximately 45 degrees to the face of the compass so that the
             scale reflection can be viewed in the mirror (see figure 2-6).
             Step 2. Level the instrument by viewing the circular level in the mirror.
             Step 3. Sight on the desired object and read the azimuth indicated on the reflected
             azimuth scale by the south-seeking compass needle (the black end).

Front Sight   Rear Sight

     Rear       Reflected
Sight Holder  Scale Image

                                Figure 2-6. Measuring an Azimuth With the M2 Compass.

Measuring Vertical Angles With the M2 Compass. The steps to measure a vertical angle with the M2
compass are as follows:

             Step 1. Hold the compass on edge with both hands and at eye level. Brace arms
             against the body and the rear sight closest to your eyes.
             Step 2. Place the cover at approximately 45 degrees to the face of the compass so that
             the elevation scale reflection can be seen in the mirror (see figure 2-7).
             Step 3. Sight on the target or crest. Center the elevation scale tubular level with the
             lever on the back of the compass by viewing the elevation scale in the mirror.
             Step 4. Read the elevation in mils on the elevation scale, measure again, and record
             the average.

Binoculars
In addition to measuring angular deviation, binoculars enable observers to focus on distant
objects. When using binoculars, the following considerations apply:

• Binoculars must be held parallel to the deck and steadied against the body or other property.
• Observer should determine, memorize, and record the interpupillary and depth settings to allow

   for quick adjustment.
• Protective plastic lens cap on the binoculars can be removed to increase the field of vision for

   observers who wear glasses.
• Masking tape can be used on the metal retaining ring to prevent scratching the glasses.
• Diopter adjustment ring can be taped in the correct position so that the observer does not have

   to adjust the diopter setting at every use.

Global Positioning System
The GPS is a space-based navigation system that provides worldwide, continuous, all-weather,
three-dimensional (3D) position information. The GPS includes satellites and receivers. The current
receiver is the AN/PSN-13 Defense Advanced Global Positioning System Receiver (DAGR).
The DAGR can provide the observer with grid location and navigational information. To use the
DAGR, the following should be observed and/or verified:

• Cryptographic keys. The proper cryptographic keys must be loaded into the DAGR to enable it
   to use the precise positioning system. The precise positioning system must be used to achieve
   the necessary accuracy and to avoid enemy electronic warfare measures. If the cryptographic
   variable is not loaded, DAGR is not as accurate.

• Datum. The map datum should be the same as the operational datum being used by all other
   maneuver, fire support, and target acquisition units. If the same datum is not used, significant
   position errors may occur.

• Coordinates. The military grid reference system (MGRS) format is preferred because it is the
   standard used by survey systems and most fire control systems.

• Elevation. Mean sea level is the preferred elevation because most military maps use it as the
   basis for the elevation scale.

• Units of measurement. Meters are the preferred selection for units of measurement because
   most military maps refer to distance and elevation in meters.

• Almanac data. Almanac data must be one day old. If almanac data is not one day old, satellite
   vehicle or timing errors not noted by the DAGR are a probability.

• Figure of merit. The figure of merit (FOM) is an accuracy estimation of the data displayed by
   the DAGR. The FOM ranges from one through nine. Figure of merit level one (called FOM
   one) is the best accuracy estimation displayed by the system, and FOM nine is the least
   accurate. For observer positioning, only coordinates determined with FOM one will be
   considered to be accurate.

• Mode of operation. The DAGR offers three choices as modes of operation—fixed, continuous,
   and averaging. The averaging mode yields the most accurate data and is preferred when
   determining a position for indirect-fire weapons employment.

                                                                   2-15


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Position. Position verification to check for gross errors should always be performed by
   separate means. The DAGR does not relieve observers of their land navigation
   responsibilities. Observers must be able to establish their own location and that of targets without
   the aid of GPS for the following reasons:
    GPS receivers rely on electronic line of sight with satellites. The DAGR must be able to
        acquire at least four satellites so that a 3D position can be determined. Dense foliage,
        buildings, mountains, and canyons will mask the GPS signal and cause the receiver to fail.
    Multi-path distortion or reflected signals may occur if the receiver’s antenna is tilted away
        from a satellite. This causes a reflected signal to be received that has more power than the
        direct signal. Coordinates determined under these conditions may be inaccurate by as much
        as several hundred meters.
    Electronic warfare can jam GPS signals.

                                                   WARNING
             Azimuth determined with the DAGR is for navigation only. The DAGR
             azimuth is not accurate enough for use when establishing directional
             control and should never be used for orienting observation devices.
             Tests show that a DAGR-determined azimuth may be in error by as
             much as 200 mils.

             For more information, refer to Technical Manual 11-5820-1172-13&P,
             Operator and Maintenance Manual Defense Advanced GPS Receiver
             (DAGR) Satellite Signals Navigation Set.

Observed Fire Fan
The OF fan is a transparent overlay that can be used to refine distance on the map (see figure 2-8).
The OF fan—

• Is not used to determine direction.
• Is used to assist the observer to identify on the map what is seen on the ground.
• Is available in the supply system as Graphic Training Aid 6-7-3, Observed Fire Fan.

             Note: The OF fan has 17 radial lines that are 100 mils apart and cover a total
             area of 1600 mils. The radial lines represent OT direction. The OT distance
             is represented by arcs marked on the fan every 500 meters and labeled
             every 1,000 meters, beginning at 1,000 meters and extending to 6,500 meters.

Orienting the Observed Fire Fan on the Map. To prepare the OF fan, perform the following steps:

          Step 1. Place the vertex of the OF fan over the observer’s location.
          Step 2. Place the center radial line in the direction of the center of the observer’s sector

             of observation.

                                                                   2-16


---

                MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Step 3. Move the fan slightly until one of the radial lines is parallel to a grid line. The
direction of that radial line is the same as that of the grid line. For example, a radial
line parallel to a north-south grid line, with the OF fan oriented generally north, would
be direction 6400 or 0.

Step 4. Attach the OF fan in position on the map by using a piece of tape along one
edge of the fan. Now the OF fan can be lifted when required without disturbing its
orientation.

Step 5. Number the radial line of known direction with a marker pen or grease pencil,
dropping the last two zeros (e.g., 6400 would be 64). Then label every second radial
line with the appropriate direction. Radial lines are 100 mils apart. Direction increases
to the right and decreases to the left.

Using the Observed Fire Fan. To determine target location, perform the following steps:

             Step 1. Determine direction and distance by using a method previously discussed.
             Step 2. Plot the data on the OF fan by finding a radial line corresponding to the OT
             direction. Visual interpolation is required if the direction plots between two radial
             lines. Follow the radial line or interpolated radial line until the estimated distance is
             scaled off.
             Step 3. Compare the terrain near the target with the terrain of the estimated point on
             the map. If the terrain matches, the target location is accurate. If terrain does not
             match, search along the radial line until the terrain matched (see figure 2-8).

Terrain Sketch
The terrain sketch is a rough panoramic drawing of the area of observation (see figure 2-9). It is used
as a rapid means for the observer to record and/or identify predetermined directions to reference
points. A properly constructed terrain sketch aids the observer in conducting an organized study of
the terrain and provides a rapid means of orienting relief personnel. The terrain sketch should
include the following:

• Skyline and/or horizon.
• Objects or features that are prominent, such as draws, hill masses, streams, wood lines,

   ridgelines, roads, buildings, battlespace debris, or other readily identifiable man-made items
   or features.
• Directions and distances to the prominent objects or features and labeling of reference and
   known points. If time permits, elevations and grid locations should also be recorded for
   these features.
• Name, date, and OP grid location should be placed in the lower right-hand corner of the terrain
   sketch with a north-seeking arrow prominently displayed for quick orientation.

Visibility Diagram
To locate targets and report battlespace information effectively, the observer must be able to see
within the area of observation. Because of terrain features, certain areas will not be visible from
the observer’s location. A visibility diagram is a sketch of the area of observation that is drawn to
scale by the observer (see figure 2-10). It shows those portions of the terrain that cannot be
observed from a given OP and will pinpoint those areas that cannot be seen by the observer.

Constructing the Visibility Diagram. The construction of the visibility diagram requires a detailed
study of the terrain. Observers should construct visibility diagrams as soon as possible, but in a
fast-moving operation this may not be feasible. In this case, observers will need to locate any
areas that cannot be observed. Possible alternatives would be to use a verbal report that provides
azimuths and distances to screening obstacles or to provide a grid location and radius of an area
that cannot be observed. Appendix B provides the steps that are involved when constructing a
visibility diagram.
