Using the Visibility Diagram. The most important use of the visibility diagram is that it helps to
coordinate the complete visual coverage of the battlespace. Ideally, the visibility diagram is
reviewed by the company commander and dispatched through the FSCC to the intelligence
section. The visibility diagram is used to—

• Evaluate target area coverage to minimize and/or cover any gaps.
• Determine the best locations and orientations for other target acquisition resources.
• Reduce errors in reporting target locations.
• Aid in defensive fire planning and provide the company commander with a battlespace

   perspective for employment of organic weapons systems to cover dead space and identify
   concealed avenues of approach.

METHODS OF TARGET LOCATION

Terrain/map analysis is essential when determining target location. The results of this analysis (i.e.,
direction, distance, and altitude) provide the basic data for determining a target’s location.
Observers select a method of target location based upon whether their position is known by the
firing unit, the availability of known reference points in the battlespace, and tools that are
available for target location. The methods of target location are polar plot, grid coordinates, and
shift from a known point. Observers, using any of these methods, must locate targets within 200
meters of the actual target location.

             Note: Regardless of the method of target location, map datum information
             is a prerequisite to accuracy. The observers and the firing unit must be
             operating within the same ellipsoid, datum (e.g., World Geodetic System
             1984, which is maintained by the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency),
             and MGRS grid zone. This can be determined from the marginal information
             that is located on the observer’s map before conducting a mission.

Polar Plot
When using the polar plot method, the observer describes the target location in relation to the
observer’s own position. This method is fast and can be performed without a map, though CLRFs
greatly enhance the accuracy of the polar plot.

                                                                   2-20


---

              MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

North

        Direction 0370     T-62 Tank with
       Distance 3200 m  Dismounted Infantry

       370 m  Estimated Distance 3200 m

                            OP (Plotted By Firing Unit)

                          NOTE:
                          The FO has determined that there is no obvious altitude difference.

                                    Figure 2-11. Polar Plot Method of Target Location.

The primary disadvantage of this method is that the observer’s position must be known by the
firing unit (i.e., battery, mortar platoon, or ship). This may require transmission of the observer’s
location and elevation before sending the call for fire (see figure 2-11).

The steps to determine polar plot data are as follows:

             Step 1. Determine the OT direction to the nearest 10 mils. Laser data will be
             determined to the nearest mil.
             Step 2. Determine or estimate the distance to the target to the nearest 100 meters.
             Laser data will be determined to the nearest 10 meters.
             Step 3. Determine the vertical difference between the observer and the target. The
             vertical difference will be transmitted in the call for fire as an up or down shift from
             the elevation of the observer to the elevation of the target if the shift is 35 meters or
             more. Any vertical shift that is determined will be transmitted to the nearest 5 meters
             or 20 feet. The vertical shift increases the accuracy of the target location and should
             always be given if possible.

             Note: When employing an instrument that provides greater accuracy than
             the minimum requirements, it is acceptable to determine and transmit the
             more accurate data.

                                                                   2-21


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Laser polar missions provide the quickest, most accurate means of target location. The preferred
mission type for laser polar missions is fire for effect. If an observer’s tactical situation precludes
a fire for effect mission, a one-round adjustment is adequate prior to entering the fire for effect
phase. In addition to the laser polar missions providing a more accurate target location, the
subsequent corrections are also processed differently from regular missions. Target location is
determined by the FDC using vertical angle to the nearest mil and incorporating distance as the
slant range. Laser polar data determines direction to the nearest mil, distance to the nearest 10
meters, and vertical angle (versus shift) to the nearest +/- 1 mil.

Grid Coordinates
Observers can locate a target by using the grid system of the military map. If an observer has
conducted a thorough terrain/map study, the grid coordinate method of target location is
recommended. There is no requirement for an observer’s position to be known by the firing unit.
Observers can locate targets to an accuracy of 100 meters using a 6-digit grid. When additional
accuracy is required (e.g., known points), observers should locate targets to the nearest 10 meters
with an 8-digit grid. Always include the 100km grid zone identifier in front of the grid. The steps
to determine grid coordinates are as follows:

             Step 1. Determine the OT direction to the nearest 10 mils.
             Step 2. Determine or estimate the distance to the target to the nearest 100 meters.
             Laser data will be determined to the nearest 10 meters.
             Step 3. Plot direction and distance on a map or imagery and use terrain association to
             determine the grid coordinates.
             Step 4. Add grid zone identifier to the front of the grid.

Artillery and Mortars. The grid method of target location for artillery and mortars is transmitted in
the call for fire as grid, followed by the coordinates. The OT direction can be transmitted after the
call for fire has been sent, ideally when reading back the message-to-observer (MTO). Nevertheless,
it must be transmitted before or with the first adjustment.

                                Examples: Grid method of target location

              Grid 452 677.

              Grid 452 677, elevation 65, direction 0830.

              Grid 452 677, elevation 65, direction GTL.

Naval Surface Fire Support. The grid method of target location for NSFS is transmitted in the call
for fire as grid, followed by the coordinates, and altitude, expressed as elevation, followed by the
altitude of the target measured from sea level to the nearest 5 meters or 20 feet. Direction is also
transmitted in the target location element if the method of control is spotter adjust.

                                                                   2-22


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Shift From a Known Point
In shift from a known point, the observer locates the target in relation to a known point. Target
location by shifting from a known point offers several advantages—it is accurate, it does not
require the use of a map, and the observer’s location is not required to be known by the firing unit.
However, the firing unit must know the location and altitude of the known point. The known point
may be a terrain reference point that was previously reported or a target that was previously fired
on and recorded. For NSFS, it may also be a radar beacon position. The steps to locate a target by
shift from a known point are as follows:

             Step 1. Identify the known point to be used:
               • Artillery and mortars. The known point is transmitted in the warning order
                  element of the call for fire by using the words shift known point (or target
                  number) ______.
               • NSFS. The known point is transmitted in the target location element of the call for
                  fire by using the words from reference point (or target number) ______.

             Step 2. Determine the OT direction to the nearest 10 mils.
             Step 3. Determine the lateral shift between the known point and the target to the
             nearest 10 meters. This is a two-step process:

               • First, the observer determines the angular deviation (in mils) between the known
                  point and the target.

               • Next, by using the mil-relation formula (width = range x mils), the observer
                  multiplies the angular deviation (m) by the range to the known point (R) to the
                  nearest 100 meters expressed in thousands (called the shift factor) to determine
                  the lateral shift (W) in meters (see figure 2-12). The lateral shift will be
                  transmitted in the call for fire as a left or right in meters. The shift factor is used to
                  determine only the initial lateral shift. The OT factor is used for all subsequent
                  corrections.

            Example: Lateral shift

Right 110.

Note: If the angular deviation is greater than 600 mils, using the
shift from a known point method of target location should be
avoided because the mil-relation formula becomes inaccurate at
angular deviations greater than 600 mils.

            2-23


---

                              MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                              Known Point

Step 4. Determine the range shift in meters between the known point and the target to
  the nearest 100 meters. The range shift will be transmitted in the call for fire as an add
  if the target is farther away from the observer than the known point or as a drop if the
  target is closer. Meters are understood and do not need to be specified.

                              Example: Range shift

Add 500.

Step 5. Determine the vertical difference between the known point and the target. The
  vertical shift will be transmitted in the call for fire as an up or down shift from the
  elevation of the known point to the elevation of the target. Only vertical shifts of 35
  meters or greater will be transmitted. In addition, any vertical shift that is transmitted
  will be expressed to the nearest 5 meters or 20 feet, if possible.

                              Example: Vertical shift

Up 35.

                              2-24


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

MOBILE OPERATION TARGET LOCATION

Observers with motorized or mechanized units (e.g., mounted in trucks, amphibious assault
vehicles) must be proficient in map reading and terrain association.

Preparations
An observer’s success depends upon preparations that are made before an operation begins,
including the following:

• Becoming acquainted with the vehicle, crew chief, SOP, communications suite, position
   assigned, and positions of other team members.

• Ensuring that the assigned position will allow observation, communication, and coordination
   with other supporting arms representatives.

• Ensuring that the required equipment and information (e.g., maps, targets, radios, frequencies)
   are placed in an accessible location for quick reference.

• Studying the map to provide a mental picture of the operation (e.g., prominent landmarks).
   Points and features along the planned route should be identified to assist in determining
   orientation.

Considerations
Observers must consider certain factors when conducting target location during mobile
operations. The most important factors include direction, selection method of target location, and
actions to be taken in the event of disorientation.

Direction. Because observers move, it is necessary to change OT direction frequently. Another
factor to consider is the magnetic effect of vehicles on the compass. Observers need to move a
minimum of 10 meters away from vehicles to avoid interference or use another method of
determining direction (e.g., scaling from a map, estimating, using the GTL).

Selection Method of Target Location. The grid method is the fastest and most accurate method for
mobile operations if observers have prepared a detailed terrain/map analysis. Observers can call
for fire on planned targets or use these targets to shift to a target of opportunity. The accuracy of
the shift depends upon the accuracy of the OT direction. The polar plot method is of limited use in
mobile operations unless observers have short, static periods and constantly update their position.
Polar plotting is feasible if GPS is used in these situations.

Actions to be Taken if Observers Become Disoriented. As a result of the tactical situation, observers
may have difficulty maintaining adequate observation and orientation. This may make it difficult
to self-locate or identify the target’s location. In these situations, observers can call for fire on a
planned target and shift from the burst location. If completely disoriented, observers can call for
mark center of sector. If there is any doubt about the safety of the firing, a high air burst (e.g., 200
meters) with shell high explosive (HE) or white phosphorous (WP) can be used.

             Note: The tactical soundness of the high air burst must be considered.
             Knowledge of the tactical situation and disposition of friendly forces by the
             firing unit will prevent a fratricide.

                                                                   2-25


---



---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 3
   ARTILLERY AND MORTAR CALL FOR FIRE

The call for fire is a request for fire containing data necessary for obtaining the required fire on a
target. It is a concise message prepared by an observer and transmitted as a request, not an order. It
contains the information needed by the FDC to determine the method of target attack. The call for
fire is sent quickly, but clearly enough to be understood, recorded, and read back without error by
the FDC recorder. In the C-FST, the initial elements of the call for fire should be transmitted by
the radio operator while the observer determines the remaining data (e.g., target location). The
radio operator completes the call for fire as data becomes available. In artillery and mortars,
direction is not included in the initial call for fire when the grid method of target location is used.
When conducting a grid mission, the observer transmits the direction when reading back the MTO
or with the first subsequent correction in adjustment.

The components of the call for fire and the number of transmissions required to communicate
them depends on mission type: adjust fire, fire for effect, or suppressive fire.

ADJUST FIRE AND FIRE FOR EFFECT CALL FOR FIRE

Regardless of the method of target location, the call for fire is transmitted in three parts, consisting
of six elements with a break, and read back after each transmission:

• Transmission one:
    Observer identification.
    Warning order.

• Transmission two:
    Target location.

• Transmission three:
    Target description.
    Method of engagement.
    Method of fire and control.

Observer Identification
The first element of the call for fire identifies to the FDC/FSCC who is calling for fire. The
observer uses a call sign and a suffix. Once given, call signs are omitted from subsequent
transmissions during the mission unless there is a chance of confusion (e.g., when missions are
being conducted simultaneously by other observers).

                                                                    3-1


---

                         MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Warning Order
The second element, the warning order, is a preliminary notice of an action that is to follow. (DOD
Dictionary of Military and Associated Terms, hereafter referred to as DOD Dictionary) The
warning order consists of the type of mission, the size of the element to fire for effect, and the
identification of the method of target location, described in the following subparagraphs.

Type of Mission. When the observer decides that an adjustment is needed because of a questionable
target location or lack of registration corrections, the observer announces adjust fire in the
warning order.

When the observer has an accurate target location and is certain that the first volley will have an
effect on the target, the observer announces fire for effect in the warning order. The accuracy
required to fire for effect depends on the target and the ammunition being used. The observer
should strive for first-round fire for effect.

Size of Element to Fire for Effect. The observer may request the size of the unit to fire for effect (e.g.,
a battalion). Normally, this request is performed by announcing the last letter in the battalion FDC’s
call sign. For example, T6H24 will be announced H. The observer should never refer to a unit in
the clear. If the observer does not specify a size of element to fire for effect, the FDC will make the
decision based on the attack guidance received and the Joint Munitions Effectiveness Manual
solution. Mortars may be employed by platoon or section, which must be specified.

Identification of the Method of Target Location. The observer identifies, in the warning order, the
method that will be used to locate the target. The method of target location may be polar, grid, or
shift from a known point:

• Polar or shift method. Observer announces polar or shift and the known point identified.
• Laser polar plot. Observer announces laser polar.
• Grid method. No requirement to announce grid in the warning order.

                         Examples: Warning orders

Situation 1: Grid

Observer: R2S this is W2P31, adjust fire, over.

FDC:               P31 this is R2S, adjust fire, out.

Situation 2: Polar Plot

Observer: R2S this is W2P31, adjust fire, polar, over.

FDC:               Reads back.

                                3-2


---

           MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

           Examples: Warning orders (Continued)

Situation 3: Shift From a Known Point

Observer:  R2S this is W2P31, fire for effect, shift from AB
           1037, over.

FDC:       Reads back.

      Note: The observer used the fire for effect type of
      mission in situation 3, because there was a good
      target location.

Target Location
The second transmission of the call for fire contains the third element, which is target location. The
observer provides the FDC with the target location data that was determined by using either the
grid, polar, or shift method. The firing unit uses this data to determine firing data (see figure 3-2).
The observer transmits the target location data as described below:

• Grid. The observer announces the word grid followed by the coordinates of the target.
• Polar. The observer announces the word direction followed by the azimuth to the target (to the

   nearest 10 mils); the distance from the observer to the target in meters (to the nearest 100
   meters); and, when 35 meters or greater, an up or down vertical shift (to the nearest 5 meters).

             Note: When conducting a laser polar plot, the observer announces the word
             direction to the nearest one mil, distance to the nearest 10 meters, and the
             vertical angle (up or down) to the nearest 1 mil.

• Shift from a known point. The observer announces the word direction, followed by––
    OT direction (to the nearest 10 mils).
    Lateral shift, if any, transmitted as left or right (to the nearest 10 meters).
    Range shift, if any, transmitted as add or drop (to the nearest 100 meters); and, if
        applicable, up or down vertical shift (to the nearest 5 meters, but never less than 35 meters).

                        3-3


---

      MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

      Examples: Target location

Situation 1: Grid Method of Target Location

Observer: Grid 347 689, over.

FDC:  Grid 347 689, out.

Situation 2: Polar Plot Method of Target Location

Observer: Direction 1680; distance 3,500; down 35, over.

FDC:  Reads back.

Situation 3: Shift From a Known Point Method of Target Location

Observer: Direction 0680, right 250, add 200, over.

FDC:  Reads back.

Target Description
In the fourth element, target description, the observer describes the target by size, type, and degree
of protection to enable the FDC to determine the amount and type of ammunition to be used. The
observer’s description should be brief, accurate, and contain the following:

• Number of elements in the target (e.g., squad, three trucks).
• Type of target (e.g., troops, supply dump, trucks).
• Target’s action (e.g., digging in).
• Degree of protection (e.g., in the open, in fighting holes, in bunkers with overhead protection).
• Target’s size and shape, if significant (e.g., length, width, attitude, radius):

    Rectangular targets. Defined as greater than 200 meters in length and width and are
        described by length, width, and attitude (i.e., the azimuth of the long axis [0000–3200] of
        the target in relation to grid north, to the nearest 100 mils).

                                        Example: Rectangular target

               Length 400, width 250, attitude 2800.

                   3-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

    Circular targets. Irregular-shaped targets with a radius greater than 100 meters. They are
        described by using the radius of the target area.

                                          Example: Circular target
               Radius 200.

    Linear targets. Targets greater than 200 meters long but less than 600 meters long. They are
        described by length and attitude.

                                            Example: Linear target
               Length 400, attitude 1300.

    Irregular-shaped targets. Described by using the location of the target center, length, and
        attitude. Sufficient detail should be provided to enable the FDC to determine the
        appropriate method of attack. An irregular-shaped target can also be described by
        transmitting multiple grids to paint a picture for the FDC of the target to be engaged.

                                     Example: Irregular-shaped target
               Grid 245 876, length 400, attitude 0700.

Method of Engagement
In the fifth element, method of engagement, the observer describes the attack of the target. The
sub-elements of the method of engagement include the type of adjustment, danger close, mark,
trajectory, ammunition, volume of fire, and distribution. Some of the sub-elements are standard
and will be provided automatically unless the observer specifies otherwise; for example—
• Type of adjustment is area fire.
• Trajectory is low angle for artillery and high angle for mortars.
• Ammunition is shell high explosive/fuze quick (HE/Q).
• Distribution is a circular sheaf for artillery and parallel sheaf for mortars.
When required, nonstandard sub-elements must be addressed in the call for fire. For example,
danger close for artillery fires is addressed if the target is within 600 meters of a friendly position.
The following subparagraphs identify the sub-elements of the method of engagement and their
sequence of transmission.

                                                                    3-5


---

                        MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Type of Adjustment. Two types of adjustment are employed as either precision or area fire. Unless
precision fire is specified, area fire will be used:

• Precision fire. Conducted with one weapon on a point target. It is used either to obtain
   registration corrections or to destroy a target. When the mission is a registration, it is initiated
   by the FDC with an MTO. If the target is to be destroyed, the observer announces destruction.

• Area fire. Used to attack an area target. Because many area targets are mobile, the adjustment
   should be made as rapidly and as accurately as possible to keep the target from escaping. A
   well-defined point should be selected at or near the center of the area to be attacked and used as
   an adjusting point. To achieve surprise, fire should be adjusted on an auxiliary adjusting point.
   After adjustment is completed, the fire for effect is shifted to the target. Typically, adjustment
   on an area target is conducted with one adjusting weapon.

Danger Close. “Danger close” is included in the method of engagement where the predicted
impact of a round or shell is within 600 meters of friendly troops for mortars and artillery. When
alerted that the target is danger close, the FDC will employ an added precaution in the delivery of
fires. The observer will use the creeping fire method of adjustment. Omission of this sub-element
indicates that the target is separated from a friendly position by at least 600 meters. Maintaining
the minimum safe distance provides a 99-percent assurance that a casualty-producing hit will not
occur on friendly troops. Once announced, danger close remains in effect until canceled.

Mark. The term “mark” is included in the method of engagement to indicate that the observer is
going to call for rounds either to self-orient in the zone of observation or to indicate targets to
ground troops, aircraft, or other observers. This term is only included in the method of
engagement when needed.

                        Example: Mark

Mark center of sector.

Trajectory. There are two types of trajectory: low-angle fire and high-angle fire.

• For artillery, low-angle fire is standard and need not be specified. If high-angle fire is desired, it
   must be specified.

• For mortars, high-angle fire is standard and need not be specified. For more information on
   high-angle fire, see chapter 6.

                                             Example: Trajectory

               High angle.

                        3-6


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Ammunition. Several types of ammunition are available to the observer. The standard ammunition
for the request is HE/Q; however, the fire direction officer (FDO), based on ammunition
availability and the target description, determines the ammunition/fuze combination to be fired
and announces this in the MTO. The term “in effect” indicates that the projectile/fuze specified is
desired during fire for effect. Nonstandard types of ammunition are specified in the call for fire.
The observer must specify either a projectile or a fuze.

                                            Example: Ammunition
               When requesting HE and fuze variable time (VT) the observer announces
               VT in effect. Because it is the standard, HE is not announced.

Illumination, improved conventional munitions (ICMs), and M825 (smoke) can only be fuzed
with fuze time. Therefore, when firing these projectiles, fuze time is understood and need not be
specified. See appendix D for additional information regarding projectiles and fuzes.
Volume of Fire. The observer may request the number of rounds to be fired in adjustment and then
in effect. For example, requesting three rounds in effect results in 18 rounds fired by a 6-howitzer
battery. If not requested, the volume of fire is determined by the FDO and announced in the MTO.

                                    Example: Requesting volume of fire
               In adjustment: 2 rounds in adjust. (Firing unit fires two rounds).
               During fire for effect: 3 rounds in effect. (Firing unit fires three volleys).

Distribution. In artillery, the observer may control the pattern of bursts in the target area. This
pattern of bursts is called a sheaf. The sheaf denotes the lateral distribution of bursts of two or
more pieces fired together. Special sheafs of any length and width may be requested. When target
length and width are given, attitude must also be provided. When target length is equal to or greater
than five times the target width, the Advanced Field Artillery Tactical Data System (AFATDS)
assumes a linear target. Figure 3-1 depicts the various sheafs. In mortars, the mortar ballistic
computer assumes that the target is linear and fires a parallel sheaf unless a special sheaf is
requested.

                                                                    3-7


---

MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Unless otherwise requested, the firing data computer assumes  A converged sheaf places all rounds on a specific point and is
a circular target with a 100-meter radius. The firing data    used for small, hard targets.
computer determines individual weapon aiming points around
the target.                                                                   Converged Sheaf

                  Circular Sheaf

An open sheaf separates the burst by the maximum effective    A parallel sheaf places the bursts of all pieces parallel. This
burst width of the shell fired.                               sheaf is normally used when firing data is computed manually.

                    Open Sheaf                                                  Parallel Sheaf

Figure 3-1. Sheafs.

Method of Fire and Control
The sixth element of the call for fire, method of fire and control, indicates the desired manner of
attacking the target: whether or not the observer wants to control the time of delivery of fire and
whether or not the observer can actually view the target. The following subparagraphs identify
how methods of fire and control are announced by the observer.

Method of Fire. In area fire, adjustment is normally conducted with one howitzer or with the center
gun of a mortar platoon or section. However, adjusting at long ranges may sometimes be easier
with two howitzers or guns firing. If the observer determines that a two-howitzer or gun
adjustment is more appropriate for the mission, the observer may specify that either two guns
adjust for artillery or a section adjusts for mortars. Whether for adjustment or effect, if the

                                                            3-8


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

observer wants the firing unit to fire by pieces from right to left or left to right, the observer
specifies the unit and indicates right by piece (for left to right) or left by piece (for right to left).

                                     Example: Method of fire by piece

               Battery right.

The typical time interval between rounds fired in a platoon or battery right (left) is 5 seconds. The
observer may specify a different time interval if desired.

Method of Control. In the absence of a specified method of control, fire missions are executed by
the firing unit when ready using the following commands:

• At my command. If the observer wants to control the time of delivery of fire. When the pieces
   are ready to fire, the FDC announces section/platoon/battery/battalion is ready, over, and call
   signs are used. The observer announces fire when it is time for the pieces to fire. This only
   applies to adjusting rounds and the first volley of a fire for effect. At my command remains in
   effect during the mission until the observer announces cancel at my command, over.

• By round at my command. Controls every round in adjustment and every volley in the fire for
   effect phase.

• By piece, by round, at my command. Further combines method of fire with method of control
   and can include right or left or duration.

• Cannot observe. Indicates that the observer cannot see the target because of vegetation, terrain,
   weather, or smoke. Used when there is a reason to believe that a target exists at a given
   location, and the target is important enough to justify firing without adjustment.

• Cease loading. Used during the firing of two or more rounds to stop the loading of rounds into
   the guns. The gun section may fire any rounds that have already been loaded. This is not sent in
   the initial call for fire.

• Check firing. Used to cause an immediate halt in firing. Once check firing has been announced,
   the command cancel check firing must be given to resume firing. This is not sent in the initial
   call for fire. It is only used during emergencies or if a safety violation is noted.

• Continuous fire. Means loading and firing as rapidly and as accurately as possible within the
   prescribed rate of fire for the equipment. Firing will continue until suspended by the command
   cease loading or check firing.

• Continuous illumination. Indicates a type of fire where illumination projectiles are fired at
   specified time intervals to provide uninterrupted lighting on the target or specified area. The
   interval may be specified by the observer in seconds or determined by the FDC. If no interval is
   given by the observer, the FDC determines the interval by the burning time of the illumination
   ammunition that is in use. The continuous illumination command should be used with
   discretion to avoid excessive ammunition expenditure.

• Coordinated illumination. Indicates a type of fire where the firing of illuminating and lethal
   munitions is coordinated to provide illumination of the target and surrounding area, but only at
   the time that is required for spotting and adjusting fire. The observer may allow the FDC to

                                                                    3-9


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

   control the firing of the illumination and lethal munitions by marking the illumination round
   when it provides optimal illumination on the target, or the observer may control the firing of
   each round by using procedures of by round at my command.
• Do not load. Allows the howitzer or gun section to prepare ammunition and lay on the target
   without loading the howitzer. When the command cancel, do not load is transmitted, the section
   automatically loads and fires the weapon, with the exception of an at my command mission. Do
   not load is the recommended method of control for long waits or uncertain missions.
• Time on target (TOT). Allows a unit to schedule its fire so that the initial round strikes the
   target at the time specified. The observer indicates TOT by using one of the techniques
   discussed in appendix E.
• Repeat. Means to fire the same number of rounds using the same method of fire for effect.
   Changes in the number of guns, previous corrections, interval, or ammunition may be requested.

                                        Example: Methods of control
               Situation 1: Grid Method of Target Location
               The observer desires to adjust with HE/Q and then fire for effect with WP.
               Observer: 5 trucks refueling in the open, WP in effect, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Situation 2: Polar Plot
               The target is danger close. Observer desires HE/Q or to let the FDC
               select the projectile/fuze.
               Observer: Infantry platoon in the open, danger close, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Situation 3: Shift from a Known Point
               The observer selects a sheaf to concentrate the fires on a specific point
               and when there is a desire to control the timing of the fires.
               Observer: Machine gun position, converged sheaf, at my command, over.
               FDC: Reads back.

                                                                   3-10


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

SUPPRESSIVE FIRE CALL FOR FIRE

Suppressive fires are fires on or about a weapons system to degrade its performance below the
level needed to fulfill its mission objectives during the conduct of the fire mission. Suppressive
fires are delivered as a suppression mission or as an immediate suppression mission. See chapter 9
for a discussion of SEAD missions.

Both suppression and immediate suppression mission types yield suppressive results only during
the time when the fire is being delivered. Firing on a planned target is more responsive than firing
on a target of opportunity. The maneuver unit should perform an action (e.g., maneuver against the
target, position the observer to adjust fire to neutralize the target, bypass the target) while the
suppressive fires are making impact.

Suppression Mission
The call for fire consists of the observer identification, the warning order suppress, the target
number of the planned target, and the duration and rate of fire. This call for fire is sent in
one transmission.

                                       Example: Suppression mission

               Observer: R2S this is W2P31, suppress AB 3104, 4 minutes, 4 shells per
               minute, over.

               FDC: P31 this is R2S, suppress AB 3104, 4 minutes, 4 shells per minute,
               authenticate DELTA JULIET, over.

               Observer: I authenticate DELTA, out.

Immediate Suppression Mission
An immediate suppression mission normally requires a minimum volume of fire. The type of
ammunition, units to fire, and volume are established by unit SOP (e.g., two sections with one
round of HE/point detonating [PD] and one round of HE/VT). The call for fire is sent in one
transmission and consists of the observer identification, the warning order immediate suppression,
and the target location.

             Note: The immediate suppression mission is not a self-ending mission.

                                Example: Immediate suppression mission

               Observer: This is W2P31, immediate suppression, grid 221 432,
               authentication is TANGO UNIFORM, over.

               FDC: This is R2S, immediate suppression, grid 221 432, out.

                                                                   3-11


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

MESSAGE-TO-OBSERVER

After receiving the call for fire, the FDO determines how the target will be attacked. That decision
is announced to the observer in the form of an MTO, which the observer reads back verbatim. The
MTO should be sent to the observer before the first subsequent correction. At a minimum, the
MTO will consist of units to fire and any changes to the call for fire, including number of rounds
and target number. The FDC will provide other information, as required.

             Note: The MTO is not transmitted for an immediate suppression mission.
Units to Fire
Units to fire refers to the batteries or battalion that will fire the mission. If the battalion is firing for
effect with one battery adjusting, the FDC designates the fire for effect unit (battalion) and then
the adjusting unit (alpha battery) by using the last letter of the call sign.

                                            Example: Units to fire
               Alpha battery (R6G) will adjust and the battalion (A8T) will fire for effect.
               The MTO would begin message-to-observer, T, G . . .

Changes to the Call for Fire
Any changes to the observer’s request in the call for fire are announced, including––
• Number of rounds per gun in fire for effect.
• Target number that is assigned at the FDC to each mission to facilitate the processing of

   subsequent corrections.
• Other information as required.

                                     Example: Changes in call for fire
               The observer requested ICM in effect, and the FDO decides to fire VT
               in effect.
               The MTO would begin message-to-observer, T, G, VT in effect . . .

                                               Example: Rounds
               MTO: T, G, VT in effect, 4 rounds, target AA 7732, over.

                                                                   3-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Probable Error in Range
The probable error in range (PER) is the distance in meters over or short along the GTL of a given
point of aim where 50 percent of all rounds fired are predicted to fall (see figure 3-2). For
example, for an M777A2 howitzer firing HE at a range of 6,000 meters (charge 4H, M232), one
PER is 22 meters. There is a 50-percent chance that any given round will impact 22 meters over or
short of the point of aim when fired at this range. The majority of the remaining rounds will
impact within four PER or 88 meters over or short of the target (see figure 3-2). By contrast, one
PER for the same weapon firing at a range of 14,000 meters is 42 meters.

The PER varies with several factors, including range, propelling charge, ammunition, and
weapon. The FDC typically selects the optimal firing data to minimize PER; however,
circumstances (e.g., intervening crests, counterfire threat) may require the selection of a less than
optimal gunnery solution. The FDC reports the PER when it is equal to or greater than 38 meters
during an area fire mission and 25 meters or greater in a precision fire mission, such as
registrations or destruction. This is to prevent the observer from attempting to correct the impact
of a round caused by natural dispersion.

Angle T
Angle T is the angle formed by the intersection of the OTL and the GTL, with the vertex of the
angle at the target location. Angle T is sent to the observer when it is greater than or equal to 500
mils, or as requested. See chapter 5 for more information about angle T.

Mean Point of Impact

2%  7%  16%           25% 25% 16%  7%                       2%

                                                                Line of Fire

                           One Probable Error in Range
                      Figure 3-2. Probable Error in Range.

                      3-13


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Time of Flight
The time of flight (TOF) is the time in seconds measured from when a weapon is fired to when the
round strikes or detonates. The TOF is sent to an observer when—

• Adjusting high-angle fire during a moving-target mission.
• Using precision-guided munitions.
• Firing for an aerial observer for shell HE in a coordinated illumination mission.
• Using by shell at my command or when requested.

             Note: The MTO for registrations is discussed in chapter 6.

REPORT UPON FIRING

The firing unit will transmit shot after each round fired in adjustment and after the initial round in
the fire for effect phase. The observer will acknowledge each transmission.

                                       Example: Transmission of shot
               FDC: Shot, over.
               Observer: Shot, out.

When requested, the transmission of splash by the FDC informs the observer when the round is 5
seconds from detonation. Firing units should automatically report splash during high-angle firing,
when the observer is airborne, and for other missions as requested by the observer. When reported,
splash should be transmitted for each round in adjustment and for the initial round in each volley
of fire for effect. In coordinated illumination missions, splash is reported for illumination rounds
fired before the beginning of the HE adjustment phase of the mission and only for HE rounds
thereafter. When in the assault, splash allows the supported unit to close with its fires and allows
the observer to remain under cover and concealment while awaiting the fires, enhancing the
observer’s survivability. This transmission also helps differentiate an observer’s own rounds if
other fire missions are being conducted in the area.

                   Example: Splash

FDC: Splash, out.

Note: The observer is not required to acknowledge the transmission.

                   3-14


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

CORRECTION OF ERRORS

Sometimes errors are made by both the observer transmitting data and the FDC personnel reading
back the data. An observer announces correction and transmits the entire corrected transmission
when an error has been made. When an error has been made in a sub-element and the correction of
that sub-element will affect other transmitted data, the word correction is announced. Then the
correct sub-element and all affected data are transmitted in the proper sequence.

                                       Example: Correction of errors
               Observer: Direction 4680; left 200; add 400; up 40; over.
               FDC: Direction 4680; left 200; add 400; up 40; out.
               Observer: Correction direction 5680; left 200; add 400; up 40; over.
               FDC: Correction direction 5680; left 200; add 400; up 40; out.

                                                                   3-15


---



---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 4
               NAVAL SURFACE FIRE
             SUPPORT CALL FOR FIRE

FIRE UNIT STATUS

A ship is assigned a fire support area or a fire support station from which to fire. When a ship
arrives in its assigned firing position and has completed its prefiring tasks, it reports on station and
ready to fire as a part of the guns up ready to fire (GURF) report. The GURF report may include
other pertinent information (e.g., types and quantities of ammunition available for NSFS support).
The GURF report may be requested at other times by either the NGLO or the spotter.

ELEMENTS OF THE CALL FOR FIRE

The spotter must communicate effectively with the ship to perform the primary duty of
providing NSFS by employing a standard call for fire. The call for fire is transmitted to the ship
in two transmissions consisting of seven elements, with a read back after each transmission.
The first transmission includes spotter identification, warning order, and target number; the
second transmission includes target location, target description, method of engagement, and
method of control.

Spotter Identification
The spotter identification informs the ship who is calling for fire. The spotter and the ship will use
call signs. Once given, call signs are usually omitted from subsequent transmissions in the course
of the mission.

Warning Order
The warning order informs the ship that a call for fire is being transmitted and consists of the
words fire mission. The warning order also clears the net and warns the ship that firing on a target
is desired.

Target Number
Before calling for fire, the spotter assigns a target number to each target. By using a target number,
the ship and the NGLO monitoring the mission are able to keep track of each location being fired
upon. The target number consists of two letters followed by four numbers. For targets of
opportunity, the spotter assigns each fire mission a number in numerical sequence from the block
of target numbers allocated by the FSCC, or the FSCC may assign the target number. The spotter
uses previously assigned target numbers for planned targets.

                                                                    4-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

             Note: The assignment of target numbers to fire missions in the call for fire
             does not cause the targets to be recorded as targets.

                                 Example: Assignment of target numbers

               Spotter: A1B, this is C2D, fire mission, target number AB 2135, over.

               Ship: C2D, this is A1B, fire mission, target number AB 2135, out.

Target Location
Target location provides the ship with the information needed to plot the target and determine
firing data as described in chapter 2. The observer transmits target location data depending on the
method being used.

For grid, the spotter announces—

• Grid followed by the coordinates of the target.
• Altitude followed by the altitude of the target, which is measured from MSL (meters

   understood).
• Direction followed by the spotting line if the method of control is spotter adjust.

For polar, the spotter announces—

• Direction followed by the spotting line.
• Distance followed by the distance from the spotter to the target (meters understood).
• Up or down if there is a vertical shift.

For shift from a known point, the spotter announces—

• From reference point or the target number.
• Direction followed by the spotting line.
• Left or right if there is a lateral shift.
• Add or drop if there is a range shift.
• Up or down if there is a vertical shift.

Target Description
The spotter’s target description includes the following:

• Type of target.What the target is and what the target is doing (e.g., trucks in convoy, troops
   digging in).

• Size. The number of elements in the target or physical dimensions in meters. For target attitude,
   mils grid is understood.

• Degree of protection. Whether the target has protection (e.g., whether the target is in the open
   or in bunkers with overhead cover).

                                                                    4-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                             Example: Target size

               5 trucks, 100 troops, 400 x 400 attitude 1600.

Method of Engagement
Method of engagement is the element that the spotter uses to describe how to attack the target. The
sub-elements of the method of engagement include danger close, trajectory, ammunition, number
of guns, number of salvos, and special instructions.

Some of the sub-elements are standard and will be provided automatically unless the spotter
specifies otherwise. The standard method of engagement for trajectory uses a full charge, which
produces a high muzzle velocity and a standard trajectory.

             Note: The standard methods of engagement are ammunition (HE/Q), armament
             (main), number of guns (one gun), and number of salvos (one salvo).

When required, nonstandard sub-elements must be addressed in the call for fire; for example,
when the observer specifies danger close when the target is near a friendly position. The sub-
elements of the method of engagement are transmitted in the sequence presented in the following
subparagraphs and/or as required.

Danger Close. The term “danger close” is included in the method of engagement when there are
friendly troops or positions within a prescribed distance of the target. Danger close is used for
unobserved or initial salvos of NSFS projectiles that are smaller than 6 inches and where the target
is closer than 750 meters to friendly positions.

The spotter reports danger close, followed by a cardinal or intercardinal direction (based on grid
north) and distance (in meters) from the target to the nearest friendly position. The spotter also
designates the place where the first salvo (i.e., round) is to impact. The first salvo can be either
offset or directed at the target.

The first salvo may be offset to impact on the side of the target opposite the location of the
friendly forces or a restricted target (e.g., class E). This is performed by indicating a normal
correction (left/right, add/drop) made in relation to the spotting direction or a cardinal/
intercardinal direction and distance (in meters). The offset between the nearest friendly position
and the first salvo can be any distance specified by the spotter, but it is typically at least 750
meters for 5-inch NSFS.

                                                                    4-3


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                            Example: Danger close

               Danger close south 350. (South 350 indicates friendly position in
               relation to target.)

               First salvo at north 400. (The correction north 400 positions the offset
               at least 750 meters from the friendly position [e.g., 350 meters + 400
               meters = 750 meters]. The first salvo may be directed at the target when
               the tactical situation does not permit an offset. An example would be
               first salvo at target.)

Trajectory. This sub-element applies to ships that are capable of reduced-charge or high-angle
firing. The normal trajectory is standard, flat, and fired at full-charge propellant. If this sub-
element is omitted in the call for fire, the ship will fire full charge. The spotter or ship may specify a
nonstandard trajectory when required. Nonstandard trajectories are reduced charge and high angle.

Reduced Charge. A reduced charge is required when intervening terrain prevents the engagement of
targets in defilade. The spotter or ship raises the trajectory to increase the angle of fall by
announcing reduced charge. Reduced charge lowers the initial velocity of the projectile, which
prevents the ripping of illumination parachutes and increases the accuracy at short ranges. Once
reduced charge has been initiated, it may be terminated by the command cancel reduced charge.

High Angle. High angle is fired with a full charge. It is used to engage targets in defilade when
extended range or other considerations prevent the use of reduced charge. The spotter or the ship
announces high angle. Once initiated, it may be terminated by the command cancel high angle.

Ammunition. Several types of ammunition are available to the spotter, the standard being HE/PD.
If no ammunition is specified in the call for fire, HE/PD is fired in the adjustment and fire for
effect phases. The term in effect indicates that the projectile and/or fuze that was specified is
desired during the fire for effect. Nonstandard types of ammunition are specified in the call for
fire. The spotter must state either a projectile or a fuze.

When a mission requires a nonstandard projectile, sufficient warning should be provided to the
ship. This will allow time to ready the ammunition in the gun mount. The quantities of
nonstandard ammunition carried in the ship’s magazine are limited. See appendix D for a
discussion of projectiles and fuzes.

                     Example: Nonstandard types of ammunition in call for fire

               Spotter: Shell WP (projectile) or fuze controlled variable time (CVT) (fuze).

             Note: When firing illumination, the fuze is understood to be fuze time and
             does not need to be specified.

                                                                    4-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Number of Guns. The spotter may specify the number of guns to be used in adjustment and fire for
effect. If the number of guns in fire for effect is not specified, it is understood to be the same
number as used in adjustment. One gun is considered to be the standard and need not be specified.
If more than one gun is desired, the number of guns must be specified.
Number of Salvos. Salvo refers to the number of projectiles to be fired from each gun.

                                         Example: Number of salvos

               5 salvos (5 projectiles to be fired).

               2 guns 5 salvos (10 projectiles fired).

The number of salvos is transmitted to the ship when entering fire for effect or when the spotter
desires to adjust with multiple salvos. If the method of control is spotter adjust, the number of
salvos is omitted until the spotter is ready to enter fire for effect. If omitted, the ship will fire
one salvo.

                                      Example: Number of salvos sent

               2 salvos in adjustment.

Special Instructions. The spotter uses special instructions in the call for fire when the use of
specific, nonstandard techniques is desired to attack the target. The following are special
instructions that may be used in the call for fire:

• Coordinated illumination. Used to request simultaneous HE fire. This command informs the
   ship that an adjustment to illumination will follow.

• Continuous illumination. Used when the spotter requires constant light on a target. The spotter
   may specify an effective period of time for continuous illumination. The ship determines the
   interval for firing the subsequent illumination salvos on the basis of the burning time of the
   projectile. This command should be used with discretion to avoid excessive expenditure of
   ammunition.

• Interval. Used to cause fire for effect rounds to be fired with a specific time interval between
   each salvo (in seconds).

                                               Example: Interval

               10 salvos, interval 30, fire for effect.

                                                                    4-5


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Sustained fire. Given if there is a requirement for fire for effect to be spread over a specific
   period of time. This command includes the number of salvos and the period of time that they
   are required to be fired.

                                    Example: Sustained fire command

               20 salvos, sustained fire, 5 minutes, fire for effect.

TOT. Used by the spotter when the initial salvos in fire for effect should impact the target at a
specified time. The spotter may use any of the techniques described in appendix E.

Method of Control
Method of control indicates the spotter’s desire or ability to control the delivery of fire. The
standard method of control is spotter adjust and is omitted in the call for fire. The nonstandard
methods of control are fire for effect and ship adjust. Fire for effect can be modified by the
command cannot observe. All methods of control can be modified by the command at my command.

Spotter Adjust. When there is doubt about target location or firing accuracy, the spotter will adjust
salvos until sure that fire will impact the target. This method is understood to be standard and is
omitted from the call for fire. The spotter may state this when there is a desire to revert to
adjustment during a mission.

Fire for Effect. The spotter should strive for fire for effect with the first round or as soon as
possible (ASAP) in the adjustment phase. When determining whether or not to fire for effect on
the first round, the spotter must consider the target location, how accurately the ship has been
firing its initial rounds during previous missions, and the dispersion pattern of NSFS. If the spotter
believes that the first salvo will affect the target, the best results are normally achieved by surprise
fire. When fire for effect is desired, the spotter specifies the number of salvos and the number of
guns, if different from that used in adjustment, and announces fire for effect.

                                           Example: Fire for effect

               10 salvos, fire for effect.

Fire for effect can be modified by the command cannot observe when the spotter desires to fire on
a suspected target or a target provided by intelligence sources. Cannot observe is used when
neither the spotter nor the ship can see the target. The spotter must specify the number of salvos to
be fired in the method of engagement.

                                  Example: Number of salvos to be fired

               2 guns, 4 salvos, cannot observe, fire for effect.

                                                                    4-6


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Ship Adjust. This method of control is employed when the spotter believes that the ship has a
better view of the target than the spotter does. Because direct fire is faster and more accurate, this
method is used whenever possible. After positively identifying the target to the ship, the spotter
announces ship adjust. The ship takes the target under fire. The spotter may assist the ship by
providing range spottings along the GTL, particularly when the spotter is looking perpendicular to
the GTL.
At My Command. The spotter will include at my command in the method of control when the
spotter wants to control the precise firing of each salvo in spotter adjust and the first salvo of fire
for effect. When the ship is prepared to fire each round, the ship will transmit ready over. The
spotter commands fire when ready for the ship to fire the round. At my command remains in effect
throughout the mission or until the spotter announces cancel at my command. At my command is
used frequently by aerial observers.

                                         Example: At my command
               Fire for effect, at my command.

After the ship receives the call for fire and determines firing data, a prefiring report will be made
to the spotter before firing begins. The spotter reads back this transmission to the ship and
commands break . . . fire, over.

                                          Example: Prefiring report
                Ship: GTL 1680; ready 17; over.
                Spotter: GTL 1680; ready 17; break . . . fire, over.
                Ship: Fire, out.

                                                                    4-7


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                 Example: Target location and description
                                with methods of engagement and control

               Situation 1: Grid method of target location; spotter adjust mission

               Spotter: Grid 786 543; altitude 05; direction 2680; 50 troops in open;
               fuze CVT in effect; over.

               Ship: Reads back.

               Situation 2: Polar plot method of target location; at my command
               mission

               Spotter: Direction 2680; distance 3000; down 40; 50 troops in open;
               fuze CVT in effect; at my command; over.

               Ship: Reads back.

               Situation 3: Shift from a known point method of target location; at my
               command mission

               Spotter: From target number AB 3772; direction 3,470; left 280; add
               500; supply depot in defilade; radius 200; reduced charge; 2 guns 10
               salvos; fire for effect; at my command; over.

               Ship: Reads back.

The ship reports its firing direction by using the same directional reference and units used by the
spotter. The ship notifies the spotter of subsequent changes to the GTL of 200 mils (i.e., 10
degrees) or more. Other considerations and commands related to the ship adjust method are as
follows:

• Line of fire. If the ship is firing illumination, the firing direction may not pass over the target
   because of the drift of the flare parachute. To indicate the illumination projectile trajectory, the
   ship will report line of fire, followed by the directional reference. The line of fire may be
   different from the GTL.

• First salvo at point of aim. When the spotter has reported a danger close situation, the ship
   confirms the point of aim that was previously ordered by the spotter.
                                   Example: Point of aim confirmation

               First salvo at north 400.

                                                                    4-8


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Summit. If the spotter is airborne, the ship routinely reports the highest altitude above MSL that
   the projectile will reach on its flight path to the target. Summit may also be requested by
   ground spotters or the NGLO. Summit is reported in feet to aerial observers and in meters to
   ground units.

• Changes to the spotter’s fire request. If the ship must change any portion of the spotter’s fire
   request, the ship notifies the spotter of the change. For example, if the spotter requests WP in
   effect and the ship has none remaining, the ship announces cannot comply with WP, HE in effect.

• Ready/TOF. When the ship is prepared to fire the first salvo, the ship reports ready, followed by
   the TOF (seconds understood). The spotter reads back the entire prefiring report and commands
   break . . . fire. If the method of control included at my command, the ship also reports ready
   before firing each subsequent round. The ship informs the spotter when there is a TOF change
   of more than 5 seconds.

• Authentication. To avoid deception, the ship should initiate authentication procedures upon the
   establishment of initial communications with the spotter.

REPORT UPON FIRING

The ship will transmit shot and splash after each salvo is fired in adjustment and after the initial
salvo in the fire for effect phase. There is no requirement for the spotter to acknowledge these
transmissions:
• Shot is transmitted at the moment the guns are fired.
• Splash is transmitted 5 seconds before the round is expected to detonate.
Splash is not reported during fire for effect when two or more ships are conducting a massed-fire
mission. In coordinated illumination missions, splash is reported for illumination shells before the
beginning of the HE adjustment phase; thereafter, it is reported for only HE rounds.

                                     Example: Transmission of splash
               Shot . . . splash, out.

                                                                    4-9


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

CORRECTION OF ERRORS

Sometimes errors are made by the spotter or the ship when transmitting data. The following
procedures should be used to correct any data errors:
• Correction. The spotter should immediately transmit the word correction, followed by the

   corrected data when an error in transmission has occurred. If the correction affects other sub-
   elements, the correction should include a restatement of all affected data.

                                   Example: Transmission of correction
               Correction, direction 0670, over.
• Wrong. If an error is made during a read back, the word wrong, followed by the correct data, is
   transmitted at the end of the transmission. The word wrong is then read back, along with the
   corrected version.

                                     Example: Transmission of wrong
               Wrong, direction 0670, right 300, add 400, up 15, over.

                                                                   4-10


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 5
               ADJUSTMENT OF FIRE

An adjustment of fire is the process used in artillery and naval gunfire to obtain the correct
bearing, range, and height of burst (if time fuzes are used) when engaging a target by observed
fire. After a round bursts, a spotting is made and a correction is transmitted to adjust the fires onto
the target. Upon a satisfactory adjustment, the observer enters the fire for effect phase. The
procedures and conditions observed during adjustment of fire are applicable to the artillery
observer, the mortar observer, and the NSFS spotter; except where addressed separately, the
word “observer” refers to all three.

SPOTTINGS

A spotting is an observer’s mental determination of the location of the artillery’s burst or mean
point of impact (MPI) of multiple bursts in relation to the adjusting point as observed along the
OTL. Normally, spottings are not announced except in situations involving straddle NSFS and
unobserved or lost spottings. Spottings must be made the instant that the burst occurs, except
where delayed deliberately to take advantage of drifting smoke or dust.

Binoculars should be positioned before the burst appears. The observer holds the binoculars just
below eye level, looking over the top of the binoculars with the naked eye until the burst is
sighted. Then, the binoculars are raised to eye level and the spotting is made.

Spottings are made for height of burst (HOB), range, and deviation. In order to determine the most
difficult spotting first, the observer should spot in that order.

Height of Burst
Height of burst spottings are used to determine how high a burst is above the target and to
determine any increase or decrease required in the HOB or altitude of impact of a round. These
spottings are used for adjusting air bursts when firing fuze time and making adjustments to ground
bursts when attacking a target on a steep slope or with a vertical face. Height of burst spottings,
which are used to make HOB corrections, are categorized as air, graze, or mixed:

• Air. A round or group of rounds that bursts in the air. Binoculars are used to observe an air
   spotting and to determine the detonation height above the target measured to the nearest mil.
                                            Example: Air spotting

               Air 12.

                                                                    5-1


---

                                                                 MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Graze. A round or group of rounds that detonates or impacts. The spotting is made as graze.
• Mixed. A group of rounds that results in an equal number of air and graze bursts, with the

   following variants:
    A mixed air spotting is a group of rounds that results in both air and graze bursts, with most

        of the bursts being air bursts.
    A mixed graze spotting is a group of rounds that results in both air and graze bursts, with

        most of the bursts being graze bursts.

When attacking a target on a steep slope or with a vertical face, HOB spottings for ground bursts
are the difference between the altitude (in meters) of the burst and the altitude of the target. The
observer determines the difference by either a visual estimation or map measurements. These
spottings are then used to make HOB corrections. If the initial rounds of a fire for effect are
spotted as mixed, the subsequent correction or refinement of up 20 will be sent, if required.

Range
Definite range spottings are required to make a proper range adjustment. A graphical portrayal of
range spottings is shown in figure 5-1. Typically, a burst on or near the OTL provides a definite
range spotting. An observer can make a definite range spotting when the burst is not on or near
the OTL by using knowledge of the terrain, drifting smoke, and shadows. However, even
experienced observers must use caution and good judgment when making such spottings. Possible
range spottings include the following:

• Over. A round that detonates beyond the target or adjusting point.
• Short. A round that detonates between the observer and the target or adjusting point.
• Target. A round that detonates on the target. This spotting is used in precision fire (i.e., artillery

   or mortar registration or destruction missions).
• Range correct. A round that impacts at the correct range.
• Doubtful. A round that is so far to the right or left of the OTL that a definite range spotting

   cannot be made.

                                                                         Target  Over
                                                                       (Bunker)          Range
                                                                                        Correct
                                                                 OT Short L
                                                                                                 Doubtful

                                                            100
                                                      100

Observer

                                                                 Figure 5-1. Range Spottings.

                                                                                 5-2


---

MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

In NSFS, a spotting of straddle is made for a multi-gun salvo when some rounds fall short and
some fall beyond the target. The spotter announces straddle followed by a correction to place
the MPI on the target. The term is normally used during a ship adjust or a massed-fire mission.

Deviation
A deviation spotting measures the angular deviation right or left of the OTL. During a fire
mission, the observer measures deviation to the nearest 5 mils. For precision fire, the observer
measures to the nearest mil. Deviation spottings may simply be a certain number of mils to the
right/left of the OTL. For example, 30 right (see figure 5-2) or 30 left (see figure 5-3.

A spotting of on line is when the round bursts along the OTL. This usually means the observer has
a good direction to the target or that the appropriate deviation corrections have been made to bring
the impacts onto the OTL. There is no mil value associated with this type of deviation spotting
(see figure 5-4).
