                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Unobserved Spottings
Under certain conditions, the observer may be able to make a spotting even though the round’s
impact cannot be observed. For example, the observer may hear, but not see, the round impact. By
knowing the terrain, the observer determines that the only place that the round could have
impacted without being seen is in a draw beyond the target. Although this is an unobserved
spotting, the observer can make adjustments from the determined impact location.

Visibility Impairment.Visibility may be temporarily impaired if the observer has to take cover from
incoming fire or if there is smoke or dirt obstructing the target area’s visibility. This environment
may also prevent identification of the observer’s own round. If this occurs, the observer
announces repeat to indicate the desire for another round to be fired with the same data.

Lost Rounds. If the observer is unable to locate the round either visually or by sound, the spotting
is considered a lost round. This spotting is announced, followed by the appropriate corrective
action. Rounds may be lost for several reasons, including fuze malfunctioning (i.e., a dud), terrain
or weather that prevents the observer from spotting the round, or errors by the firing unit. When a
round is lost, positive action must be taken. In selecting the course of action, the observer should
consider experience, the firing unit’s proficiency as demonstrated on previous missions, and the
location of friendly elements with respect to the target. Corrective actions by the observer may
consist of one or more of the following:

• Begin with a data check of the target location data and the call for fire.
• Request that the firing unit check their data. In NSFS, the spotter would announce check

   solution.
• Request repeat. In this instance the firing unit will fire the same shell fuze combination with

   the same data as before.
• Request shell WP, 200-meter air burst with HE, or M825 smoke (for artillery only) for the

   next adjustment.
• Make a bold shift, but ensure that friendly troops are not endangered

If the corrective actions fail, send an end of mission and transmit a new call for fire.

CORRECTIONS

After spotting the burst, the observer determines and transmits a correction in meters to move the
next round to be fired onto the target or adjusting point. Corrections are sent in the reverse order of
the spotting (i.e., deviation, range, and HOB). The observer simultaneously corrects for deviation,
range, and HOB as required. The observer should always specify the target number before sending
the correction.

Sequence of Subsequent Corrections
After the initial burst appears, the observer transmits subsequent corrections until the mission is
terminated. These corrections include appropriate changes in elements that were previously

                                                                    5-5


---

                    MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

transmitted and the necessary corrections for deviation, range, and HOB. Table 5-1 provides a
means for the observer to organize a transmission of multiple corrections in an orderly flow and as
appropriate.

        Table 5-1. Sequence of Subsequent Corrections.

            Artillery/Mortars                     NSFS

Direction                           Direction

Danger close                        Danger close

Trajectory                          Trajectory

Method of fire                      Ammunition

Distribution                        Deviation

Shell                               Range

Fuze1                               HOB

Volume of fire                      Number of guns

Deviation1                          Number of salvos

Range1                              Method of control

HOB1

Target description

Change in type of mission/control1

Splash

Repeat1

1 These commands constitute a command for the firing unit to deliver
another round unless modified by the command, at my command.

Observer-Target Direction
Direction is the key element for adjustment of rounds and precedes all other subsequent
corrections when announced. In NSFS, direction is sent as part of the call for fire. In artillery or
mortars, if direction is not included in the call for fire, it may be sent immediately after the call for
fire, while reading back the MTO, or with the first subsequent correction. Refinement of or
changes to direction are made when the original direction changes by 100 mils (i.e., 5 degrees) or
more. Elements of the call for fire that may require correcting and the sequence of these
corrections are discussed in the following subparagraphs.

                               5-6


---

                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Deviation Corrections. Deviation corrections are determined by observing the following steps:

Step 1. Determine deviation spotting to the nearest 5 mils or 1 mil for precision fire.

Step 2. Determine the OT factor. When the distance is greater than 1,000 meters, the
OT distance is determined to the nearest 1,000 meters. The distance is then expressed
in thousands (drop the last two digits) as the OT factor. If the OT distance is less than
1,000 meters, then the distance is determined to the nearest 100 meters and expressed
in thousands.

Step 3. Multiply the deviation spotting by the OT factor. Express to the nearest 10
meters. Deviation corrections are transmitted in multiples of 10 (meters
understood), with the minimum correction being 30. The direction of the deviation
correction is opposite that of the spotting. The computed deviation correction is
announced as left or right (meters understood).

Table 5-2 provides examples of how deviation corrections are made.

Note: Large deviation corrections (e.g., right/left 400) may indicate an error
in direction. This could be attributed to errors in initial target location,
improper orientation of the OF fan, or malfunction of direction-measuring
equipment. When large corrections occur, the observer makes an
appropriate check by verifying direction before continuing the mission.
When necessary, the observer refines and transmits the new OT direction.
Accuracy of adjustments depends, in part, on an accurate direction.

       Table 5-2. Example of Computed Deviation Corrections.

OT Distance (meter)       OT Factor       Spotting                  Deviation Correction

4,000                4                    45 right  left 180

2,500                2                    100 left  right 200

3,400                3                    55 left   right 160

1,500                2                    20 right  left 40

700                  0.7                  45 left   right 30

The OTL’s relationship to the GTL may influence the determination of deviation corrections. The
natural dispersion along the GTL, which is more pronounced with NSFS, limits the accuracy of
some corrections. An observer whose OTL is perpendicular to the GTL makes deviation
corrections that are actually range corrections for the weapons system. When this occurs, the
observer can over-correct by spotting and correcting what is actually natural dispersion along
the GTL. As mentioned earlier, the angle formed by the intersection of the OTL and the GTL with
the vertex of the angle at the target location is referred to as angle T (see figure 5-5). The observer is
notified when angle T is significant enough to affect the adjustment of fire 500 mils or greater. When
notified, the observer continues to make deviation corrections as shown in table 5-2. However, if the
observer sees that the corrections do not have the proper effect (e.g., a right 200 correction appears
to shift right 300), the observer may consider adjusting the corrections proportionately to
compensate for dispersion resulting from angle T (i.e., cutting the correction in half).

                                     5-7


---

MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

GTL                    OTL

              Angle T

Gun Position                Observer

Figure 5-5. Angle T.

The spotter must remain abreast of the OTL/GTL relationship because the characteristic flat
trajectory of NSFS results in a pronounced dispersion pattern along the GTL range dispersion.
The spotter must derive the OTL/GTL relationship from the prefiring report and visualize
the OTL/GTL relationship.

Range Corrections. The observer must aggressively adjust and should strive to enter fire for effect
ASAP. When conducting an adjustment onto a target, the observer makes range corrections along
the OTL. Range corrections consist of either the command add (move burst away from the
observer) or drop (move burst closer to the observer). Range corrections are transmitted in
multiples of 100 meters with the smallest correction being 100 meters. However, 50-meter
corrections may be used when entering fire for effect. The methods for conducting range
corrections are successive bracketing, hasty bracketing, one-round adjustment, and creeping fire.

Successive Bracketing. After a definite range spotting of over or short has been determined, the
observer performs the following steps:

             Step 1. Select a range correction that is large enough to ensure that the next round’s
             impact is on the opposite side of the target from the previous round’s impact. This
             brackets the target between the two rounds.
             Step 2. Select a correction of add or drop 100, 200, 400, or 800 meters to achieve a
             bracket. Once a bracket has been established, corrections are made that successively
             split the bracket in half in multiples of 100-meter increments, always moving the next
             burst toward the target.
             Step 3. Continue this process until a 100-meter bracket is established around the
             target or adjusting point.
             Step 4. Add or drop 50, with a deviation correction as required, and enter the fire for
             effect phase.

                                                                    5-8


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

             Note: The fire for effect phase is entered after a 200-meter bracket has been
             established when missions use a PER greater than 38 meters or when
             missions using shell dual-purpose improved conventional munitions
             (DPICM), M825, or family of scatterable mines (FASCAM).

In NSFS, once a 200-meter bracket is achieved, the spotter may enter fire for effect when
engaging an area target, such as a supply depot. If the observer fails to achieve a bracket on the
initial correction, another range correction is retransmitted until a bracket is achieved. Successive
bracketing procedures are time consuming. This procedure should be used only when time is not
critical and the situation requires this type of adjustment.

                                      Example: Successive bracketing

               The observer’s spotting for the first round is over, 75 mils left.

               Using an OT factor of 2 and an OT distance of 2400. The observer’s
               first correction would be right 150, drop 200.

               The next spotting is short, 15 mils right.

               The observer transmits left 30, add 100. The round impacts short, 5 mils
               right. The observer transmits add 50, fire for effect.

               No deviation corrections of less than 30 are made.

             Note: Large range corrections (e.g., add/drop 800) may indicate an error in
             the initial OT factor calculated by the observer. A new OT factor can be
             refined by combining the initial distance to target with subsequent range
             corrections. Determine a new OT factor with bold range corrections.
             Accuracy in adjustment depends on an accurate OT factor.

Hasty Bracketing. Effectiveness on the target decreases as the number of rounds used in adjustment
increases because of the loss of surprise. An alternative to successive bracketing is hasty
bracketing. In this technique, the observer uses an initial bracket as a yardstick to determine a
correction necessary to move the next rounds onto the target in fire for effect. Hasty bracketing
can achieve effective results depending on the nature of the target, the terrain, the firing unit’s
proficiency, and the observer’s experience. However, hasty bracketing is difficult to use when
adjusting NSFS because of its characteristic dispersion pattern.

One-Round Adjustment. Unlike successive and hasty bracketing, this method does not require the
establishment of a bracket. The observer spots the location of the first round, calculates, transmits
a correction that is necessary to move the next burst onto the target, and fires for effect. One-round
adjustment requires an experienced observer who is familiar with the terrain. This method can be
used when insufficient time exists for adjustment, when the observer is equipped with a CLRF, or

                                                                    5-9


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

when the continued adjustment of fire may endanger the observer. The laser polar technique uses
a one-round adjustment because of the accuracy of its measurements.

Creeping Fire. The creeping fire method of adjustment is used when the observer desires to make
range corrections by creeping the rounds closer to the target instead of bracketing or making large
corrections. This method of adjustment is always used in danger close missions. In danger close
situations, the observer must remember the position of the friendly troops to ensure that a
correction will not cause rounds to endanger them. This may be applicable in situations where lost
rounds are likely, such as adjusting onto a target located on the topographic crest of a hill.

For artillery and mortars, the observer makes corrections for creeping fire in 100-meter
increments or less when moving rounds toward friendly forces. All mortars that will fire for effect
are used in adjustment. For battalion missions, firing batteries should be adjusted individually.

In NSFS, the spotter moves each round in creeping fire toward the target in increments of 100
or 200 meters until the effect is obtained. The combined effect of the correction should not
exceed 200 meters toward friendly forces. If more than one gun is to fire for effect, the spotter
should check the MPI of all guns to be used before entering fire for effect. Fires may be crept to
within a minimum safe distance of the friendly position. Recommended minimum safe distances
for adjusting salvos of a 5-inch gun are 200 meters when firing parallel to front lines and 350
meters when not firing parallel to the front lines. The ship will normally advise the spotter when
the predicted fall of shot approaches the minimum safety distances.

Height of Burst Corrections. Height of burst corrections are used to adjust the difference in altitude
between the impact of rounds and the target and to make corrections for air bursts. Height of burst
corrections are needed when attacking targets that are located on a steep slope or presenting a
vertical face. The observer uses up or down corrections (presented in meters) to move the impact
of the round. These corrections allow the observer to deliver rounds on target with fewer
adjustments than when using other corrections (i.e., right/left, add/drop). When using up or down
corrections, the observer adjusts to the target and does not bracket (see figure 5-6).

Add Correction        Up Correction

Figure 5-6. Examples of Corrections.

                5-10


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Repeat. During the subsequent correction phase, the command repeat means to fire another round
using the same firing data that was used to fire the previous round. The observer may adjust for
any change in ammunition. Repeat is not sent in the initial call for fire.

FIRE FOR EFFECT PHASE

Conditions for Entering the Fire for Effect Phase
The fire for effect phase is entered when the deviation, range, and HOB are correct or if effective
fire will result when the range bracket is split. In artillery and mortars, the observer enters the fire
for effect phase in the following situations:
• When an adjusting round has an effect on the target.
• When splitting a 100-meter bracket.
• When splitting a 200-meter bracket if notified by the FDC that the PER is 38 meters or larger

   or when firing shell DPICM, M825, or FASCAM.
During NSFS the spotter enters fire for effect in the following situations:
• When an adjusting round has an effect on the target.
• When splitting a 100-meter bracket for a point target.
• When splitting a 200-meter bracket for an area target.
Procedures for Entering the Fire for Effect Phase
In artillery and mortars, the observer announces fire for effect, preceded by any corrections.

                                           Example: Fire for effect
               Fire for effect, over
               or
               Right 30, add 50, fire for effect, over.

During NSFS, the spotter transmits corrections as required, including the number of guns or
salvos if different from that used in adjustment or stated in the call for fire, then fire for effect.

                                   Example: Transmission of correction
               Right 30, add 50, 6 salvos, fire for effect.

                                                                   5-11


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Procedures for Obtaining Additional Fire After Fire for Effect
The observer analyzes the results of the fire for effect. The situation may require additional rounds
to be fired on either the same or a different location. If additional fire is needed for artillery and
mortars, the observer announces repeat, indicating that the observer desires to use the same
volume of fire.
Adjust the Mean Point of Impact (Artillery) of the Fires
If the location of the fire needs to be moved to achieve satisfactory results, the observer announces
the appropriate corrections or refinement and repeat or adjust fire.

                                        Example: Adjust MPI of fires
               Right 100, repeat, over.
               Add 400, adjust fire (spotter adjusts for NSFS), over.

For NSFS, the spotter distributes the fire over an area target by announcing spreading fires,
followed by a correction or repeat.

                                          Example: Spreading fires
               Spreading fires, right 100, repeat, over.

Refinement
Refinement is furnished for all missions. It is a term used in artillery to indicate a final correction
to move the center of impact to the adjusting point. This correction may be less than 30 meters and
is expressed to the nearest 10 meters.

                                    Example: Correction or refinement
               Right 20, add 20.

End of Mission
Once the desired effects are achieved on a target, the observer should transmit an end of mission
statement to the firing unit. Four items are needed in the end of mission statement: refinement,
record as target, end of mission, and surveillance. With all artillery calls for fire, SEAD,
immediate suppression, and other types of missions, the FDC should receive an end of mission
statement from the observer. There are no self-ending missions.

                                                                   5-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Refinement. In artillery, if fires have been inaccurate but have produced sufficient results, the
observer transmits a correction or refinement to the FDC.

                                    Example: Correction or refinement
               Right 20, add 20, end of mission, RPG silenced, over.

Record as Target. The observer announces record as target to plot the target for future use. In
artillery and mortars, the FDC applies the refinement, conducts a replot when necessary, and
announces the adjusted grid to the target. The target number should be the same number that is
provided in the MTO. During NSFS, the target number is assigned by the spotter.

                                     Example: Assigned target number
               Record as target, target number AB 1250, over.

End of Mission. Announcing end of mission, followed by surveillance, is the last transmission in
the course of the fire mission. Once it has been announced, the mission is considered to be
terminated.
Surveillance. Surveillance should be brief, but it should provide casualty and/or damage
information as accurately as possible. This requires the correct usage of the terms destroyed,
neutralized, and suppressed, as addressed in appendix D.

                                            Examples: Surveillance
               End of mission, BMP neutralized, estimate 6 casualties, over.
               Right 20, add 20, record as target, end of mission, 3 trucks destroyed,
               estimate 15 casualties, over.

NAVAL SURFACE FIRE SUPPORT ADJUSTMENT PROCEDURES

The flat trajectory and high muzzle velocity of NSFS make the adjustment of NSFS difficult,
particularly on flat terrain. The spotter must use accurate target location, bracketing, and the
following procedures:
• Identify the GTL relative to the spotter’s own position and the target. This will provide the

   basis for the spotter to identify round-to-round dispersion in adjustment.

                                                                   5-13


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Recognize the dispersion pattern of NSFS. The fall of shot of NSFS can be described as a
   narrow, elongated pattern as seen along the GTL. The size of the pattern varies with range. For
   example, at 21,000 meters, the 5-inch gun mount will cause a round-to-round dispersion
   pattern that is approximately 150 meters long and 50 meters wide. Figure 5-7 displays the
   NSFS dispersion pattern.

• Predict each fall of shot before impact. While the ship is determining firing data for the next
   round, the spotter should visualize the fall of shot based on corrections. The spotter compares
   the actual impact of the round with the predicted fall of shot. Differences that occur along the
   GTL may indicate round-to-round dispersion.

T

Figure 5-7. Naval Surface Fire Support Dispersion Pattern.

• Ignore errors to the fall of shot attributed to round-to-round dispersion. When a round has
   impacted contrary to its predicted fall of shot due to dispersion, the spotter makes a correction
   from the predicted point of impact instead of the actual point of impact. This correction should
   result in the next fall of shot impacting as predicted, keeping the spotter from getting a ping-
   pong effect in adjustment (see figure 5-8).

• Correct from MPI. When consecutive rounds impact differently from their predicted fall of shot,
   the spotter should make a correction from the average or MPI of the rounds (see figure 5-9).

• Adjust multiple rounds. There may be times when the spotter needs to adjust with multiple
   rounds, fire multiple salvos from a single gun, or use multiple guns in adjustment. Using

                                                              T

    add 200. The predicted fall of shot is                       3
    over, on line A.                                                       1

    of drop 100.

The third spotting impacts as predicted,

Figure 5-8. Example of Dispersion.

                                                    5-14


---

                                                 MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

multiple rounds as an adjustment technique requires the spotter to adjust from the MPI of all
rounds fired. This technique is typically used when firing on a large-area target or when
visualizing the GTL. This technique can also be used by spotters who are experiencing
difficulties in range dispersion during adjustment.

ARTILLERY AIR BURST ADJUSTMENT AND CORRECTION

On steep terrain, up or down corrections are essential in the adjustment of fires. The spotter
should—

• Use the corrections to bring the fall of shot to the same elevation as the target. Up or down
   corrections will be reflected on the ground concerning GTL.

• Transmit up or down corrections in increments of 5 meters.

• Use the map to help determine these corrections.

• Avoid mixing add or drop with up or down corrections for impact fires because both of these
   corrections involve an elevation change on the gun and the results would be unpredictable.
   Deviation and range adjustments along the OTL will usually equate to elevation changes on the
   gun when transformed to GTL corrections.

• Use up or down corrections to bring the impacts to the same altitude as the target, then switch
   to deviation and/or range corrections.

Fuzes that create an air burst with shell HE are either mechanical or time fuzes or they are
proximity or VT fuzes. When firing fuze time, the observer must adjust the HOB. The adjustment
of air bursts is made by using HOB spottings to determine a correction. The firing battery fires one
gun in adjustment. Variable time fuzes do not require adjustment.

   200. The predicted fall of shot is over, on line A. A

    left. (The apparent error is along the GTL.)
    The spotter transmits a correction of drop                   T
    100. The predicted fall of shot is short,
    on line B.

3. The third spotting impacts short, 30 mils left.        3  B

The final correction is right 50, add 50, five salvos,

                     Figure 5-9. Adjustment of Mean Point of Impact (Artillery).

                                                    5-15


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Mechanical or Time Fuzes
Adjustment. After HE/PD is adjusted for deviation and range to within 50 meters of the target, the
observer makes HOB adjustments by initiating the firing of an air burst. The observer announces
time repeat or time, followed by a deviation and/or range correction. When an air burst detonates,
the HOB spotting (to the nearest mil) is multiplied by the OT factor to determine a correction in
meters. The correction is made to cause the next air burst to detonate 20 meters above the target.
The correction is transmitted as an up or down correction followed by the number of meters.
Deviation or range corrections are not made after entering the time phase.
Correction. When making HOB corrections, the observer must be aware of the gunnery solution
and its effect on the round (see figure 5-10). When an HOB correction is given by the observer,
the FDC converts the HOB correction to a fuze setting correction for the mechanical time fuze.
An up HOB correction will result in a decrease in the fuze setting. A down correction will result in
an increase in the fuze setting. The change in fuze setting will change the point of detonation
along the trajectory of the round. In addition to achieving a change in HOB, the observer will also
notice a change in range. An up correction will result in the round detonating short of the target. A
down correction will result in the round detonating closer to the target.
If the initial round is a graze spotting, then an automatic correction of up 40 is made. Subsequent
up 40 corrections are given until an air burst is achieved. Consecutive graze spottings may
indicate an error by the battery (e.g., improper fuze setting) or an error by the observer in target

                                                                          Air Burst at 21.0
                                                                               Seconds

                                                                       (After Up Correction)

                                                        Air Burst at 21.2
                                                             Seconds

                                                    (Before Up Correction)

                                                 Change in Range Because of Up Correction
                       Figure 5-10. Effect of Height of Burst Corrections on Burst of Round.

                                                                   5-16


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

location altitude. The observer must avoid making deviation and range corrections from a graze
burst unless unable to achieve effects on target.

Fire for Effect. When an air burst is achieved, the fire for effect phase is entered if the observer
believes that the HOB correction will result in a 20-meter HOB. Fire for effect is never initiated if
the last round observed was spotted as graze or if the HOB correction is greater than 40 meters.

                                           Example: Fire for effect

               Down 10, fire for effect, over.

There are several possible spottings for both the initial fire and any needed corrections:

• Air. Adjust the mean HOB to 20 meters.
• Mixed. The correction given is up 10.
• Mixed air. The HOB is correct.
• Mixed graze. The correction given is up 20.

Time fuzes are normally selected for adjust-fire missions because of the requirements to achieve
an effective HOB. However, it is possible to initially fire for effect with time fuzes if a registration
has been conducted with that particular fuze lot. The closer the target is to the registration point,
the greater the likelihood of achieving effects without adjustment.

Proximity or Variable Time Fuzes
Adjustment. Variable time fuzes require no adjustment before firing for effect. The fire for effect
phase is entered after the appropriate bracket is established with the adjusting shell and fuze.
Variable time fuzes should always achieve an air burst.

Correction. Variable time fuzes achieve their 7-meter HOB by using radar, not the time setting on
the fuze. Graze bursts of an entire volley indicate one of the following:

• A computational error by the battery (e.g., improper fuze settings).
• An error by the observer in target location altitude.
• A malfunctioning lot of fuzes.

In the case of graze bursts with VT, the spotting of graze is transmitted to the FDC. The FDC will
subtract 1 second from the fuze arming time in an attempt to overcome computational or observer
error. Factors affecting the performance of VT fuzes and possibly creating premature air bursts
may include snow, water, and intervening crests.

                                           Example: Fire for effect

               Graze, repeat, over.

                                                                   5-17


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

NAVAL SURFACE FIRE SUPPORT AIR BURST ADJUSTMENT AND CORRECTION

Time fires use special fuzes to achieve an air burst over the target. These fuzes include
mechanical and electronic time fuzes that require a time setting before firing. Air bursts may also
be delivered by proximity fuzes (i.e., VT or CVT) designed to explode at an optimal HOB (e.g.,
7 meters for 5-inch NSFS) based on a radio-activated signal. Time fires using time or electronic
time fuzes require adjustment to ensure detonation at an optimal HOB (i.e., 20 meters) in fire
for effect. Proximity fuzes do not require adjustment.

Adjustment of Time Fires
The observer announces fuze time in effect in the method of engagement element of the call for
fire and conducts adjustment with fuze quick. The observer will enter the time phase of the
adjustment process in the following situations:

• When splitting a 200-meter bracket for an area target.
• When splitting a 100-meter bracket for a point target.
• When an adjusting round has an effect on the target.

The transmission to enter into the time phase of adjustment is fuze time, followed by a correction
or repeat.

                                      Example: Fuze time adjustment

               Fuze time, right 30, add 50, over.

               Fuze time, repeat, over.

Correction for Height of Burst
If the initial time round is spotted as a graze, then the correction given is up 40. A 40-meter HOB
correction will be applied until an air spotting is obtained. Consecutive graze spottings may
indicate either an error in the altitude of the target reported in the call for fire or an error by the
mechanical fuze setter on the gun. The spotter must avoid making deviation and range corrections
from a graze burst. Usually, a graze burst will be over the target on the GTL. Once an initial air
spotting is achieved, the spotter measures the spotting to the nearest mil and computes an HOB
correction by multiplying the spotting by the OT factor. The HOB correction is made to the
nearest 5 meters to correct the HOB to 20 meters. If a correct HOB is expected, the spotter enters
fire for effect. If the air spotting is abnormally high (i.e., 60 meters or greater), then the spotter
should observe another salvo before entering fire for effect. Abnormally high bursts (HBs) are
typically short on the GTL and out of the target area because the fuze functioned prematurely in
the projectile’s trajectory. If a graze burst is obtained after an air burst, the correction given is up 20.
Fire for effect is never begun when the last burst observed results in a graze spotting.

                                                                   5-18


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 6
ARTILLERY AND MORTAR SPECIAL SITUATIONS

The C-FST may be required to perform artillery and mortar special situation missions. These
special situation missions are usually designed for a specific purpose and are not the normal
means of providing fires.

HIGH-ANGLE FIRE

High-angle fire is fire delivered at angles of elevation greater than the elevation that corresponds
to the maximum range of the gun and ammunition concerned. High-angle fire is employed in two
instances: for artillery when the weapons fire out of defilade from within built-up areas or over
high terrain features near friendly troops and against targets in defilade when the target is directly
behind hill crests, in jungles, or in deep gullies or ravines and cannot be reached by low-angle fire.

The procedures for the adjustment of high-angle fire are the same as those for the adjustment of
low-angle fire (see chapter 3). The observer must realize that small deviation corrections during
adjustment may be unnecessary and time consuming because of the increased dispersion
experienced during high-angle fire. Because the TOF is long in both adjustment and fire for effect,
the FDC announces shot when the round is fired and splash 5 seconds before the burst occurs.
Fuze time is not used with HE projectiles in high-angle fire because of the high probability of
errors in HOB. If an air burst is desired, fuze VT provides excellent results.

PRECISION FIRE

The use of precision fire places a heavy responsibility on the observer, who must consider the time
required, ammunition expenditure, and signature of the firing unit in relation to the effect that may
be gained from precision fire missions. When conducting a precision mission, an 8-digit grid
should be used unless the observer is equipped with a CLRF. When so equipped, a near mensurated
coordinate should be used. The two types of precision missions are registration and destruction.

Registration
Registration is the adjustment of fire to determine firing data corrections. The observer is directed
to conduct registrations when the firing unit has no other means to determine firing data
corrections to improve its accuracy.

                                                                    6-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Precision Registration. A precision registration is conducted with a single weapon. The FDO may
instruct the observer to conduct a registration on a designated point or may have the observer
select the registration point. The registration point should be accurately located, semipermanent,
near the center of the zone of fire, on fairly level terrain if possible, and on common survey with
the firing unit. The registration may include an impact portion (i.e., adjustment of fuze quick) or
an impact and time portion (i.e., adjustment of fuze quick and fuze time). The precision
registration is initiated with an MTO.

                                      Example: Precision registration

               FDC: H18 this is H44, register on known point 2, quick and time, over.
               Observer: Reads back.
               Observer: Direction 6400, over. Transmission given when ready to
               observe.
               FDC: Reads back.
               FDC: Shot, over.
               Observer: Shot, out.

The FDC may provide a general vicinity for the observer to select a registration point. If this
occurs, the observer transmits the selected 8-digit grid, including altitude and direction.

                                     Example: Grid registration point

               Grid 6124 3843, altitude 210, direction 6310, over.

Impact Portion: Adjustment of Fuze Quick. The objective of the impact portion of a registration is to
obtain spottings of 2 over and 2 short along the OTL from rounds fired with either the same data
or 25 meters apart. Fifty meters is applicable when PER is greater than or equal to 25 meters.
These rounds are called usable rounds and typically require spottings from four separate rounds.
However, a target hit or a round that is spotted as range correct counts as a spotting of both over
and short. Therefore, the objective could be achieved with two consecutive target hits or range
correct spottings. The following applies in these situations:

• Deviation. The observer spots the rounds for deviation to the nearest mil and brings rounds onto
   the OTL before splitting a 200-meter bracket. Deviation corrections should not be made after a
   200-meter bracket has been established. Once the rounds are brought onto the OTL, the observer
   measures and records deviation spottings but makes no correction. When a doubtful range
   spotting is obtained, the observer corrects for deviation only. If a deviation correction must be
   made after a 200-meter bracket has been established, the last round fired and all previous rounds
   cannot be considered usable for determining range and deviation refinement data.

                                                                    6-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Range. The observer spots the rounds for range (i.e., over, short, or range correct) and makes
   range corrections by using successive bracketing until a 50-meter range bracket is established
   (i.e., add or drop 50). Once this round impacts, the observer makes a spotting, a range
   correction to fire 2 rounds with data 25 meters in the direction opposite that of the spotting, and
   transmits 2 rounds, add or drop 25. If both rounds result in spottings of short or over, the
   observer changes the volume to one round and transmits add 25 or drop 25. Firing is continued
   until 2 definite range spottings have been obtained at the opposite end of the 25-meter bracket.

• Refinement. When the requirement of 2 over and 2 short with the same data or data fired 25
   meters apart has been met, the impact portion of the registration ends with necessary
   refinement data. Refinement data may include a deviation correction, a range correction, or
   both to the nearest 10 meters.

When determining refinement data for range, the location of the registration point is determined
with respect to the two sets of spottings, and then refinement data is determined and announced. If
the registration point is closer to the last rounds fired, then no range refinement is necessary to
move the impact toward the registration point (see figure 6-1).

                                                                                             Last

                                                       5 Round
                                                                               8

Deviation refinement is determined by adding the deviation spottings of the rounds, establishing
the 2 over and 2 short. This may include two, three, or four deviation spottings. This total is then
divided by the number of rounds to obtain an average deviation, which is expressed to the nearest
mil. The average deviation, multiplied by the OT factor, equals the correction, which is expressed
to the nearest 10 meters.

Example: Deviation corrections for impact registration

The observer has determined four usable rounds with the following
spottings:

Round         Spotting
   4        Over, 6 right
   5        Short, 8 right
   6        Short, 5 right
   7        Over, 7 right

The observer determines the sum of the deviation spottings to be 26
right (6 right + 8 right + 5 right + 7 right).

The average deviation of 6 right (26 right ÷ 4 rounds = 6.5 [6 right]) is
multiplied by the OT factor (3) to determine the deviation refinement of
left 20 (6 right x 3 = 18 meters right [left 20]).

The average deviation of 6R (26R ÷ 4 rounds = 6.5 [6R]) is multiplied
by the OT factor (3) to determine the deviation refinement of left 20 (6R
x 3 = 18 meters R [left 20]).

If the spottings include right and left spottings, the observer must
algebraically compute the average deviation: 7 right and 3 left = 4 right.

If the calculated deviation refinement is less than 10 meters, it is omitted.

The registration point was equidistant between the two sets of rounds,
as illustrated in figure 6-2. The observer determines the range
refinement to be drop 10.

The observer transmits the following correction:

Left 20, drop 10, record as registration point, end of mission.

       6-5


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

If the MTO specified only an impact portion of the registration, then the observer terminates the
impact registration by transmitting refinement data, followed by the announcement of record as
registration point, end of mission.

                                Example: Impact registration termination

               Left 20, drop 10, record as registration point, end of mission, over.

If the MTO specified an impact and time portion, then the observer terminates the impact portion
and initiates the time portion by transmitting refinement data followed by the announcement of
record as registration point, time, repeat.

                                     Example: Time portion initiation

               Left 20, drop 10, record as registration point, time, repeat, over.

Time Portion: Adjustment of Fuze Time. The objective of the time portion of precision registration is to
correct the mean HOB (4 rounds fired with the same data) to 20 meters above the registration point:

• Adjustment. If the first round is spotted as graze, then a correction of up 40 is given. If the first
   air burst is extremely high, the observer may send a down correction and repeat. Once a
   measurable air burst (i.e., an HOB of 60 meters or less) has been obtained, the command given
   is 3 rounds, repeat, over.

• Refinement. When 4 rounds have been fired with the same data, the registration is ended with
   the appropriate correction to achieve a 20-meter HOB. When 4 air bursts are spotted, the HOB
   is corrected to 20 meters.

The mean HOB is determined by adding the four spottings in mils, dividing by four, expressing
the sum to the nearest mil, and then multiplying by the OT factor. This is the same technique used
in determining deviation corrections. The sum is then expressed to the nearest 5 meters, and the
appropriate correction is determined to achieve the desired 20-meter HOB.

                                  Example: Refinement to achieve HOB

               Up 10, record as time registration point, end of mission, over.

For mixed spottings, the following automatic corrections are used:

• Three air bursts and one graze—no correction required.
• Two air bursts and two grazes—up 10.
• One air burst and three grazes—up 20.

Check rounds may be fired to verify time registration validity; however, they are not necessary.

                                                                    6-6


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Second-Lot Registration. The FDC may direct the observer to conduct a second-lot registration.
Second-lot registrations are conducted in a manner similar to that for first-lot (i.e., single)
registrations. After the first-lot impact registration has been completed, a precision registration for
fuze time is conducted if required. The FDC will announce to the observer observe second-lot
registration after the observer has recorded the registration of the first lot. The observer must then
establish the appropriate range bracket and complete the second-lot registration. The time portion
of the registration is not fired with the second lot.

Abbreviated Registration. At times, the tactical situation or ammunition constraints may prohibit
conducting a full-scale precision registration. Although not as accurate, an abbreviated precision
registration can compensate for the effects of nonstandard conditions. Abbreviated registrations
are more accurate and therefore more feasible if the observer is equipped with a CLRF. The FDO
makes the decision to conduct an abbreviated registration. For this type of registration, the
observer shortens the standard procedures of a precision registration.

             Note: Concerning impact portion-adjustment of fuze quick, normal adjust-
             fire procedures are followed until a 100-meter bracket is split. The
             correction sent is add or drop 50. The burst that is a result of an add or drop
             50 correction is spotted. Minor corrections for both deviation and range to
             the nearest 10 meters are sent to the FDC, along with the announcement
             record as registration point, (time repeat), as shown in figure 6-5.

             Note: Concerning time portion-adjustment of fuze time, normal adjust-fire
             time-adjustment procedures are followed in the time portion. An air burst is
             obtained and then corrected to a 20-meter HOB. Instead of firing for effect,
             refinement is sent to the FDC, the time registration point is recorded, and
             the mission is ended.

Mortar Precision Registration. Precision registration for mortars is conducted for impact fires only.
The procedures are almost identical to the precision registration of fuze quick (i.e., impact)
procedures for artillery. The exceptions are as follows:

• Only spottings of one round over and one round short are required.
• Once a 100-meter range bracket has been split and the last fired round is within 50 meters of

   the target, refinement corrections are sent to the FDC and the mission is ended.
• Range refinements are made to the nearest 25 meters.

The transmissions to the FDC after the last two rounds are similar to those used for artillery.

Adjustments for the mortar sheaf must ensure that all of the mortars fire parallel. The sheaf may
be adjusted at any time during a fire mission but may be directed by the FDC after a registration.
The FDC will announce prepare to adjust sheaf, over. The mortars will be positioned and
numbered in sequence from right to left as seen from behind the tubes. For 81-mm mortars,
numbers may be 1 through 8 when positioned as a platoon and 1 through 4 for a 2-section position.

             Note: In the 81-mm mortar platoon, the term “mortar section” refers to 4
             tubes (i.e., squads).

                                                                    6-7


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

To adjust the sheaf, the observer requests section right or section left, repeat, over. Each squad will
then fire, in order, from left to right or right to left, with 10-second intervals between rounds. The
mortar that was used to register will not fire.

                                         Example: Sheaf adjustment

               If the observer requests section right, repeat, over and number two
               conducted the registration, then numbers one, three, and four would fire.

When adjusting the sheaf, all rounds must be adjusted on line at approximately the same range,
within 50 meters, and with 40 meters of lateral spread between rounds. Range corrections for
rounds impacting within 50 meters of the sheaf line are ignored. The sheaf is adjusted
perpendicular to the GTL. If angle T is greater than 500 mils, each piece is adjusted onto the
registration point and the FDC computes data for the sheaf. Lateral refinement corrections are
made to the nearest 10 meters, but corrections of less than 50 meters are not fired. Once
refinement corrections for all mortars have been determined, the sheaf has been adjusted.

In the figure 6-6 example, an 81-mm mortar platoon sheaf is being adjusted where the platoon is
employed in two 2-section positions. Tube 2 conducted the registration. The observer requested
section right, repeat, over. All rounds are within 50 meters of the correct range. Only number 3 is

Destruction
Destruction fires are fires delivered for the sole purpose of destroying objects. The destruction
mission is fired by using one weapon to destroy a point target. It is similar to the registration

mission because the observer continues adjustments to establish a 25-meter bracket by adding or
dropping 10 meters. The observer then continues to fire additional rounds, making corrections to
the nearest 10 meters after every third round until the target is destroyed or the mission is ended.
After every third round, an additional refinement is made, and firing is continued until the target is
destroyed or the mission is ended. Corrections may be made after every round if desired. Because
of the amount of time and ammunition required, destruction missions should be used only when
required by the tactical situation. Only a target that is critical to an operation should be engaged in
this manner and only if no other means exists to destroy the target. An example of a destruction

HIGH-BURST OR MEAN POINT OF IMPACT REGISTRATIONS

The opportunities for artillery to execute a precision registration are limited because registration
requires visual observation on a clearly-defined, accurately-located registration point within the
target area. At night, visual adjustment of fire on a registration point is impossible without some
type of illumination or night observation device. In desert, jungle, or arctic operations, clearly
defined registration points in the target area are not usually available. Special procedures have
been developed to provide for registration under these conditions: HB and MPI registrations.

The HB and MPI registrations are identical, except that the rounds for an HB registration are air
bursts and the rounds for the MPI registration are impact rounds. Two observers, referred to as 01
and 02, are required to conduct these registrations. Each observer must occupy a surveyed OP and
have a means to accurately conduct angular measurement, such as a CLRF. Directional control, or
a line of known direction, must be established on the ground so that the observer can orient the
instrument for direction. For night observation, this may require the prior orientation of
instruments during daylight. The exact location of the instrument and the line of known direction
should be marked so that they can be identified during darkness. Additionally, for HB registration,
one observer must be prepared to measure vertical angles.

High-Burst Registration Procedures
Observing Instruments Initial Orientation. To prepare the instruments for orientation, the exact
location of the instrument and lines of known direction must be identified.

Observing the High-Burst/Mean Point of Impact Registration. For HB registration, the two observers
simultaneously observe time fire aimed at a point that is located in the air above the target area.
This location is determined as follows:

             Step 1. The FDC selects the point where the fire will be aimed by selecting a point
             that is located on the ground in the area where the registration is desired and projects
             this point into the air with a prescribed HOB. This is the orienting point.
             Step 2. The FDC controls the firing of the HB registration. One weapon is used to fire
             the registration. All rounds are fired with the same data.
             Step 3. Each observer reports the direction from the observer’s own position to the
             bursts. One of the observers will be directed to report the vertical angle after each
             round.

For MPI, the registration is the same, except the rounds are fired with fuze quick.

Observing Instrument Orientation on the Orienting Point. The FDC orients the observer by sending
an MTO that tells each observer the direction and vertical angle from the observer’s position to the
selected orienting point. Each observer rotates a CLRF to the correct direction. The horizontal line

                                                                   6-11


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

of sight of the instrument now coincides with the horizontal line of sight from each observer’s
position to the orienting point.

                               Example: Observing instrument orientation

                Message-to-observer, observe HB registration: 01 direction 1164,
                vertical angle plus 12. Measure the vertical angle. 02 direction 0718,
                vertical angle minus 3. Report when ready to observe.

Measuring and Reporting the First Round. When the observers report ready to observe, the FDC
directs the firing of the rounds, one at a time. The FDC reports shot and splash for each round.
When the burst of the first round appears, each observer determines the actual observer direction
to the round. The observers report their observations in turn.

Determining the Vertical Angle. The vertical angle is determined by measuring with a CLRF.
                                           Example: Observations

               01 direction ____, vertical angle ____, over.
               02 direction ____, over.

Reporting Unobserved Spottings. If the observers do not observe the initial round within the
instrument’s field of view, this should be reported along with the location of the burst to the FDC.

                                       Example: Unobserved spotting

               02 round unobserved, too far left, over.

Reorienting on the First Round. Once the instrument is reoriented on the direction and vertical
angle where the initial round burst, the observers report the direction and vertical angle, if
applicable, of the first round to the FDC. This will allow for smaller deviation measurements for
subsequent rounds.

Measuring and Reporting Subsequent Rounds. The procedures for measuring and reporting
direction and vertical angle for subsequent rounds are the same as those for the first round.
However, the observers typically do not reorient the instrument after subsequent rounds.
Depending on proficiency, the observers may reorient on each subsequent round. When the FDC
has obtained enough readings to compute the registration data, it terminates the registration by
sending end of mission to 01.

                                                                   6-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Mean Point of Impact Registration Procedures
In MPI registration, the FDC selects a ground location as the orienting point and uses impact fuzes
in the registration. The establishment of the OPs and the procedures followed by the observer are
the same as those in an HB registration.

IMPROVED CONVENTIONAL MUNITIONS

Improved conventional munitions are base-ejection projectiles with a mechanical time fuze and a
body assembly containing a number of submunitions. The submunitions (i.e., grenades) are
ejected through the base of the projectile and scattered in the target area. There are two types of
ICM rounds: the antipersonnel round and the dual-purpose round. Table 6-1 shows the number of
grenades in each ICM round.

Table 6-1. Improved Conventional Munitions Round Grenades.

                                                   Number of

ICM  Weapon                            Projectile  Grenades

Antipersonnel 105-mm                   M444        18

     155-mm                            M449 family 60

     105-mm                            M915/6      42

Dual-purpose 155-mm                    M483A1      88

     155-mm                            M864        72

     226-mm multiple                   M79         644

     launch rocket system

Antipersonnel Round
The antipersonnel round is most effective against exposed personnel. When the fuze activates, an
expelling charge forces the grenades out through the base of the projectile. Small vanes on each
grenade flip forward, arming the grenade and stabilizing it in flight (see figure 6-9). When the
striker plate on the base of the grenade makes contact with the ground, the grenade is hurled
upward 4 to 6 feet and detonates. Antipersonnel improved conventional munitions (APICMs) are
no longer manufactured, but they are still held in war reserve.

Dual-Purpose Round
The dual-purpose round is most effective against personnel and lightly armored or thin-skinned
vehicles. After the grenade is ejected, a ribbon streamer arms and stabilizes it during its descent (see
figure 6-9). Upon impact, a shaped charge that can pierce 2.75 inches of rolled steel armor is
detonated. The surrounding steel case fragments are very effective against personnel.

Dispersion
All ICM projectiles are base ejecting. The fuze functions at varying HOBs, and an expelling
charge is detonated that ejects the entire payload of submunitions out of the rear of the projectile.
Centrifugal force disperses the grenades radially from the projectile line of flight. The size and
shape of bomblet dispersion patterns are not constant and change over range. Also, the
concentration of bomblets is not uniform over the entire surface area. There is a noticeable

                                 6-13


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

decrease of submunitions in the center of the dispersion pattern that is sometimes referred to as the
doughnut effect. The different types of dispersion are discussed in the following subparagraphs.

Antipersonnel Improved Conventional Munitions. The M449 APICM dispersion is generally an
elliptical shape. It disperses its submunitions approximately 100 meters by 60 meters.
Depending on the observer’s orientation to the GTL, a varying distribution of submunitions can be
observed.

Dual-Purpose Improved Conventional Munitions. The M483A1 DPICM dispersion generally
changes shape from elliptical at minimum ranges and lower charges, to almost circular at
maximum ranges. This variation is because of the angle of fall and HOB. At minimum ranges, the
dimensions are approximately 50 meters by 100 meters unless the observer is closer to the GTL. If
this occurs, a bomblet dispersion of approximately 100 meters per projectile should be observed.
At maximum ranges, they are approximately 100 meters by 120 meters.

Base Burn Dual-Purpose Improved Conventional Munitions. The M864 base burn dual-purpose
improved conventional munitions (BBDPICM) are currently held in war reserve. Despite
having fewer grenades, the dispersion pattern is larger than that of DPICM. However, because
the M864 BBDPICM are designed for employment at higher ranges, which produce a steep angle
of fall, the dispersion pattern is typically circular. At the designed range, the dimensions of
dispersion are approximately 150 meters by 150 meters.

Advanced Field Artillery Tactical Data System Special Circular Sheaf. Based on the dimensions of
a single projectile’s dispersion, the dimensions of the typical sheaf can be predicted. The default
AFATDS special circular sheaf is a 100-meter radius with aim points equally distributed on a
concentric circle at half the target radius (i.e., 50 meters). Depending on the range, the DPICM
sheaf is typically 200 meters by 200 meters. Figure 6-10 shows four guns firing DPICM at

105-mm/155-mm/203-mm        155-mm/203-mm/MLRS
      APICM Grenade             DPICM Grenade

maximum range and at shorter ranges. Angle T can affect what the observer sees regarding the
sheaf. The BBDPICM sheaf is consistently 250 meters by 250 meters. Figure 6-11 shows four
guns firing BBDPICM. If errors occur as a result of the dispersion of the projectiles, a random gap
or overlap within the sheaf may be observed.

Improved Conventional Munitions Call for Fire
The ICM call for fire is the same as any other call for fire.The observer identifies the type of ICM
that should be fired in effect by referring to APICM as APICM and DPICM as ICM. A BBDPICM
is only employed at ranges beyond DPICM as determined by the FDC (see appendix E). Normal
corrections for HE in adjustment apply; however, the fire for effect phase is entered once a 200-
meter bracket is established. Procedures for the adjustment of ICM are addressed in the
following subparagraphs.

                        M864 - Base Burn

                         X         X

                        X         X

          of Fire

       Figure 6-11. M864 Base Burn Dual-Purpose

                        6-15

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                         Example: ICM call for fire

          HOB adjustment DPICM in effect

          P51 this is P87, adjust fire, over.
          Grid 933 876, over.
          Infantry company halted, APICM in effect, over.
          Fire for effect with DPICM

          P51 this is P87, fire for effect, over.
          Grid 372 461, over.
          Platoon assembly area, ICM, over.

Range and Deviation. Because of the size of the effects pattern, deviation shifts of less than 50
meters and range corrections of less than 100 meters should not be made. Normal range and
deviation corrections are used when adjusting DPICM in the self-registering mode.

Height of Burst. Because of the reliability of the round, no adjustment for HOB is required before
firing for effect. However, errors in either target location or firing solution can produce effects
other than the designed HOB. The result is bomblet dispersion that is either too large to be effective
or too small to adequately cover the target area. If a repeat of fire for effect is required, HOB may be
adjusted. The observer corrects the HOB in 50-meter increments—either up to increase dispersion
or down to decrease dispersion.

Danger Close. When adjusting close-in fires with ICM, the observer must start the adjustment at
least 600 meters from friendly troops, depending on the relative locations of weapons, the target,
and friendly troops. Special consideration must be given to the direction and speed of the wind in
the target area. The adjustment should be made with the entire battery. Corrections should be
made from the near edge of the effects pattern. Use of a converged sheaf should be considered to
decrease the size of the sheaf.

Employment Considerations
Improved conventional munitions should not be fired into wooded areas, as grenades may become
suspended in tree branches and later pose a threat to friendly forces. Discretion should be
exercised when using ICM in an area that friendly forces must cross because the unexploded
submunition rate is approximately 1.5 percent. Precautions should be taken when firing ICM
rounds in snow, marshy areas, and loose sand. These types of terrain conditions do not increase
the dud rate, but do pose more of a threat to friendly forces by concealing duds. Slopes of more
than 60 degrees reduce the effectiveness of ICM. Because of the large area covered, limited use
of ICM is recommended when in close proximity to unprotected friendly troops.

                                                                   6-16


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

FAMILY OF SCATTERABLE MINES

The FASCAM are time-sensitive, self-destructing mines that are emplaced by different assets,
including aviation. Artillery FASCAM consists of antiarmor and antipersonnel mines in a base-
ejecting projectile. These projectiles consist of the remote antiarmor mine system (RAAMS) and
area denial artillery munition (ADAM).

Remote Antiarmor Mine System Projectiles
The RAAMS minefields are effective against enemy armored vehicles.The RAAMS projectile is
fired by a 155-mm howitzer, and 9 antiarmor mines are base ejected over the target area. After a
short delay to allow for mine free-fall, impact, and roll, the magnetically-fuzed mines arm
themselves. Some of the mines have an antidisturbance feature that causes the mines to detonate if
they are moved or picked up. If the RAAMS mines are not detonated, they will begin to self-
destruct after 80 percent of the factory-set, self-destruct time elapses:

• M718 projectile. Long duration, factory-set, self-destruct time of 48 hours.
• M741 projectile. Short duration, factory-set, self-destruct time of 4 hours.

Area Denial Artillery Munition Projectiles
The ADAM mines are used against personnel—either dismounted personnel in an armored attack
or on existing antitank obstacles to hinder dismounted breaching. When employed against an
enemy that has a dismounted breaching capability, ADAM mines are delivered directly on top
of a RAAMS minefield. The ADAM rounds are always the last rounds fired when used in
conjunction with RAAMS or other munitions. This prevents the activation or destruction of the
ADAM munitions by other means.

The ADAM projectile is fired by a 155-mm howitzer, and 36 antipersonnel mines are base ejected
over the target area. When an ADAM mine comes to rest on the ground, several tripwire sensors
are deployed out to a maximum distance of 20 feet. When a sensor is disturbed or tripped, a small,
ball-like munition is propelled two to eight feet upward. The munition detonates and scatters
approximately 600, 1.5-grain steel fragments in all directions. If the mine tripwire sensors are not
disturbed, the mine will self-destruct after the factory-set time has elapsed:

• M692 projectile. Long duration, factory-set, self-destruct time of 48 hours.
• M731 projectile. Short duration, factory-set, self-destruct time of 4 hours.

Types of Minefields
Three types of RAAMS/ADAM minefields may be used, depending on the amount of planning
and coordination time available and the density of the mines: planned, target of opportunity, or
other munitions.

Planned. These minefields are typically initiated and coordinated at higher level FSCCs. They are
either scheduled or on-call targets that support barrier/obstacle plans and consist of long or short
duration, self-destruct mines. Safety zones are computed before firing.

                                                                   6-17


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Target of Opportunity. These minefields are initiated through the call for fire and support the
maneuver commander with an immediate minefield. They consist of only short duration, self-
destruct mines. The standard minefield module is 400 meters by 400 meters. Target-of-
opportunity minefields are typically low density with a combination of RAAMS and ADAM
projectiles. The number of projectiles varies depending on the range, angle of fire, and minefield
attitude. The safety zone is based on aim points and is computed immediately after firing. The
safety zone contains 99 percent of the deployed mines and represents the effective friendly
obstacle for maneuver elements.

Other Munitions. Minefields established in conjunction with an attack that is using other munitions
are initiated through the target list or the call for fire. They support operations by harassing enemy
targets within constraints set by the supported commander. These minefields are sized on the basis
of the method of attack and use of RAAMS, ADAM, or a combination in the last volley. They
consist of only short duration mines. The safety zone is computed immediately after firing.

Target Location
Aimpoints must be established for both moving and stationary targets.

Moving Targets. The aim point for a moving target is placed directly in front of the enemy axis of
advance. An aim point is placed 1,000 meters in front of the enemy target for every 10 km per
hour of speed, as shown in figure 6-12. This allows enough time for mine delivery and arming
before an enemy encounter.

Stationary Targets. The aim point for a stationary target is placed directly over the target center.
Aim points are located to an accuracy of 100 meters for adjust fire and 10 meters for fire for effect.
If adjustment is necessary, it will be conducted with shell M483A1 in the self-registering mode.

Aimpoint                 Enemy

1,000 m x  Akm/hr   = X

              10

           X

Figure 6-12. Aimpoint Location for a Moving Target.

              6-18


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Call for Fire and Adjustment
The FASCAM call for fire is usually transmitted and processed in the same manner as other
requests for target-of-opportunity fire missions. The adjusting shell is DPICM in self-registration
mode and shell ADAM, which is always fired last. Targets of opportunity are either fire for effect or
adjust-fire missions. Missions within 1,000 meters of friendly forces should be referred to the firing
unit FDC for computation of the buffer zone before firing. Adjustment procedures for FASCAM are
identical to those described for ICM.

                                      Example: FASCAM call for fire
               Fire for Effect Mission
               Observer: Z57 this is Z42, fire for effect, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: Grid 1804 5132, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: Platoon in the open, ADAM, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Adjust-Fire Mission
               Observer: Z57 this is Z42, adjust fire, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: Grid 180 513, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: 5 T-72 tanks attacking, RAAMS in effect, over.
               FDC: Reads back.

Employment Considerations
Authorization for FASCAM employment is established in the OPORD and is usually held at the
regimental level. Caution should be exercised when employing these mines in areas where
friendly troops may be located. Although the mines have a self-destruct mechanism, the dud rate
is comparable to that of DPICM.

                                                                   6-19


---

                                       MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

ILLUMINATION

Battlespace illumination provides friendly forces with enough light to aid them in ground
operations that are conducted at night. Illumination also facilitates operations for both the
observer and the maneuver unit. Characteristics of artillery illumination are contained in
appendix D. See chapter 7 for NSFS adjustment procedures for illumination.

Illumination Employment
Illumination should be used with caution to avoid the accidental compromise of, or interference
with, a friendly unit by premature or inappropriate fires. These fires must be coordinated before
delivery if it is suspected that the effects of these fires will cross the boundary of an adjacent unit.

Volume. The amount of illumination that is required for a particular mission depends on the OT
distance; the conditions of visibility; and the size, width, and depth of the area to be illuminated.
By selecting the proper illuminating pattern and controlling the rate of fire, the observer can
illuminate an area effectively with a minimal expenditure of ammunition (see table 6-2).

                         Table 6-2. Employment Factors for Illuminating Shells.

           M301A1                      Between Bursts                Illumination  (meters per
155-mm     M301A2                          (meters)                  (rounds per     second)
81-mm      M301A3

81-mm
