Uses. Illumination shells are used for the following:

• Illuminating areas of suspected enemy activity.
• Providing illumination for night fires.
• Harassing enemy positions.
• Furnishing directions to friendly troops for attacks or patrol activities.
• Marking targets for attack by CAS.
• Washing out enemy passive night-sight systems.

Illumination Patterns
One-Gun Illumination Pattern. The one-gun illumination pattern is used when effective
illumination can be obtained by firing one round at a time. To create this pattern, the observer calls
for illumination as both the type of adjustment and type of projectile.

Two-Gun Illumination Pattern. The two-gun illumination pattern is used when an area requires
more illumination than can be furnished by one-gun illumination. In two-gun illumination, two

                                       6-20


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

rounds burst simultaneously in the same place. To create this pattern, the observer requests
illumination two guns. This pattern is not necessary with the M485A2 155-mm projectile.
Two-Gun Illumination Range Spread Pattern. The two-gun illumination range spread pattern is used
when the area to be illuminated has greater depth than width, as seen along the OTL (GTL for
mortars) (see figure 6-13). An illumination that is spread causes less shadow than an illumination
concentrated in one place. To create this pattern, the observer calls for illumination range spread.
The FDC centers the spread over the point indicated by the observer and orients the spread along
the OTL for an automated artillery FDC equipped with AFATDS (see table 6-2).

                                                                                OTL

                                                                                        Target

                                            Figure 6-13. Illumination Range Spread.

Two-Gun Illumination Lateral Spread. The two-gun illumination lateral spread pattern is used when
the area to be illuminated has greater width than depth (see figure 6-14). To create this pattern, the
observer calls for illumination lateral spread.

                                                                                          OTL

                                                                                  Target

                                            Figure 6-14. Illumination Lateral Spread.
                                                                   6-21


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

The FDC centers the spread over the point indicated by the observer and orients the spread
perpendicular to the OTL for an automated artillery FDC equipped with AFATDS. Distances
between bursts are the same as those for a range spread.
Four-Gun Illumination Range and Lateral Spread. The four-gun illumination pattern is used to
illuminate a large area (see figure 6-15). Four rounds burst simultaneously, both parallel and
perpendicular to the OTL for artillery and GTL for mortars. This pattern illuminates an area with a
minimum of shadows or dark spots. To create this pattern, the observer calls for illumination range
and lateral spread. The pattern of bursts is the combination of range and lateral spreads.

                                                                               OTL

                                                                                     Target

                                    Figure 6-15. Illumination Range and Lateral Spread.

Illumination Call for Fire
In the illumination call for fire, illumination is given as the type of projectile. If desired, the
appropriate range or lateral spread is given as the distribution.
Illumination Adjustment. Considerations for adjusting illumination are as follows:
• Range and deviation corrections. These corrections are made in 100-meter increments. The

   adjustment of illumination to within 200 meters of the adjusting point is considered to be
   adequate because of the size of the area illuminated by the flare. Range and deviation
   corrections of less than 200 meters should not be made.
• Position of the flare. The correct position of the flare in relation to the area to be illuminated
   depends on the terrain and wind. Generally, the flare should be to one flank of the area and at
   about the same range. In a strong wind, the point of burst must be upwind from the area to be
   illuminated because the flare will drift. If the area is on a forward slope, the flare should be on
   the flank and at a slightly shorter range. When illuminating a very prominent object, visibility
   is better if the flare is placed beyond the object so that the object is silhouetted.
• HOB. The proper HOB is obtained when the flare burns out just as it strikes the ground. The
   HOB corrections are made in multiples of 50 meters. Variations in time of burning between

                                                                   6-22


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

   individual flares make any finer adjustment of the HOB impractical. When burnout occurs
   during descent, the HOB correction is made by estimating the height of the flare when it burned
   out. When visibility permits, the spotting (i.e., height of the burnout above the ground) may be
   measured with binoculars. The HOB spotting in mils is multiplied by the OT factor to
   determine the height of burnout in meters. The height is expressed to the nearest 50 meters and
   is transmitted as a down correction.

                                     Example: Obtaining proper HOB

               The flare burns out 20 mils above the ground.
               The OT factor is 3.
               The correction is down 50 (20 mils x 3 = 60 [50 meters]).

When the flare continues to burn after it strikes the ground, a correction is required to raise the HOB.
Multiply the number of seconds the flare burns on the ground by the rate of descent (refer to table
6-2). The result is expressed to the nearest 50 meters and transmitted as an up correction.

             Note: When using a night observation device, the observer should ensure
             that the flare burns out appreciably (i.e., 100 to 200 meters) above the
             adjusting point to keep the device from washing out.

                                       Example: Illumination mission

               P53 this is P67, adjust fire, over.

               Grid 616 376, over.

               Vehicle noises, suspected tanks, illumination, over.

               This fires an illuminating round that bursts about 100 mils left of the
               suspected area and burns out 40 mils too high (measured with
               binoculars). Using an OT factor of 2, the observer transmits––

               Direction 5800, right 200, down 100, over.
               Deviation (100 mils x 2 = 200 meters).
               HOB (40 mils x 2 = 80 meters » 100 meters).
               The second round bursts short near the OTL, but is too low. The round
               burns for 6 seconds on the ground. The observer requests––

               Add 400, up 50, over.
               (6 x 5 = 30 meters » 50 meters).

                                                                   6-23


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

               The third round bursts at the appropriate height over the suspected area,
               but haze and the distance between the suspected area and the observer
               cause poor visibility when only one illuminating round is used. The
               observer believes that two rounds will be adequate to extend the visible
               area and reduce shadows. The observer requests––

               Lateral spread, over.

               Two rounds burst in a lateral spread over the suspected area. The
               observer notices two tanks and a number of infantrymen moving to the
               right of the extreme edge of the illuminated area. The observer prepares
               and transmits a second call for fire, moving illumination over to the
               adjusting point, and transmits illumination mark when maximum
               illumination of the target has been obtained:

               Right 400, coordinated illumination, over.
               Adjust fire, over.
               Grid 611 382, over.
               Two tanks and platoon of infantry, ICM in effect, over.
               The observer may also transmit the target location by polar plot:

               Adjust fire, polar, over.

               Or by shifting from the center of the illumination:

               Adjust fire, shift, illumination, over.

               The observer transmits illumination mark when maximum illumination
               of the target has been obtained, adjusts the HE, and fires for effect.

Illumination Coordination. When the observer has located a target suitable for HE or other fire, a
call for fire is initiated by announcing coordinated illumination and the mission data. If a better
means of designating the location of the target is not possible, the burst center of the illumination
can be used as a reference point.

                                             Example: Call for fire

               Illumination right 200, HE add 100.

                                                                   6-24


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Once the observer has adjusted the illuminating shell to the desired location, the rate of fire and
the number of pieces firing should be controlled to reduce the ammunition expended to the
minimum that is required for observation.

Fire Direction Center Control. The observer allows the FDC to control firing of both illumination
and HE by announcing coordinated illumination in the call for fire. While adjusting the
illumination to yield the best light on the target, the observer should announce illumination mark
to the FDC to notify it of the exact time when the target is best illuminated. Several marks may
have to be established because a new mark must be established each time the illumination is
corrected. By marking the adjusting rounds, the observer avoids the need for a separate
illumination round to mark. The FDC times the interval between the actual firing of the
illuminating round and the receipt of the observer’s illumination mark. By comparing this time
interval with the TOF of the HE, the FDC can control the firing of the HE rounds so that they
arrive at the target during the period of maximum illumination. If the observer adjusts the
illuminating fire and the HE fire concurrently, the word illumination precedes corrections
pertaining to illumination, and HE precedes those pertaining to HE.

Observer Control. As an alternate method, the observer may request coordinated illumination and
announce the method of control as by round at my command for both missions. This indicates that
both HE and illumination will be fired only at the observer’s command. In this method, the
observer is responsible for timing the mark and incorporating the HE TOF into the delivery of
fires. As soon as the FDC reports that the illumination and HE fires are ready, the observer
commands the firing of illumination. Then the observer commands the firing of the HE so that it
will impact during maximum illumination of the target. The observer must request the HE TOF to
better coordinate the firing of each round. An experienced observer and battery may be able to
adjust more than one HE round under each round of illumination.

The least desirable method of coordinated illumination is continuous illumination because of the
amount of ammunition that is expended. However, this is an acceptable method when illumination
is enhancing the performance of optics to acquire direct-fire targets. During this method—

• The FDC fires illumination continually.
• The intervals between firing depend on the type of projectile.
• The observer adjusts the HE.

SMOKE

The main objective of employing smoke is to create a one-way mirror so that forces may attack
the enemy without being seen. Smoke is a combat multiplier that can be used in many ways to
help the combined-arms team accomplish its mission. It can be used to conceal both friendly and
enemy troop movements, slow attacking forces, disrupt command and control, and reduce
exposure of critical friendly and enemy assets. Observers must synchronize, integrate, and sustain
smoke to defeat enemy electro-optical systems. The force that most effectively employs smoke
and functions better in limited visibility has the advantage in battle.

                                                                   6-25


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Categories
Smoke is categorized as deliberate or hasty.

Deliberate. Usually, deliberate smoke is employed at the regimental level or higher. Any smoke
source can provide deliberate smoke. Deliberate smoke can conceal forces or protect large areas
several square kilometers behind the forward line of troops with smoke generators supplemented
by smoke pots. This employment of smoke requires large amounts of fuel, fog oil, and munitions,
as well as extensive use of artillery assets and scarce smoke generators. Point or small-area targets
can be obscured by other smoke assets (e.g., artillery, mortars, NSFS, air-to-ground rockets).

Hasty. During hasty operations, smoke is often used by battalions and smaller units against
immediate tactical threats. Hasty smoke employs the unit’s basic load of both smoke-producing
sources in addition to rapid-response assets (e.g., mortars, artillery). These operations typically
require short planning and execution times, minimal coordination, relatively short duration of
effects, and the use of organic assets. Hasty smoke obscurants are created to support small-unit
maneuver or disengagement and must be carefully planned for operational and logistical support.

Battlespace Applications
The battlespace applications for using smoke in deliberate and hasty operations are as follows:

• Obscuring. Degrades the enemy’s combat effectiveness, minimizes their vision and/or their
   ability to command and control their forces, and suppresses enemy observers when placed on
   or near the enemy.

• Screening. Conceals friendly forces, positions, and activities from enemy ground or air
   observation when placed between friendly and enemy forces.

• Marking. Used to identify targets for aircraft, to identify friendly locations, or as a navigational
   aid. Also used to signal prearranged battlespace cues.

• Deceiving. Misleads the enemy about friendly force intentions. Supports the deception plan by
   drawing attention away from the main effort.

Delivery Systems
Effective smoke delivery systems include mortars and artillery.

Mortars. Mortar units can deliver a high volume of smoke at short to medium ranges. Typically,
mortars provide the most rapid and responsive fires for the commander. The various types of
mortar smoke include WP and red phosphorous (RP) (see table 6-3):

• WP smoke. Available from company mortars (M224 60-mm) and battalion mortars (M252 81-
   mm). Mortar WP produces rapid smoke buildup, which can be used for marking targets. Its
   effects are of limited duration.

• RP smoke. Time-fuzed round (M819) that contains RP smoke pellets. At a preset time along the
   round’s trajectory, the fuze functions to expel and ignite the RP pellets at an approximate HOB of
   175 meters. The burning pellets produce a cloud of dense smoke after hitting the ground. A
   3-round volley is required to develop the basic smoke screen. Red phosphorous smoke is
   only available from 81-mm mortars.

                                                                   6-26


---

                                        MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                        Table 6-3. Smoke Munitions Data.

                                        Time to Build                Average Obscuration Length (m/round)
                                          Effective
Delivery  Type of Round                    Smoke          Average    Crosswind    Headwind/
System                                    (minutes)    Burning Time               Tailwind

                                                         (minutes)

155-mm    WP                            ½                    1–1½         150     50

          M825                          ½                    5–8          350     100–200

81-mm     WP                            ½                    1            100     40

          RP                            ½                    1½–2         90–150  40–50

60-mm     WP                            ½                    1            7–5     40

5”/54     WP                            ½                    1            150     40

Notes:

Planning factors are meteorologically dependent.

Data is based on favorable conditions.

Artillery. Various smoke munitions can be fired by cannon artillery. The artillery provides smoke at
short, medium, and long ranges. Artillery units can provide longer, more effective smoke effects
than the mortar platoons. However, excessive use of smoke should be planned and coordinated so
that artillery units can meet the commander’s needs. The types of artillery smoke are WP and
improved smoke (i.e., felt-wedge WP) (see table 6-3):

• WP smoke. Either PD or time fuzed. It is the primary marking round and builds quickly, but has
   little sustainment. With a time fuze, it disperses more for obscuring. It is not fired with a VT
   fuze.

• Improved smoke. A canister filled with WP-impregnated felt wedges that are expelled from the
   base. It is time fuzed and functions at a predetermined HOB that varies with the propellant and
   charge fired. The wedges build rapidly into an effective screen with lasting duration.

             Note: Soft sand or mud can prevent wedges from completely burning. This
             poses a nonlethal hazard to maneuvering forces when wedges are re-
             exposed to air.

Planning Considerations
The factors to consider when planning smoke are weather, terrain, delivery means available,
ammunition, the enemy, and command and control. On the basis of these factors, the observer can
advise the company commander on the feasibility or likely performance of smoke.

Weather. Weather aspects are interrelated. The wind, temperature gradient, humidity, precipitation,
and cloud cover will indicate the effectiveness of smoke and the volume required. Wind speed and
direction are the primary factors in smoke performance.

Wind speed. Wind speed indicates whether smoke will be effective. High winds push smoke down,
and effective smoke will not thicken until it has blown farther downwind. Low winds allow smoke
to billow high and thicken closer to its source. The optimal wind speed for WP smoke is 9 to 14

                                                       6-27


---

       MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

knots, with the ranges of 5 to 9 knots and 14 to 17 knots being marginally effective. The equivalent
wind speed scale, the grass drop or flag method, and the on-board computer of the M1A1 are valid
methods to determine wind speed:

• Equivalent wind speed scale. Wind speed is the least accurate and most subjective method.
   Table 6-4 will aid the observer in determining wind speed.

• Grass drop or flag methods. The grass drop or flag methods are accurate methods that can be
   used to determine wind speed:
    Grass drop method:

    Extend the arm downwind and parallel to the ground.

    Drop some grass from the hand and point to the grass on the ground.

    Estimate the angle formed by the arm and torso, in degrees, and divide by four to determine
        wind speed in knots.

    Flag method:

    Use a flag or lightweight strip of cloth attached to a vertical object.

    Estimate the angle of the flag or cloth and the vertical object, in degrees, and divide by four
        to determine the wind speed in knots.

• On-board computer of the M1A1 tank. The observer uses the on-board computer to determine
   the wind speed at the tank location. This is the most accurate method of wind measurement.

Knots                  Table 6-4. Equivalent Wind Speed Scale.
1                                                     Observation

1–3    Smoke, vapor from breath, and dust raised by vehicles or personnel rise vertically
4–6    There is no leaf movement
7–10   Direction of wind is slightly shown by smoke, vapor from breath, or dust raised by vehicles
11–16  or personnel
17–21  There is a slight, intermittent movement of leaves
22–27  Wind lightly felt on the face
28–33  Leaves rustle
       Leaves and small twigs are in constant motion
       Wind raises dust from the ground
       Loose paper and small branches move
       Small trees with leaves sway
       Coastal wavelets form on inland waters
       Large branches on trees are in motion
       A whistle can be heard in telephone or fence wires
       Entire trees are in motion

       6-28


---

                        MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Wind Direction. Local area wind direction indicates where the smoke will travel. Wind direction is
measured as a horizontal clockwise angle, in mils, from grid north. It extends from the direction
that the wind is blown to the direction that the wind is blowing. A field-expedient method of
determining wind direction is to face away from the wind and measure the actual wind direction
with a compass.

Temperature Gradient. By itself, temperature has no direct impact on the effectiveness of smoke, but
temperature gradients do directly affect smoke performance. Temperature gradients are
determined by comparing air temperatures at 0.5 meters and 4 meters. Unstable, neutral, and
stable are the three general temperature gradients:

• Unstable (lapse). The air temperature decreases with an increase in altitude. An unstable
   condition is characterized by vertical air currents and turbulence. Smoke tends to break up and
   become diffused under these conditions. Unstable conditions should be considered by planners
   as marginal for smoke employment.

• Neutral. An increase in altitude is accompanied by little or no change in air temperature. Limited
   vertical air currents also cause neutral conditions when the wind speed is greater than 5 knots.
   Planners should consider neutral conditions as moderately favorable for smoke employment.

• Stable (inversion). The air temperature increases with an increase in altitude. This condition
   limits vertical air currents. Smoke produced during inversion conditions remains low to the
   ground and may reduce visibility at ground level. Stable (inversion) conditions should be
   considered favorable by planners for smoke employment if there is enough wind to carry the
   smoke over the target area.

Table 6-5 is used to predict these conditions and how the smoke will perform under varied
conditions.

(Temperature gradient)         (All conditions must be met)     Drifts Downwind (Wind direction )

        Stable          Night: until 3 hours after sunrise
      (inversion)
                        Wind speed 5–10 knots
        Neutral
      (favorable)       Sky cover total overcast              Favorable for smoke employment
                        Mid-morning
       Unstable
        (lapse)         Wind speed less than 5 knots

                        Sky cover is 50% or less              Moderately favorable for smoke

                        Wind speed more than 10 knots

                        Sky cover is no clouds

                                                              Marginal for smoke employment

---

                    MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Humidity. White phosphorous and RP smoke are hygroscopic and produce smoke particles that
absorb moisture from the air. Moisture increases particle size and density and makes the smoke
more effective. Smoke munitions usually produce denser, thicker smoke in high humidity than in
low humidity; therefore, high humidity is more favorable for smoke employment.

Precipitation. Precipitation is a natural obscurant. Using smoke in mist or fog produces thicker
smoke that is more effective against electro-optical systems. Light rain produces moderately
favorable conditions for using smoke because the smoke might not rise high enough to produce
effects. Heavy rain and snow are effective natural obscurants and will degrade electro-optical
systems effectively. As a general rule, precipitation pushes smoke down to the ground and spreads
it over larger areas.

Cloud Cover. Clouds will give an indication of how smoke will perform in the battlespace. As a
general rule, the atmosphere is stable (favorable) when the sky is covered with clouds. Scattered
clouds indicate moderately favorable conditions. No cloud cover indicates marginal conditions.

Terrain. Winds follow the contours of the Earth. The type of terrain that smoke travels over has a
tremendous effect on how the smoke will cover a specific area:

• Flat, unbroken terrain creates effective smoke farther downwind.
• Trees and small buildings will break up smoke, which may then re-form to cover a larger area

   and create effective smoke near the source.
• Steep hills or mountains create volatile winds, usually resulting in gaps and uneven smoke.
• Slopes and valleys create thermal slope winds at different times.

Heating effects during the day cause up-slope winds. Cooling effects at night cause down-slope
winds. Table 6-6 summarizes atmospheric and terrain effects on smoke operations.

                    Table 6-6. Terrain and Atmospherics.

       Factor               Unfavorable          Moderately Favorable             Favorable
Wind                More than 10 knots        Less than 5 knots        5–10 knots
Atmospheric         Unstable (lapse) (favor-  Neutral                  Stable (inversion) (unfavor-
stability category  able for smoke curtain)                            able for smoke curtain)
Humidity            Low                       Moderate                 High
Precipitation       None                      Light rain               Mist/fog
Cloud cover         None                      Scattered                Overcast, low ceiling
Terrain             Even                      Gently rolling           Complex topography
Vegetation          Sparse or none (desert)   Medium to dense          Heavily wooded or jungle
Time of day         Late morning through      Mid-morning              1 hour before EENT to 4
                    late afternoon                                     hours after BMNT
LEGEND:

BMNT-beginning of morning nautical twilight

EENT-end of evening nautical twilight

                                              6-30


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Delivery Means Available. The observers will make recommendations to the maneuver
commanders concerning the best delivery means for providing smoke. Higher level FSCCs must
review and then approve, modify, or deny the maneuver commander’s request based on the effects
that the smoke will have on all maneuvers.

Ammunition. Smoke ammunition is a limited asset. Because ammunition requirements vary with
each mission, observers should know the amount and types of smoke ammunition available and
how many minutes of coverage it can provide. Extensive, planned smoke employment should be
coordinated early with firing units to allow for redistribution or requisition of ammunition.

The Enemy. When considering smoke employment, the enemy’s electro-optical capability should
be known, and the likely positions for enemy weapons systems and observers that may threaten
friendly forces should be anticipated. On the basis of the commander’s plan, observers should
determine when smoke employment will enhance friendly operations and hinder enemy operations.

Command and Control Desired Effects. Clearly stated guidelines, restrictions, desired effects
against enemy electro-optical systems, and other smoke employment instructions should be part
of the commander’s guidance and unit SOP. Desired effects are specified as visible or infrared (IR).
All types of smoke are capable of producing visible and IR effects. Table 6-7 shows the
effectiveness of the types of smoke against enemy and friendly electro-optical systems. Figure
6-16, shows the effectiveness of all types of obscurants against enemy and friendly electro-
optical systems.

Visible. The visible wavelength is relatively easy to defeat with smoke. Obscurants hinder enemy
viewers (e.g., binoculars, weapons sights, night observation sights, CLRFs). Smoke can also
degrade friendly visibility. Smoke that is sufficient to degrade less sophisticated electro-optical
systems may be relatively ineffective against more sophisticated electro-optical systems. Infrared
device-equipped observers have an inherent tactical advantage over observers who rely solely on
the visible wavelength. Although observers can acquire targets more easily when using IR devices
through smoke, positive target identification is more difficult.

Infrared. Visual obscurants may degrade the visibility of observers using IR devices. However,
bispectral obscurants can defeat sophisticated battlespace viewers and weapon guidance systems,
such as command line of sight or terminal homing systems on antitank missiles. Bispectral
obscurants defeat or degrade both the visual and IR portions of the electromagnetic spectrum
simultaneously. Firing units employing bispectral fires deliver sufficient amounts of smoke to
degrade both less sophisticated and advanced electro-optical systems. Like visual obscurants,
bispectral obscurants can degrade both enemy and friendly systems. Bispectral obscurants are
required to defeat an enemy that is equipped with more sophisticated electro-optical technology.

Types of Missions
Immediate smoke and quick smoke are the two types of smoke missions that are fired by artillery
and mortars.

Immediate Smoke. Immediate smoke provides obscuring, protecting, or marking smoke over small
areas. It is more responsive than quick smoke, but the duration and area of effects are limited.
Immediate smoke is effective against point targets for short periods. Unit SOPs should state the
amount and type of smoke ammunition to be fired and the number of guns to be used.

Adjusting Point. The adjusting point for immediate smoke is typically the target itself. However, the
observer may determine that offsetting the adjusting point into the wind may produce more
obscure conditions (see figure 6-17).

Call for Fire. The call for fire for immediate smoke consists of three elements sent in one
transmission:

• Observer identification (the call sign identifying the observer to the FDC).
• Warning order (the observer announces immediate smoke).
• Target location (any method of target location may be used).

Quick Smoke. Quick smoke provides obscuring, screening, or deceiving smoke for larger areas.
More planning and coordination are required for quick smoke than for immediate smoke. Smoke
rounds for quick smoke missions are fired in a linear sheaf and cover larger areas than immediate
smoke missions.

                                         6-33


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                 Example: Requesting immediate smoke

               Grid

               Observer: W52 this is H24, immediate smoke, grid 628 545, I
               authenticate ROMEO DELTA, over.

               FDC: Reads back.

               Shift From Known Point

               Observer: W52 this is H24, immediate smoke, shift target number AB
               3058, direction 4360, left 130, add 300. I authenticate MIKE LIMA, over.

               FDC: Reads back.

               Polar

               Observer: W52 this is H24, immediate smoke, polar, direction 4360,
               distance 2700, up 30. I authenticate PAPA KILO, over.

               FDC: Reads back.

               Laser Polar

               Observer: W52 this is H24, immediate smoke, laser polar, direction
               4362, distance 2730, vertical angle plus 11. I authenticate CHARLIE
               ALPHA, over.

               FDC: Reads back.

Quick smoke is effective against enemy positions and formations. A standard call for fire includes
target location; target description; length of the sheaf; direction of the maneuver-target line (MTL);
direction of the wind in relation to the MTL; and desired time, duration, and effects of the smoke.
Fire for effect should be requested for planned targets or refined target locations. Adjust fire can be
conducted when the method of target location is less accurate. When adjusting fire, the OT direction
must be provided for grid missions.

Quick Smoke Call for Fire. The standard call for fire includes the following:

• Target location. Any method of target location may be used. The target location is the center of
   the area where the screen needs to be most effective. Adjusting rounds are delivered to where
   the screen is planned; however, during the fire for effect, smoke rounds will be offset by the
   FDC for the effects of wind.

   Figure 6-17. Optional Adjusting Points for Immediate Smoke.

• Target description. Announce screen followed by an accurate target description. The screen
   alerts the FDC that smoke mission data will follow. Avoid vague target descriptions such as
   open area, tree line, or hilltop. If the enemy situation is vague or unknown, transmit a
   description of the suspected target for screening.

• Length of the smoke sheaf. In coordination with the commander, determine the area to obscure,
   screen, protect, or deceive. The actual length of the smoke sheaf in meters is determined and a
   recommendation should be made to the commander regarding the length of the sheaf and the
   weapons system that should be used.

• MTL. The MTL is a horizontal clockwise angle measured from grid north. Depending on the
   situation, the MTL can extend from the observer’s location to and through the target, or it can
   extend from the most vulnerable point along the route of march to and through the target. The
   MTL is determined to an accuracy of 10 mils.

• Wind direction in relation to the MTL. Determine whether the wind is a right crosswind, left
   crosswind, headwind, or tailwind in relation to the MTL. Figure 6-18 shows how to convert the
   wind direction in relation to the MTL to a right crosswind, left crosswind, headwind, or

                                  6-35


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

   tailwind and how to compute the difference between the actual wind direction and the direction
   of the MTL:
    A left crosswind blows from left to right across the MTL from the observer’s perspective.
    A right crosswind blows from right to left across the MTL from the observer’s perspective.
    A headwind blows along the MTL toward the observer.
    A tailwind blows along the MTL away from the observer.
• Time and duration of smoke effects. Consult with the commander to determine how long the
   smoke effects are required:
    In the offense, the duration of required smoke effects is based on the friendly unit’s average

        rate of speed and the distance of the friendly movement.
    In the defense, the duration of required smoke effects is based on the enemy’s closure rate

        and the distance that needs to be covered before an effective engagement can occur.
• Desired effects of smoke. Announce either smoke to request visible effects or infrared smoke to

   request IR effects.

             Note: During planning, the observer can provide mission information to
             the FDC and allow it to compute the number of rounds required for
             support. This allows feasibility assessment, as well as ammunition
             preparation. The FDO will select the appropriate smoke munition that will
             produce the requested effects.

          Trees

          Vulnerable to Enemy Observation

Requesting Quick Smoke. Quick smoke is requested by using the six elements of the standard call for
fire. These elements are sent in three transmissions:

• Observer identification. Identify the observer to the FDC.
• Warning order. Announce fire for effect or adjust fire.
• Target location. Use any of the methods of target location (see chapter 2).
• Target description. Announce obscure followed by the actual or suspected target description.
• Method of engagement. Announce the length of the smoke sheaf, MTL, direction of the wind in

   relation to the MTL, and desired time and duration of smoke effects. Use the memory aid L-
   M-Dir-T:
    L—length of the linear smoke sheaf.
    M—MTL direction.
    Dir—direction of the wind in relation to the MTL.
    T—desired time and duration of smoke effects in minutes.

Next, announce either smoke or infrared smoke.

• Method of fire and control. This is not typically requested. However, any method of control can
   be specified based on the tactical situation.

                                   Examples of Methods of Fire Control

                                                  Grid Method
               Observer: W52 this is H24, adjust fire, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: Grid 617 538, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: Screen T-55 company dug in, length 600, MTL 1850, right
               crosswind, 5 minutes, smoke in effect, over.
               FDC: Reads back and challenges.
               Observer: Replies.
               FDC: Announces MTO.
               Observer: Reads back MTO.

                                                  Shift Method
               Observer: R5M this is M24, fire for effect, shift target number AB 3062,
               over.
               FDC: Reads back.Observer: Direction 4570, right 280, add 400, down
               35, over.

Observer: Reads back MTO.

                                  Polar Method

FDC: Reads back.
Observer: Screen BRDM-2 with AT-5, length 200, MTL 5240, tailwind,
10 minutes, IR smoke, time on target 0615, over.
FDC: Reads back and challenges.

                              Laser Polar Method

                                                6-38


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Adjusting Quick Smoke. Shell HE is used in adjustments for quick smoke missions. When firing M825
smoke, use the same adjustment procedures as for DPICM. Height of burst adjustment rounds are
not necessary; fire for effect when splitting the 200-meter bracket. Carefully analyze the behavior
of the smoke to ensure effective smoke coverage. Make deviation, range, and HOB corrections
from the center of the smoke sheaf as required. Because the smoke will cover a larger area,
minimum corrections are as follows:

• Deviation. Left/right 50 meters.
• Range. Add/drop 100 meters.
• HOB. Up/down 50 meters.

White phosphorous, M825 improved smoke, and RP should be adjusted as follows:

• WP smoke. Adjusted for range and deviation only when it is fired with a PD fuze. When it is
   employed with a time fuze, it is adjusted for HOB to 20 meters.

• M825 improved smoke. Range, deviation, and HOB corrections are transmitted when
   necessary. Range and deviation corrections are used as stated in chapter 3. However, M825
   improved smoke rarely requires HOB corrections. If the observer spots the M825 as graze,
   graze must immediately be announced to the FDC. The FDC must verify that the firing data
   was correct. The HOB corrections for M825 improved smoke are announced in 50-meter
   increments. The following HOB corrections are given for M825 improved smoke:

    Graze/firing data correct. Up 100 meters.
    Thick/dense separated clouds. Up 50 meters.
    Thin/uneven clouds. Down 50 meters.

• RP smoke. Range, deviation, and HOB corrections are transmitted when necessary. Range and
   deviation corrections are used as stated in chapter 3. However, RP rarely requires HOB
   corrections. Use the M825 HOB adjustment criteria for RP smoke.

                                                                CAUTION

             Personnel must wear respiratory protection (e.g., protective masks) when
             exposed to phosphorous smoke; all types of phosphorous munitions
             produce phosphoric acid, which is poisonous. The M825 felt wedges and
             RP smoke pellets sometimes do not burn completely. This is referred to as
             crusting over. Crusted-over felt wedges and pellets will reignite when
             disturbed and could cause serious burns. Personnel should be warned of
             their use within the area that they will be occupying.

FINAL PROTECTIVE FIRES

An FPF is an immediately available prearranged barrier of fire designed to impede enemy
movement across defensive lines or areas. (DOD Dictionary) Final protective fires consist of final

                                                                   6-39


---

MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

protective lines and principal directions of fire from direct-fire weapons and continuous indirect-
fire barrages.

In artillery, a battery is assigned one FPF. In mortars, an FPF may be assigned to a squad, section,
or platoon. The size of the FPF depends on the type and number of weapons (see table 6-8). Once
assigned the FPF, the firing unit remains laid on that data in between missions and prepares
ammunition. A unit cannot be laid on both an FPF and a priority target.

Final Protective Fire Employment
The location of FPF is typically designated by the supported commander. It may be any distance
from the friendly position that supports the current tactical situation within range of organic
direct-fire weapons (i.e., 200 to 400 meters or danger close). The importance of accurate defensive
fires and the danger close situation usually requires that each weapon firing the FPF be adjusted
into place, if possible.

Table 6-8. Final Protective Fire Dimensions.

           Weapon  Size (m)
60-mm mortar:

 3-tube section    90 x 30
81-mm mortar:

8-tube platoon     280 x 35

4-tube section     140 x 35

 1-tube weapon     35 x 35
155-mm howitzer:

6-gun battery      300 x 50

Final Protective Fire Procedures
An FPF can be either adjusted or unadjusted. Considerations include the tactical implications of
adjusting the FPF (i.e., loss of surprise) versus the accuracy that is acquired for firing close to
friendly troops. If adjusting, the initial target location transmitted is not the location of the center
of the FPF, but a grid that is a safe distance (e.g., 400 to 600 meters) from friendly troops. Because
this grid is part of a final defensive plan, it should be encrypted. The call for fire is similar to the
normal call for fire with the exception of the third transmission. The attitude or direction of the
long axis of the FPF is announced as follows:

• In target description, final protective fires is announced.
• In method of engagement, danger close is announced.

Fuze delay should be requested whenever possible to minimize the safety hazard to friendly units
while providing close-in fires.

Executing the Final Protective Fire. Once established, the FPF is initiated by the company
commander. The observer fires the artillery FPF using an established code or announcing fire the
FPF with the appropriate authentication. To halt firing of the FPF, either the command cease
loading or check firing is given. To terminate the FPF, cancel FPF is announced.

                                                                   6-40


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Adjusting the Final Protective Fire
Manual Fire Direction Centers. The procedures for mortar FDCs and manual artillery FDCs are the
same. The entire battery or mortar platoon fires one round (i.e., a battery or platoon fires one
volley) centered on the initial grid sent by the observer. If the rounds impact as shown in figure 6-19,
the observer begins adjustment with the flank piece impacting closest to the FPF line (in this case,
number 1). Creeping fire adjustment procedures must be used in danger close situations.

                                      Example: Manual fire direction

               Number 1, right 100, drop 50, over.

Corrections are made and fired until the observer believes that a final correction can be made to
within 50 meters of the FPF line. The observer sends a final correction, states that the weapon is
adjusted, and calls for the next weapon to be adjusted. This final adjustment of 50 meters or less is
computed and recorded, but not fired.

Automated Fire Direction Centers. If the artillery FDC is automated, only the center weapon will be
adjusted onto the center grid of the FPF. The procedure is the same as for an adjust-fire mission.
All corrections are automatically applied to the other weapons without firing.

             Note: Announcing end of mission ends the adjustment phase and cancel FPF
             terminates the FPF.

                         Example: Adjusted FPF mission with a manual FDC

               Observer: Number 1, drop 30. Number 1 is adjusted. Number 2, repeat,
               over.
               H12, this is H18, adjust fire, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: Grid 6732 8674, altitude 710, over. Encoded when not radio
               encrypted.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: Final protective fire, attitude 1900, danger close, delay, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               The unit fires a battery one round with a sheaf. The observer observes
               that gun number one is closest to the FPF line and begins adjustment
               with number one.

                                                                   6-41


---

                                  MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Observer: Direction 0810, number 1, right 100, drop 50, over.
FDC: Reads back.
The unit fires the adjustment. The observer makes a final correction to
within 50 meters of the FPF line.
Observer: Number 1, drop 30. Number 1 is adjusted. Number 2 repeat,
over.
FDC: Reads back.
The FDC makes this adjustment, records the adjusted data as the
priority target for gun number one but does not fire this adjustment, then
fires gun number two. This procedure is continued until all guns are
adjusted.

      Example: Adjusted FPF mission with an automated FDC

Observer: H12 this is H18, adjust fire, over.
FDC: Reads back.
Observer: Grid 5784 4893, altitude 475, over. Encoded when not radio
encrypted.
FDC: Reads back.
Observer: Final protective fire, attitude 1900, danger close, VT, over.
FDC: Reads back.
The unit fires one round with a selected howitzer. The observer
observes and corrects the round just as in adjust-fire missions. The
round represents the center of the linear sheaf.
Observer: Direction 0810, left 80, drop 50, over.
FDC: Reads back.
The unit fires the adjustment. The observer believes a final correction
can be made to within 50 meters of the FPF line.
Observer: Right 20, drop 50, end of mission, over.
FDC: Reads back.
The FDC processes this adjustment but does not fire. Data is then
determined for all howitzers. The data is passed to all howitzers, which
remain laid on the FPF when not engaged in other missions.

                                                6-42


---

   MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

4  3            2  1

      FPF Line

                   Friendly
                    Troops

   Figure 6-19. Adjustment of an FPF.

Unadjusted Final Protective Fires. In some instances, the tactical situation dictates against adjusting
the FPF. In these cases, it is essential for the FDC to be automated. If this is not possible, then the
observer should consider the proximity of the FPF to friendly troops. The observer will then
establish the FPF and give the FPF location, including altitude and attitude.

                                         Example: Unadjusted FPFs

               Observer: H12 this is H18, fire for effect, polar, over.

               FDC: Reads back.

               Observer: Direction 3160, distance 400, down 20, over. Encoded when
               not radio encrypted.

               FDC: Reads back.

               Observer: Establish as final protective fire, attitude 1900, danger close,
               delay, over.

               FDC: Reads back.

               The FDC processes the mission and transmits data for the guns to lay on
               without firing.

Laser Final Protective Fires. Laser FPFs are unadjusted FPFs. They are similar to laser draw missions
with one major difference—unlike laser draw missions, the lased points of the FPF each represent
a howitzer aim point. The number of aim points must match the number of howitzers.

             Note: It is possible to create gaps in the FPF wall of steel with a laser FPF.
             Each point is lased and transmitted to the FDC as polar data or converted to
             a grid.

      6-43


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                        Example: Laser FPF mission

               Observer: H12 this is H18, fire for effect, laser draw, over.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Observer: Direction 1562, distance 380, vertical angle plus 3, over.
               Encoded when not radio encrypted.
               FDC: Reads back.
               Direction, distance, and vertical angle to remaining points are
               transmitted and read back by FDC.
               Observer: Establish as final protective fire, danger close, VT, over.
               FDC: Reads back. The FDC processes the mission and transmits data
               for the guns to lay on without firing.

NONSTANDARD PROCEDURES

Nonstandard procedures for artillery and mortars include adjusting fires by sound, multiple
missions, and the auxiliary adjusting point; assisting the disoriented observer; calling for fire on
irregular-shaped targets; and executing emergency observer procedures.

Adjusting Fires by Sound
During operations where observer visibility is limited, fire may be adjusted by the use of sound
alone. Target location may be reported to the observer by the supported unit or as determined by
the observer. If the observer can hear noises at the enemy position (e.g., weapons firing, vehicles,
troop movements) the enemy’s direction and distance from the observer’s position can be estimated.

Procedures. The observer must alert the FDC when planning to adjust fires by the use of sound.
The battery FDC must plot the observer’s location, and the observer executes the following:

             Step 1. Determines the direction to the target and selects a target grid along the
             direction to the target.
             Step 2. Sends a call for fire using that direction and target grid.
             Step 3. Hears the burst of the adjusting round, estimates the direction to the burst, and
             compares it with the direction to the target.
             Step 4. Converts the deviation to a lateral shift in meters. Distance to the adjusting
             point is difficult to judge; therefore, the observer may have to use creeping techniques
             to adjust onto the target.
             Step 5. Determines distance by measuring the time it takes to hear the sound of the
             burst and multiplying the time interval by the speed of sound (i.e., 350 mps).

                                                                   6-44


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

             Note: To assist the observer in determining distance accurately, the FDC
             must announce the precise moment of impact, for example, with splash.

Employment Considerations. The following should be considered when adjusting fires by sound:

• The observer must exercise caution in broken terrain. In hills or mountains, the sound may
   travel around a hill mass before it arrives at the FO’s position and may produce a false direction
   to the burst.

• If the observer’s position is inaccurate, the initial round will be inaccurate. The initial round
   can be used to determine a resection.

• The observer may consider using a WP round with a 200-meter HOB as the first round in
   adjustment.

• The FDC may take the adjustments from two or more observers to increase accuracy in
   adjustments when using sound.

• Aerial observers may be used to assist the ground observer in making adjustments.

Adjusting Multiple Missions
Contact with the enemy may be so intense that the observer must transmit two or more calls for
fire and adjust all missions simultaneously. When this occurs, the observer should—

• Consult the maneuver unit commander, if possible.
• Consider previous guidance on target priorities and mission precedence.
• Use judgment to determine which of several important targets should be engaged first.
• Use target numbers assigned during the MTO (this assists the experienced observer when

   handling multiple missions).

The observer may also record the corrections determined for each target to eliminate any
confusion that may arise in the heat of battle. If other observers are using the same COF net,
observers should continue to use call signs during the mission.

Adjusting the Auxiliary Adjusting Point
To achieve surprise, the observer may decide not to adjust directly on the target, but to adjust on a
nearby point. This nearby point, which is the auxiliary adjusting point, must be far enough from
the target (i.e., at least 500 meters) to obscure the real purpose of the adjustment (see figure 6-20).

Auxiliary Adjusting Point                                  Lateral Shift
                  Figure 6-20. Auxiliary Adjusting Point.  (Right 500)
                                        6-45


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Also, the auxiliary adjusting point must be selected so that an accurate shift to the target can be
determined, preferably lateral. When the adjustment on the auxiliary adjusting point is complete,
the shift to the target is made.

Assisting the Disoriented Observer
Poor visibility, unreliable maps, deceptive terrain, or rapid movement through unfamiliar terrain
sometimes makes it difficult for the observer to become and/or remain oriented. The observer
may call for marking rounds to be fired on a known point, previously fired target, or prominent
terrain feature.

                                          Example: Marking rounds

               Mark hill 37.

As a last resort, the observer may call for a round or rounds to be fired into the center of the
target area.

                                          Example: Marking center

               Mark center of sector.

The observer usually requests a type of projectile that is easily identifiable (e.g., WP) or a
higher burst with HE or WP. The unit may have an SOP for shell and/or fuze combinations. The
FDC prepares data that will place the round at the point requested by the observer. If the
observer fails to see the round, the FDC prepares data that will move the next round to a
different point of impact or that will raise the burst higher into the air. This procedure is
continued until the observer positively identifies the round. The observer then orders a shift from
the point of impact (i.e., burst) of the identified round to a target or object that is permanent or
semipermanent in nature (e.g., a road junction, the ruins of a building). Once this point has been
located by the adjustment of fire and has been plotted at the FDC, the observer may use it as a
known point to make shifts to subsequent targets.

Calling for Fire on Irregular-Shaped Targets
When calling for fire on an irregular-shaped target, the observer must request the appropriate sheaf
or describe the target in sufficient detail to allow the FDO to decide the best method of attack.

Irregular-shaped target choices include circular, linear, rectangular, and laser-drawn targets. For
large targets that warrant multiple batteries or battalions, the target is segmented so that individual
batteries provide coverage for a particular portion of the target.

Circular sheaves may provide the best coverage of an irregular target area. The circular sheaf is
the default sheaf for automated FDCs and has a 100-meter radius. With a single battery, aim points
are placed half the distance from the center grid and radius edge. Large targets are segmented for
multiple units.

                                                                   6-46


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Linear and Rectangular Targets. Linear and rectangular targets require orientation in terms of
direction. The observer sends the grid, size, and attitude of the target:

• The grid is the location of the center of the target.
• The size is the length and width of the target.
• The target attitude is described as a clockwise angle, in mils, measured from grid north to a line

   passing through the long axis of the target (see figure 6-21). Attitude is sent to the nearest 100
   mils and is always less than 3200 mils.

2600

      200 Meters

                                                     Figure 6-21. Target Attitude.

Laser-Drawn Targets. Laser equipment allows irregular targets to be drawn or traced by the
observer. The AFATDS determines the appropriate distribution to cover the target area. The
number of lased points does not correspond to howitzer aim points and therefore does not need to
equal the number of howitzers that are firing. With a single battery, the first and last lased points
are the only points that are also aim points for howitzers. All other howitzer aim points are equally
distributed over the target nonlinearly. A laser draw mission can be used to create linear or
rectangular targets by lasing two points and announcing the appropriate width.

Engaging Moving Targets
Targets, both planned and opportunity, will often move on the battlefield or begin to move after
being engaged during adjustment or fire for effect. Realistically, targets will not remain stationary
for long periods of time. Therefore, observers must be proficient at engaging moving targets.

A target of opportunity is a target that appears during combat and against which no attack has been
prearranged and these targets are expected to be more frequent in highly mobile situations.
Requesting fire against a stationary target is a simple matter. The observer determines the target
location and sends the call for fire. Requesting fire against a moving target is more complex. The
observer must call for fire by using a grid in front of and on the intended path of the vehicle or
vehicles and timed so that the rounds and the vehicles arrive at the desired location at the same time.

                                                                   6-47


---

   MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Intercept Point. The point or grid at which the observer wants to engage the moving target is called
the intercept point. To predict the intercept point, the observer determines the moving target
direction and speed of travel. After acquiring the target, the observer tracks it until he/she is sure of
the direction in which it is moving. As the target moves from point A to point B (see figure 6-22),
the observer can use one of three methods to determine its speed:

• Estimate the speed. Common speeds are as follows:
    Slow––3 mps (7 mph).
    Medium––5 mps (11 mph).
    Fast––8 mps (18 mph).

• Use the CLRF to measure the distance the target moves during a certain time interval. As the
   target moves, the observer lases it and converts the polar data to grid locations, points A and B.
   The observer then determines how far the target moved by measuring the distance between
   points A and B and rounding to the nearest meter. Target speed (in mps) can be determined by
   dividing the distance traveled by the time interval between points A and B.

• Use the reticle pattern in the standard binoculars or CLRF to measure the distance the target
   moves during a certain time interval. As the target moves across the reticle pattern, the
   observer measures the number of mils traveled to the nearest 5 mils. Multiplying that number
   by the OT factor allows the observer to convert the distance traveled by the target to meters.
   Target speed (in mps) can be determined by dividing the distance traveled by the time interval
   and then rounding to the nearest meter.

Once the speed and direction are determined, the observer must predict the intercept point by
adding the total processing time (observer, FDC, and gun times) and the TOF, then multiplying
that sum by the target speed. The product is the minimum distance to plot the intercept point in
front of the moving target in the direction it is traveling.

A

   50 m

B        Speed and Direction

         Determination Point

   1,000 m

C        Predicted Intercept Point

Figure 6-22. Determining the Intercept Point.

         6-48


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

So that the target will not pass the intercept point before the round impacts, the observer must plot
the intercept point distance well ahead of the moving target to allow enough time to get the grid
and prepare the call for fire. Experience dictates how far ahead of the target to plot the intercept
point. An untrained observer should add to the intercept distance half the distance determined to
allow enough time. More time is better than not enough time. To simplify plotting, the observer
can round up the intercept distance to the nearest 100 meters.

             Note: If the observer, through experience, knows how long it will take the
             firing unit to be ready to fire the mission, that is the time that should be
             used; otherwise, 200 seconds is a default time from the initiation of the call
             for fire to round impact. Intercept point can be determined by converting
             this time to distance, in meters, and applying the distance in the direction of
             movement.

                                  Example: Determining intercept point

               The distance measured between points A and B by using the CLRF is
               50 meters.

               The time interval between A and B is 10 seconds.

               The speed of the target is 50 meters in 10 seconds, or 5 mps.

               Total processing time is 180 seconds (3 minutes).

               Time of flight is 20 seconds.

               The distance at which to plot the intercept point is 5 mps x 200 seconds,
               or 1,000 meters.

                             Note: Minutes must be converted to seconds for
                             this to work.

Trigger Point. Once the intercept point is determined and the mission is sent to the firing unit, the
observer must determine a point at which to “pull the trigger.” This trigger point tells the unit
when to fire. This point is determined to ensure the rounds and target arrive at the intercept point
at the same time. Ideally, the trigger point will be an easily identifiable point. When the target
passes over or near the trigger point, the observer commands the guns to fire.

                                                                   6-49


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

To choose a trigger point, the observer must consider the intended path of the target, target speed,
TOF, and call for fire transmission time, as follows:

             Step 1. Determine the distance from the planned target location or intercept point to
             the trigger point. This is done by adding the transmission time (an average of 5
             seconds) to the TOF received in the MTO and multiplying this sum by the speed of
             the target.

                                                       Example
                Time of flight is 20 seconds.
                Target speed is 5 mps.
                Distance to trigger point = (transmission time + TOF) x target speed.
                (5 seconds + 20 seconds) x 5 mps = 125 meters.

             Step 2. Plot the trigger point by measuring the distance determined above from the
             planned target location or intercept point along the intended path toward the moving
             target (see figure 6-23).

If the target passes the trigger point before the battery reports ready, the observer should make a
bold shift to a new target location by using the same trigger point and intercept distances. A grid
for the new location should be sent to the FDC immediately.

Timing of Fires. If the observer does not intend to request at my command or by round at my
command, the trigger point becomes the initiating point for the call for fire. In this case, mission

A

B  Speed and Direction

   Determination Point

   Trigger Point

   125 m

C  Intercept Point

Figure 6-23. Determining the Trigger Point.

          6-50


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

reaction time must be included in determining the distance to the trigger point. Normal mission
reaction times are as follows:
• Priority targets: 30 to 60 seconds (plus TOF)
• On-call targets: 90 to 120 seconds (plus TOF).
• Targets of opportunity: 150 to 180 seconds (plus TOF).
Engaging Moving Planned Targets
A planned target is a target upon which fires are prearranged. Planned targets may be further
subdivided into scheduled on-call and priority targets. The degree of prearrangement varies, but
some prior coordination or action is done to facilitate engagement.
Engaging planned moving targets is basically the same as explained for engaging targets of
opportunity. However, a moving target may change its direction of travel and not travel over the
initially planned intercept point. In this case, the observer must select a new intercept point and
trigger point and engage the target as a target of opportunity.
The planned target location will be the intercept point. As soon as the vehicles to be engaged are
visible, the observer must verify their direction and speed of travel and confirm the intercept and
trigger point distances by using the procedures previously described. The planned intercept point
and trigger point may not allow enough time for total processing time plus TOF. Then, the
observer must determine a new intercept point and a new trigger point and engage the target as a
target of opportunity.

                                                                   6-51


---



---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 7
               NAVAL SURFACE FIRE
         SUPPORT SPECIAL SITUATIONS

ILLUMINATION

There are two methods of employing illumination in NSFS: continuous and coordinated.
Battlespace illumination facilitates observation for both the spotter and the combat unit and
restricts the enemy’s freedom of movement. Illumination shells can be used to illuminate areas of
suspected enemy activity, provide illumination during adjustment of night fire missions, and
harass the enemy.

Continuous
Continuous illumination is automatically used during the fire for effect phase of coordinated
illumination missions and may also be used in surveillance missions. When firing continuous
illumination, the ship fires one round approximately every 15 seconds. This results in one round
bursting, one flare at midpoint of descent, and one flare near burnout. This technique should be
used with discretion to avoid wasting the limited number of star shells (i.e., illumination
projectiles) available in the ship’s magazine.

Coordinated
Coordinated illumination is the most common technique used by NSFS spotters to adjust fire
during darkness and involves the following:

             Step 1. The spotter transmits a call for fire for a suspected target when the location is
             not sufficiently accurate to fire for effect.
             Step 2. The ship fires an illumination round over the initial target location.
             Step 3. The spotter moves the illumination by subsequent adjustments. When the flare
             is adjusted to provide sufficient target illumination, the spotter informs the ship of the
             moment of best illumination by transmitting the command stand by, mark.
             Step 4. The ship computes gun data to fire the initial round for HE adjustment to
             impact directly under the point of illumination burst at the moment of best
             illumination. There is a single salvo of illumination fire for each adjustment.
             Step 5. When fire for effect begins, the ship fires adequate continuous illumination to
             ensure that the spotter can see the target.

                                                                    7-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

ILLUMINATION CALL FOR FIRE

The spotter uses the standard call for fire format and announces either continuous illumination or
coordinated illumination in the special instructions sub-element of the call for fire.

The number of guns is omitted because one gun is standard. The ammunition (i.e., illumination
projectile and fuze) is also omitted. The mission may require reduced charge to prevent ripped
chutes, particularly when firing at ranges of less than 7,000 meters.

                                    Example: Illumination call for fire

               Spotter: AlB this is C2D, fire mission, target number AF 1011, over.

               Grid 344 677, altitude 55, direction 2680, suspected enemy activity,
               coordinated illumination, over.

To differentiate between the illumination trajectory and the trajectory to be used for subsequent
HE, the ship announces line of fire, followed by ready, and a TOF for the illumination projectile.
The spotter must consider the path of the empty canister and its probable impact point along the
line of fire. The spotter reads back the prefiring report and commands fire.

ILLUMINATION ADJUSTMENT PROCEDURES

Spottings are made to determine the location of the flare at the midpoint of descent and the height
of burnout of the flare. The flare is normally corrected to position it over (behind) the target and
along the OTL to achieve a silhouette of the target. If the target is on a slope, the flare is normally
positioned short of the target to allow the light to shine back onto the target. The direction and
speed of the wind will also affect the positioning of the flare.

Deviation and Range Corrections
These corrections are given in multiples of 100 meters, with the minimum correction being 100
meters. Because the illuminated area is large, bold corrections are normally used instead of
bracketing.

Height of Burnout
The height of burnout should be between zero and 50 meters above the deck. Corrections are
given in multiples of 50 meters, with a minimum correction of 50 meters. If the flare burns on the
deck, the spotter counts the number of seconds that it burns, multiplies that number by the rate of
descent, and rounds up to the nearest 50 meters.

                                                                    7-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

             Note: For the 5-inch illumination projectile, the rate of descent for the MK-88
             illumination round is 10 mps; for the MK-91 illumination projectile, the
             rate is 2 mps.

                                        Example: Height of burnout

               A flare burns on the deck for 4 seconds.

               The correction is up 50 (4 seconds x 10 mps = 40 [round up to 50]).

If the flare burnout is in the air, the spotter must determine the height of burnout. This can be
done by—

• Using binoculars and the formula: mils x OT factor.
• Using a technique that involves counting the number of seconds that it takes the flickering

   ember from the flare to reach the deck and then multiplying that number by the rate of descent.

After rounding down to the nearest 50 meters, a correction is supplied to cause the height of
burnout to be between zero and 50 meters.

                                           Example: Flare burnout

               A flare burns out in the air.
               The spotter counts 7 seconds from the burnout until the ember touches
               the deck.
               The correction is down 50 (7 seconds x 10 mps = 70 [round down to 50]).

CONTINUOUS ILLUMINATION PROCEDURES

The spotter adjusts the illumination. Once the target has been properly illuminated, the spotter can
begin the fire for effect phase of the mission. In the fire for effect phase, the ship fires illumination
projectiles at a certain rate of fire to keep the target area continuously illuminated. The spotter
may increase or decrease the rate of fire by ordering an interval of sustained fire.

Cease illumination should be transmitted when the spotter desires to terminate illumination early
during the fire for effect phase. If the spotter acquires a target during a continuous illumination
mission and desires to change to coordinated illumination, the command coordinated illumination
should be transmitted. This command is followed by any desired illumination corrections, target
description, method of engagement, or method of control changes.

                                                                    7-3


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

The ship fires one illumination projectile and is prepared to mark following coordinated
illumination procedures.

                               Example: Entering the fire for effect phase

               Example 1:

               Spotter: 10 salvos, fire for effect, over.
               The ship determines the rate of fire.
               Sustained fire, 5 minutes, fire for effect, over.
               l0 salvos, interval l0, fire for effect, over.
               Example 2:

               Spotter: Continuous illumination, left 200, troops in the open, fuze CVT
               in effect, over.

COORDINATED ILLUMINATION PROCEDURES

A spotter’s request for coordinated illumination may occur when a target is acquired during a
continuous illumination mission or when it is a part of the spotter’s method of engagement in the
initial call for fire.

The request for coordinated illumination alerts the ship that the spotter will adjust the illumination
and will subsequently request and adjust HE projectiles timed to impact at the moment of
optimum illumination. During the illumination adjustment phase of the mission, the ship times
every illumination projectile fired and is prepared to mark when commanded by the spotter. Then
the spotter adjusts the illumination onto the target area by using the proper procedures.

Establishing the Mark
Once the illumination flare has been positioned to yield the optimal light on the target, the spotter
transmits stand by . . . mark, over. Mark informs the ship of the optimal illumination. The ship will
time the firing of each HE projectile to impact at the optimal time.

Adjusting High Explosives
Immediately after receiving the read back mark out by the ship, the spotter initiates the HE
adjustment phase and transmits any subsequent corrections to improve the accuracy of the initial
HE salvo. If no HE correction is sent, the ship fires the initial HE projectile at the point of flare
deployment, which may be positioned off the target location for silhouette or wind purposes.

                                                                    7-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                          Example: HE adjustment
               HE left 200, drop 200, over.
               HE repeat, over.

Transmitting the Prefiring Report
The ship will transmit a new prefiring report for the HE projectile. The spotter will read back and
command fire. The ship will transmit shot for the illumination projectile and splash, out for the HE.
Transmitting Subsequent Corrections
The spotter must preface each command with the type of projectile and correction that should
be applied.

                                             Example: Correction
               Illumination add 200, HE left 200, over.
               HE add 50, l0 salvos, fire for effect, over.

Modifying the Mark
During the mission, the spotter may desire to change the timing between the illumination and
the HE projectiles. To modify this interval, the spotter uses the command advance or retard:
• Advance shortens the time of firing between the illumination projectile and the HE projectile

   (i.e., the ship will fire the HE round sooner).
• Retard lengthens the time of fires between the illumination projectile and the HE projectile

   (i.e., the ship will fire the HE round later).
                                        Example: Modifying the mark

                HE left 200, advance 5 seconds (understood).
                HE retard 5 seconds (understood).

Illuminating During the Fire for Effect Phase
During the fire for effect phase, the ship will automatically fire limited continuous illumination to
provide adequate illumination for surveillance. The ship will fire the last illumination projectile
immediately after the last impact round in fire for effect unless the spotter issues the command
cease illumination.

                                                                    7-5


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

ILLUMINATION PROJECTILE MALFUNCTIONS
