Ripped chutes and dark star are special procedures used by ships and spotters to compensate for
malfunctions that are unique to illumination rounds.
Ripped Chutes
As a result of high muzzle velocity at shorter ranges, flare chutes may rip or separate upon
deployment. Should this occur, the spotter reports to the ship either ripped chute, repeat or ripped
chute, reduced charge, repeat. The procedure used depends on how often ripped chutes occur and
whether or not the target area can be ranged by using a reduced charge. The spotter may also
request that the ship increase the range.
Dark Star
A dark star is an illumination round that fails to deploy or ignite. Such malfunctions are caused by
either faulty ammunition or improper fuze settings. When a dark star occurs, dark star, repeat,
over is reported. The ship should immediately check its time fuze settings and note the time fuze
lot being used. Additional dark stars may indicate a malfunction in the time fuze lot.

SMOKE

Ships can deliver only limited quantities of WP smoke. Early coordination with the ship will
notify the ship of the amount of WP smoke that is required and to load it into the gun turrets and/
or mounts.

FRESH TARGET SHIFT

At any time during a mission, and before transmitting end of mission, a spotter may desire to shift
fire to a higher priority target. To do this, the spotter uses the fresh target shift technique (see
figure 7-1). The fresh target shift technique—
• Allows the ship to accomplish the shift to the fresh target more rapidly than if a call for fire

   with new target location data were introduced into the gunfire control computer.
• Allows the spotter to temporarily suspend the adjustment of fire for the original target, to bring

   fire onto a higher priority target, and then resume fire on the original target, if desired.

                                                                    7-6


---

         MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

           AB 1018
         Fresh Target

Add 100                    AB 1017
                       Original Target

         Left
             280

         OT D          Last Salvo
             irection     Fired

                                           Figure 7-1. Fresh Target Shift Technique.

Call for Fire
The spotter sends an abbreviated call for fire, applying a correction from the impact of the last
salvo to cause the next impact to be on the fresh target. If initiated before the impact of the initial
salvo, corrections are made from the target location data sent in the call for fire. The abbreviated
call for fire omits the spotter’s identification, but includes the following:

• Warning order and target number. Transmit as fresh target, target number and assign the next
   target number. There is no break in transmission.

• Target location. Expressed as a deviation, range, and/or altitude correction from the last salvo
   fired. The shift is based on the original OT direction. The direction to the fresh target is not
   transmitted until after the first salvo of the fresh target shift has been fired and then only if it
   differs from the original direction by more than 100 mils or 5 degrees.

• Target description. Must always be included.
• Method of engagement. Omitted unless a change from the initial call for fire is required.

   Because the essence of the fresh target shift is timeliness, changes that may cause a delay, such
   as changes in ammunition, should be avoided. The spotter may consider using a less preferred
   shell/fuze combination to obtain a timely response.
• Method of control. Omitted unless a change from the initial call for fire is desired. If the spotter
   was in the fire for effect phase on the original target, the fire for effect phase will continue
   unless the spotter announces spotter adjust, over.

                                                                    7-7


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Adjustment
Once the initial salvo for the fresh target impacts, the spotter transmits a new OT direction if
required and conducts adjustment onto the fresh target in the typical manner.

Termination of Firing
The spotter continues adjustment until the desired effects on the fresh target have been achieved.
If the spotter wants to resume firing on the original target, the fresh target technique should be
used to return to the original target. The target is referred to by its original target number.

If the spotter wants the ship to record the target for future firing, record as target, target number
____ should be transmitted after fire for effect on that target is completed. When the spotter is
satisfied with the effects on each of the targets (i.e., fresh and original), the mission is terminated
in target number sequence. Each target must be referred to by target number when reporting the
damage assessment.

                                           Example: End of mission

               End of mission, target number AA 1001 (original target), 1 ammunition
               site destroyed, secondary explosions. Target number AA 1002 (fresh
               target), 3 T-72 tanks with dismounted infantry destroyed, estimated 30
               casualties, over.

NEW TARGET SHIFT

At any time during a fire mission, the spotter may wish to attack another target that is not
considered a higher priority. If the ship has the capability to conduct multiple missions (i.e., it has
multiple mounts and an MK-86 gunfire control system), the spotter can send an abbreviated call
for fire without interrupting the original mission. To accomplish this, the spotter uses a shift from
the last salvo, similar to the procedure described for fresh target shift. The spotter then conducts
adjustment on both targets. The difference between the fresh target shift and the new target shift is
that for a fresh target shift, the spotter conducts adjustment for one target at a time; for a new
target shift, adjustments are made on two targets concurrently. The call for fire and adjustment
procedures for the new target shift are the same as those described for fresh target shift, with the
following exceptions:

• Warning order and target number are transmitted as follows: new target, target number ____
   (use the next target number). There is no break in the transmission.

• The spotter must preface each correction with the target number that is being corrected. The
   spotter may also use the last two digits of the target number for identification purposes.

• The spotter may record the target upon completion of fire for effect for the first completed
   mission. The mission is completed by the spotter reporting end of mission.

                                                                    7-8


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

SIMULTANEOUS ENGAGEMENT OF TWO TARGETS

If the ship has the capability to conduct multiple missions, the spotter can adjust fire on multiple
targets simultaneously. The procedures for the simultaneous engagement of two targets differ
from those of the new target shift because the target location will not be sent by using the shift
from the last salvo. The call for fire for the second target in simultaneous engagement uses the
standard six-element call for fire. The spotter must preface each correction with a target number.
The ship will not transmit splash, providing more time for the spotter to transmit corrections.

FIRING ON A RECORDED TARGET

If fires are desired on a previously recorded target or a planned target, the spotter sends an
abbreviated call for fire consisting of the following elements:

• Spotter identification: Required.
• Warning order/target number: Consists of the words fire mission and fire target number. There

   is no break in transmission.
• Target location: Omitted because it is already known by the ship.
• Target description: Omitted unless changed from recorded description.
• Method of engagement: As required.
• Method of control: As required. When firing a recorded target from the same ship in the same

   firing track as when the data was recorded, a first salvo fire for effect may be feasible.

DESTRUCTIVE FIRE

Destructive fire missions involve deliberate, accurate gunfire and normally employ a single gun
against each target. The expenditure of ammunition can make this an expensive choice and may
require a considerable amount of time to execute. During the mission, the gun/ammunition lot
should not be changed. The ship should be positioned to allow for the optimum conditions and
orientation with respect to the GTL and the terrain in the target area.

Adjustment
Ship adjustment should be used if possible. When the spotter conducts the adjustment, normal
adjustment procedures are used until the MPI is at the split of the l00-meter range bracket.

Fire for Effect
Groups of rounds, usually five for a single gun, are fired, and the average deviation and range
spottings are noted. Then a correction is sent based on the MPI of all the rounds. Five rounds are
fired again. The correction is made to be as accurate as possible.

                                                                    7-9


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                           Examples: Fire for effect
               Right 10, drop 25, repeat, over.
               Left 5, repeat, over.

MASSED FIRE

Massed fire is the fire of the batteries of two or more ships directed against a single target. (DOD
Dictionary) On occasion, two or more ships are required to engage large or important targets
simultaneously. If they have not already been tasked with a direct support mission, gunfire request
procedures must be initiated using the following procedures:
• A collective call sign is used in the request. All orders from the spotter are read back by the

   senior ship. The other ships acknowledge the transmissions. The first ship to report ready is
   adjusted onto the target in the normal manner. The other ships are individually adjusted as they
   report ready. The spotter addresses individual ships (e.g., when conducting adjustment) by
   using each ship’s call sign.
• To bring MPI into the required target area, usually one or two bold corrections are used. To
   facilitate spotter control, at my command may be employed.
• At the completion of adjustment, the spotter announces cancel at my command, all guns
   (number) salvos, fire for effect, over.

SPECIAL NAVAL SURFACE FIRE SUPPORT COMMANDS AND REPORTS

The following are additional commands and reports common in an NSFS environment:
Commands
Check Firing. Anyone can command check firing when an unsafe situation becomes apparent. This
command causes the ship to instantly stop firing. The originator of check firing must announce
cancel check firing for the fire mission to continue.

                                           Example: Spreading fires
               Spreading fires, right 200, repeat, over.

                                                                   7-10


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Spreading Fires. The command spreading fires indicates that fires for effect are about to be
distributed over an area by corrections. The spotter announces spreading fires, followed by a
correction and the command repeat, when pertaining to the volume of fire.

                                                Example: Trend
               Trend, southwest 100 per salvo.

Trend. A trend is the straying of the fall of shot from salvo to salvo. The spotter announces trend if
one is noticed, along with an indication of direction and distance in meters. This is a correction to
move the MPI back to the target.
Check Solution. If the spotter suspects an error in the gunnery solution for a salvo, check solution
is transmitted after the spotter checks the target location data. The ship will respond with either
solution checks or neglect.
Reports
Neglect. This report is sent by the ship to indicate that the last salvo was fired with incorrect data.
The ship corrects the settings and transmits ready, over when prepared to fire.
Delay. This report indicates that the ship is not ready to fire. The report is followed by an estimate
of time in minutes, usually of short duration, that the ship is unable to fire. When the ship is
prepared to fire, the ship reports ready, over.
Will Not Fire. This report indicates that the ship will not continue the mission for a stated reason (e.g.,
a gun mount malfunction, a higher priority mission or circumstance).

                                                                   7-11


---



---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 8
                  REQUESTING AND
      CONTROLLING CLOSE AIR SUPPORT

This chapter provides procedures for requesting and controlling both fixed-wing (FW) and rotary-
wing (RW) CAS. Typically, the terminal control of CAS aircraft is conducted by JTACs.
However, in combat or emergency situations where a JTAC is not available, other Marines who
are knowledgeable of these procedures, particularly observers and spotters, may request CAS and
function as the terminal controllers for CAS aircraft. An understanding of these procedures by
observers and spotters also enhances the coordination of missions that integrate indirect fires
and CAS. See appendix F for a list of air brevity codes commonly used in CAS communications.

Because these procedures are applied by all Marines requesting or controlling CAS, including
Marines who are not formally trained or designated as forward air controllers (FACs) or JTACs,
this chapter will refer to the CAS requester and controller as the terminal controller. The CAS
procedures discussed here are oriented to high-threat conditions, because these conditions are the
most difficult to understand and the most demanding that may be encountered in the battlespace.
In lower threat situations, these procedures may be streamlined. More detailed discussion of CAS
terminal control procedures can be found in Joint Publication (JP) 3-09.3, Close Air Support.

TYPES OF CLOSE AIR SUPPORT

There are two types of CAS requests that are available to support the ground forces: preplanned
and immediate. The type of CAS used depends upon the time that is available for planning.

Preplanned
A preplanned CAS is an air strike that is anticipated in advance and permits detailed mission
coordination and planning via the air tasking order (ATO). Once the requirements are identified, CAS
planners submit the request prior to the cutoff time as directed by higher headquarters (HHQ), to be
included in the ATO. These missions are categorized as either scheduled or on call. A scheduled
mission is executed at a specific time. An on-call mission involves aircraft placed in a ground and/or
air alert status and preloaded with ordnance for a particular target or type of target.

Immediate
Immediate CAS is an air strike on a target of opportunity that was not identified or requested
sufficiently in advance to permit detailed mission coordination or planning and inclusion on the
ATO. An immediate mission usually involves both the observer and the spotter and requires
impromptu coordination.

                                                                    8-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

LEVELS OF THREAT

Some enemies may present an air defense threat that will affect the employment of CAS. The
feasibility of using CAS on a specific target will be driven, in part, by the threat. Attack tactics
selected by the aircraft pilot or flight leader will be influenced by the specific threat. For example,
the greater the threat, the lower the altitude; the higher the dive angle, the more accurate the
delivery. The threat to CAS may be high, medium, or low:

• High. The hostile force masses heavy combat power, including an integrated air defense system
   and electronic warfare capabilities that may seriously diminish the ability of the ACE to
   provide the necessary air support. In this type of threat, aircraft tactics are typically
   characterized as fast and low. This results in a minimal time for target acquisition, which may
   affect accuracy.

• Medium. The enemy possesses a limited radar or electro-optical acquisition air defense system
   that is not fully integrated. Specific aircraft performance and associated weapons systems
   permit tactics that allow acceptable exposure to those enemy defenses.

• Low. No known integrated or sophisticated surface-to-air or air-to-air capability exists. The
   threat is limited to visual target acquisition and fire control systems (i.e., small arms, large-
   caliber automatic weapons, and shoulder-fired IR missiles). In this type of threat, aircraft
   tactics may be fast or slow, at low to medium altitude, with ample time to acquire the target.

AIRSPACE COORDINATING MEASURES

Airspace coordinating measures (ACMs) are procedural control measures designed to maximize
the effectiveness of combat operations by promoting the safe, efficient, and flexible use of
airspace. Airspace coordinating measures are used to delineate or modify hostile criteria, delegate
target identification authority, serve as aids for fire control, and assert control of aircraft over the
battlespace. Air control agencies, such as the DASC or Navy TACC, use these measures when
directing aircraft to and from the target area. Terminal controllers and observers must understand
the meaning of each ACM described in the following subparagraphs.

Control Point
Control points route aircrews to their targets and provide a ready means of conducting fire support
coordination. Control points—

• Must be easily identified from the air and support the MAGTF’s scheme of maneuver. The
   MAGTF FFCC and the ACE select control points based on MAGTF requirements. The
   MAGTF commander approves control points.

• Are given names or numbers and are often used to facilitate the establishment of airspace
   coordination areas (ACAs) for aircraft operating in areas where flight routes or tactics can
   conflict with indirect-fire trajectories.

                                                                    8-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Contact Point
A contact point is the position that an aircrew uses to establish radio contact with an air control
agency. Typically, a contact point is outside the range of enemy surface-to-air weapons. The
aircrew contacts the terminal controller at the contact point during ingress. A contact point allows
coordination of final plans before heavily-defended airspace is entered.

Initial Point
Aircrews use an initial point (IP) to begin the run to the target or the approach to a landing zone.
The IP is well-defined and easily identified, both visually and electronically. Terminal controllers
and aircrews use IPs to help position FW CAS aircraft delivering ordnance.

Holding Area
Holding areas (HAs) are areas that are well forward in the battlespace and provide for helicopter
dispersion, cover, and concealment from enemy observation and fires. To achieve this, HAs may
be located at maneuver, regimental, or battalion headquarters or at the terminal controller’s
position. Rotary-wing CAS aircrews typically occupy HAs while awaiting targets or missions.
While in the HA, RW CAS aircrews receive CAS briefings and perform final mission
coordination with terminal controllers.

Kill Box
A kill box is a 3D area used to facilitate the integration of joint fires. It is a permissive fire support
coordination measure as described in JP 3-09, Joint Fire Support. When established, the primary
purpose of a kill box is to allow a lethal attack against surface targets without further coordination
with the establishing commander and terminal attack control. When used to integrate air-to-
surface and surface-to-surface indirect fires, the kill box will have appropriate restrictions. The
goal is to reduce the coordination required to fulfill support requirements with maximum
flexibility while preventing fratricide.

Battle Position
Battle positions (BPs) are maneuvering areas that also contain various firing points, both laterally
and in depth. Helicopters maneuver in BPs while awaiting the TOT or time to target (TTT).
They are coordinated and selected by the supported commander. Once the terminal controller
approves RW CAS aircraft into a BP, these aircraft should not exit until authorized. Aircrews have
freedom of movement within the BP unless restricted by the flight leader. To avoid enemy
counterfire, aircraft may need to displace and resume the attack from a different BP; therefore,
alternate BPs should be established. Terminal controllers should consider requesting ACAs
around BPs. If an approved BP does not exist, aircrews can plan a hasty BP in coordination with
the terminal controller. If there is not a properly placed or existing BP, the terminal controller may
create a new BP. Terminal controllers identify the BP by grid, known point, prominent terrain, or
by shifting an existing BP. The positions of new BPs must be given to the appropriate FSCC for
fire support coordination. Battle position selection is based on the following factors:

• Field of fire. The BP should permit the sighting of targets throughout the kill zone.
• Adequate maneuver area. The BP must be large enough for RW CAS aircrews to maneuver

   between various firing points.

                                                                    8-3


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Effective range. The BP should be located so that the kill zone is within the effective range of
   RW CAS aircraft.

• Target altitude. Typically the BP elevation should be equal to or higher than the target area.
• Background. Terrain features behind the BP should prevent RW CAS aircraft from being

   silhouetted against the horizon.
• Sun/moon location. If possible, the sun or moon should be located behind or to the side of the

   aircraft. This allows the RW CAS aircrews to view the kill zone and also prevents the enemy
   from seeing and targeting the aircraft.
• Shadow cover. If possible, terrain or vegetation should cover the BP with shadows.
• Rotor wash. The BP location should reduce the effects of rotor wash on the surrounding terrain
   (e.g., debris, leaves, snow, sand, dirt).
• Backblast. The BP location should reduce the effects of backblast on surrounding terrain.

COORDINATION TECHNIQUES

Run-in Heading
The run-in heading is the heading from the IP to the target. For RW CAS aircraft, this may be the
heading from the center of the BP to the target. The terminal controller determines this heading
from the map and converts it to a magnetic heading for transmission in the brief. The assignment
of run-in headings facilitates fire support coordination by establishing a corridor for the aircraft
between the IP and the target. Without a run-in heading, the aircrews may exercise a number of
options for proceeding from the IP to the target. The decision to use run-in headings depends on
other factors, such as target orientation, terrain, disposition of defense, and weather.

Offset Direction
An offset direction is used to ease fire support coordination, safeguard friendly forces, aid target
acquisition, or align aircraft for the attack or egress. The offset direction—

• Informs the aircrew which side of the IP-to-target line they can maneuver on for the attack.
• Aids fire support coordination by restricting aircrews from using airspace on the other side of

   the IP-to-target line. It keeps aircraft clear of enemy air defenses, reduces interference with GTLs,
   and reduces an aircrew’s chance of being hit by either friendly direct and/or indirect fires.

             Note: During combat, the danger to aircraft from friendly direct and/or
             indirect fires is small compared to the threat from enemy air defenses.
             The offset direction regulates the attack quadrant without assigning a
             specific attack heading. This technique protects friendly ground forces
             from range errors in the delivery of ordnance without unduly restricting
             the attacking aircraft.

                                                                    8-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Attack Heading
An attack heading is the assigned magnetic compass heading that an aircrew flies during the
ordnance-delivery phase of the attack. Terminal controllers assign attack headings to—

• Improve ground troop safety.
• Aid in target acquisition.
• Ease fire support coordination.

Attack headings, particularly during visual attacks, may reduce the aircraft’s flexibility and
survivability.

Attack Routes
Rotary-wing CAS aircrews use attack routes to move from the HA to the BP. Aircrews select
primary and alternate attack routes. If an ACA is required, the aircrew or terminal controller
coordinates with the appropriate FSCC. The ideal attack route is a corridor that allows aircrews to
move into the BP undetected. Detection, even if beyond the range of enemy air defense weapons,
removes the advantage of surprise. Terrain and vegetation should be used to conceal aircraft
movement on the attack route, and SEAD fires may also be employed to conceal aircraft
movement and prevent detection.

Firing Points
A firing point is a specific point within a BP that a single aircraft occupies while engaging targets.
The flight leader determines movement of aircraft within the BP. Although the supported
commander coordinates and controls the location of the BP, aircrews choose their own firing points.
Alternate firing points should be identified so that aircrews can displace after the initial engagement.

AIRCRAFT ORDNANCE

Knowledge of aircraft ordnance is important to ensure the proper employment of CAS. This
knowledge makes the terminal controller aware of the effects that can be produced on the target
and how to make target marking and airspace coordination compatible with the type of delivery.
See Marine Corps Reference Publication (MCRP) 3-31.6, Multi-Service Tactics, Techniques, and
Procedures for the Joint Application of Firepower (JFIRE), for the most current inventory of
ordnance and munitions.

CLOSE AIR SUPPORT REQUEST AND CONTROL PROCEDURES

Typically, the terminal controller follows a sequence that will provide the essential information
needed by those who must approve, coordinate, and fly the mission. The sequence includes—

• Mission request.
• Request processing and routing.

                                                                    8-5


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Briefs.
• Time to ordnance impact.
• Mark, adjust, and control initial and subsequent aircraft.
• Report of BDA.

Mission Request
Requests for immediate CAS missions are made to support requirements that arise during battle
and that cannot be identified far enough in advance to permit detailed mission coordination and
planning. Immediate missions provide crisis response to unforeseen dilemmas and sacrifice detailed
planning and tailored ordnance loads in return for timely response. The format used for immediate
requests corresponds to the appropriate lines of the joint tactical air strike request (JTAR) (refer to
Department of Defense Form 1972, Joint Tactical Air Strike Request, and JP 3-09.3). The request
may be made in one transmission or in separate bursts, consistent with the communications security
situation, and should include pertinent lines from the JTAR. See appendix G for a sample JTAR.

Requests for preplanned CAS missions are submitted by using the same JTAR format as for
immediate requests, but are submitted to the appropriate FSCC for consolidation, approval, and
forwarding to higher echelon FSCCs. Immediate requests contain the elements described in the
following subparagraphs.

Identification of Unit Called/Requester. This element includes the call signs of the unit called and
the requester, followed by the words immediate CAS. The transmission of immediate CAS
prioritizes the mission for responsiveness.

Mission Category. The mission category for an immediate CAS mission is indicated by announcing
immediate and the mission priority. The following numerical designations are used to define the
tactical situation:

• 1––Alpha: targets that require immediate action and supersede all other categories of mission
   priority.

• 2––Bravo: targets that require immediate action and supersede routine targets.
• 3––Charlie: targets of opportunity that do not demand urgent execution.

Target Description. The target is described by type, size, and mobility. This information is used in
mission processing to determine the type and number of aircraft and type and amount of ordnance
needed for the mission.

Target Location. The target location is provided by using the UTM 100,000-meter square
designation and grid coordinates (e.g., SS623 456) and the elevation in feet at MSL. The ordnance
delivery systems of F-14, F/A-18A, older model F/A-18C, and some Air Force aircraft require
latitude and longitude, rather than UTM grid (see app. H).

             Note: Although all pilots can manually convert UTM grid to latitude/
             longitude if necessary, it is usually easier for a trained terminal controller to
             make this conversion. Pilots who are distracted by threat conditions or
             other flight requirements may specifically request latitude and longitude if
             a UTM grid is given.

                                                                    8-6


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                        Example: Immediate request

               Transmission:

               Terminal controller: Budworth, this is Tiger 14 with an immediate CAS
               request, over.

               Terminal controller: Immediate, Bravo, 5 armored vehicles moving in
               column, SS623 546, elevation 55, ASAP, Tiger 14, Orange, Chevy,
               friendly troops 1,000 meters south, over.

               Description of transmission:

               Unit called/request identification: Budworth, this is Tiger 14 with an
               immediate CAS request, over.
               Mission category: Immediate, Bravo.
               Target description: 5 armored vehicles moving in column.
               Target location: SS623 546, elevation 55.
               Time on target: ASAP.
               Desired ordnance/results: Not typically transmitted.
               Final control:
               Call sign: Tiger 14.
               Frequency: Orange.
               Contact point: Chevy.
               Remarks: Friendly troops 1,000 meters south.

Time on Target. The time that the ordnance should be on the target may be specified as—

• ASAP. Target is to be attacked as soon as possible.
• TOT NLT. Target is to be attacked not later than (NLT) the specified time on a synchronized

   clock.
• TOT. Target is to be attacked at a specified time on a synchronized clock.

Ordnance Results. This element is not typically transmitted in the immediate request because
responsiveness overrides ordnance selection.

Final Control. This element identifies the terminal controller who will conduct the attack briefing
and control the release of ordnance. The briefing includes the call sign of the terminal controller,

                                                                    8-7


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

the frequency recommended for terminal control, and the contact point or IP to be used. The
frequency is transmitted by using a pre-established color code pertaining to a selected frequency.
The frequency modulation (FM) may be UHF or VHF, depending on the type of aircraft.

Remarks. This element provides any additional information that may assist the air control agency
in mission processing (e.g., friendly location, weather, threats).

Request Processing and Routing
Typically, requests for immediate CAS are transmitted on the tactical air request net directly to the
air control agency, which is usually the DASC. The air officers at intermediate-echelon FSCCs
monitor the requests for coordination purposes and indicate approval in the method prescribed by
the commander—either positive or passive clearance. Requests for CAS beyond the fire support
coordination line need not be cleared, but FSCCs must inform all other affected units (e.g., special
operations forces, reconnaissance units) of these missions in sufficient time to allow necessary
reaction to avoid friendly casualties. In exceptional circumstances, the inability to allow sufficient
reaction time will not preclude the attack of targets beyond the fire support coordination line by
CAS aircraft. However, failure to coordinate these missions increases the risk of friendly
casualties and could waste limited resources through duplication of attacks. The MAGTF FSCC
will establish procedures for exchanging this information with other components of a joint force.

The DASC begins processing immediate CAS requests upon receipt and completes mission
processing upon receiving clearance from the FSCC where it is collocated. After examining assets
and priorities and coordinating with the Navy TACC as required, the DASC identifies what
aircraft are available for the mission. The aircraft assigned may come from multiple sources,
including ground alert aircraft, airborne alert aircraft, or aircraft that have been diverted from
another mission. The aircrews will be provided with the final control data. The DASC keeps the
CAS requester informed of the status of the aircraft filling the request. The DASC assigns a
request number for the mission.

Procedural control of assigned aircraft is provided by the DASC until control is assumed by the
terminal controller. The DASC determines aircraft routing in coordination with the senior ground
combat element FSCC based upon the tactical situation and the threat. Aircraft are typically
routed by using pre-established control points throughout the area. The terminal controller
provides routing for the aircraft until initial contact is made with the aircraft. Initial contact with
the terminal controller is initiated by the aircraft on the assigned frequency

Positive Clearance. Under positive clearance procedures, each request for immediate CAS must be
specifically cleared by the FSCC of the unit that will be impacted by the fires. The FSCC that is
collocated with the DASC must advise the DASC that the mission is approved or disapproved
before final mission processing is completed and control of CAS aircraft is passed to the
terminal controller.

Passive Clearance. Under passive clearance procedures, FSCCs monitor the transmitted CAS
request and either remain silent if the request is cleared (i.e., silence is consent) or interject if the
request is not cleared or if more time is required to clear the request. In this case, the DASC
processes the mission request unless the FSCC that is collocated with the DASC disapproves the
request for immediate CAS.

                                                                    8-8


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

To avoid inadvertent clearance resulting from loss of communications, commanders may modify
passive clearance procedures by stipulating that immediate CAS requests are not cleared unless
the appropriate FSCCs acknowledge that the request was monitored. For purposes of confirmation
or when doubt exists, the DASC will coordinate with the FSCC air officer that is collocated with
the DASC.

                                   Example: Transmission of immediate
                           mission status to terminal controller by the DASC

               Transmission:
               DASC: Tiger 14, this is Budworth, mission 10-2, Check, 2 F/A-18s
               inbound with Rockeye ETA 15, contact on Orange.

               Description of Transmission:
               Identification: Tiger 14, this is Budworth
               Request number: mission 10-2
               Call sign of flight: Check
               Number of aircraft: two F/A-18s inbound
               Type of ordnance: Rockeye
               Estimated time of arrival in minutes: ETA 15
               Frequency of terminal control: Contact on Orange.

               Legend:
               ETA-estimated time of arrival

                             Note: The aircraft flight is referred to collectively
                             by call sign. Individual aircraft in the flight are
                             referred to as “lead” (flight leader) and “dash”
                             (number) plus (remaining aircraft in flight). For
                             example, dash 2.

Briefs
The terminal controller provides a brief to the aircrew that contains essential information for
execution of the mission. This brief may be transmitted in one transmission or in bursts of several
lines, consistent with the communications security situation. The brief follows a numbered format
sequence; therefore, reference to line numbers or elements is not required. Ideally, this brief is
passed to the aircrew as early as communications can be established. As a last resort, the aircrew
can receive the brief at the contact point or HA.

Due to recent technological advances in aircraft capabilities, weapons systems, and munitions,
terminal controllers have more options when employing aircraft. These options mitigate the risks
of fratricide when employing air power close to ground forces. As a result of these changes, there

                                                                    8-9


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

are three types of terminal control available to the terminal controller. Each type follows a set of
procedures with an associated risk. The ground commander chooses which option to employ
based on recommendations from the terminal controller and the associated risks of the current
tactical situation. These controls are risk-specific, not ordnance-specific.

Remarks may follow the CAS briefing, as required. Information may be provided concerning the
threat, hazards, weather, ordnance delivery, attack heading, airspace coordination, and other
appropriate subjects. This additional information is passed from the terminal controller to the
aircrew to increase situational awareness. Typically, the first communication between the pilot and
terminal controller is the check-in brief.

                                           Example: Check-in brief

               Flight leader: Tiger, this is Meat 11.

               Terminal controller: Roger, Meat, proceed to Chevy, hold 13-15, you are
               the only aircraft on station.

               Flight leader: Advise when ready to copy check-in brief.

               Terminal controller: Ready to copy.

               Flight leader: Tiger, this is Meat 11; mission number 10-2; two AV-8B
               currently 10 nautical miles north of Chevy, block 13-15; ordnance, 300
               rounds 25mm, 1 GBU-12, 1GBU-54; laser codes: Meat 11 1688, Meat
               12 1731; 25 minutes time on station; Meat-11 is FAC(A) capable; Meat
               11 VDL code 4927, Meat 12 VDL 4977; we have SITREP C and GRG
               15-7 on board; abort code none.

               Description of Transmission:

               Identification: Tiger (controller) Meat 11 (flight call sign).
               Mission number: 10-2.
               Number and type of aircraft: Two AV-8Bs.
               Position and altitude: 10 nautical miles north of Chevy, 13-15,000 ft.
               Ordnance and laser codes: 300 rounds 25mm, 1 GBU-12, 1 GBU-54;
               laser codes: Meat 11 1688, Meat 12 1731.
               Time on station: 25 minutes.
               Capabilities: FAC(A) capable, Meat 11 VDL code 4927, Meat 12 VDL
               4977; we have SITREP C and GRG 15-7 on board.
               Abort code: no code required.

               Legend:
               GRG-gridded reference graphic
               SITREP-situation report
               VDL-video down-link

                                                                   8-10


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Type 1 Control. Type 1 control is when the FAC/JTAC must visually acquire the attacking aircraft
and the target for each attack. This ensures that the aircraft is oriented at the correct target and
engaged from a safe direction in relation to friendly troops to prevent fratricide. The terminal
controller will always announce the type of control upon aircraft check in.

                                           Example: Type 1 control

               Transmission:

               Check 21 de B1D, Type 1 in effect, advise when ready for 9-line. Snake
               045, left 12.3, 55, 5 armored vehicles moving north to south in column,
               SS623 456, WP, south 1000, egress south to Ford.

               Description of Transmission:

               IP or BP: Snake.
               Heading and offset: 045 left.
               Distance: 12.3.
               Target elevation: 55.
               Target description: 5 armored vehicles moving north to south in
               column.
               Target location: SS623 456.
               Type of mark: If beacon or laser, include the appropriate code. WP.
               Location of friendly forces: South 1000.
               Egress: South to Ford.

                             Note: “de” means “this is.”

Type 2 Control. Type 2 control is used when the terminal controller desires control of individual
attacks but cannot visually acquire the attacking aircraft or target at weapons release or when
attacking aircraft are not in a position to acquire the target or mark prior to weapons release.
Examples of such situations include night attacks, periods of adverse weather or low visibility, and
high altitude or standoff weapons employment. When employing guided munitions in Type 2
control, the aircraft will confirm targeting locations with the terminal controller prior to launch by
plotting the location on a map and cross-checking that this designation coincides with the
expected target area. When employing unguided munitions, careful consideration of the weapons
systems’ accuracy and aircraft navigation systems must be made. Detailed planning and
communications are necessary between the terminal controller and aircraft to ensure a safe
delivery of ordnance.

                                                                   8-11


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                           Example: Type 2 control
               Transmission:
               Hog 11 de A3C, type 2 in effect, advise when ready for 9-line. Mazda,
               360 right, 9.0, 450, T-80 dug-in, NB865 427, none, south 1000, troops
               in contact, egress east to Chevy, final attack heading 300-345.
               Description of Transmission:
               IP or BP: Mazda.
               Heading and offset: 360 right.
               Distance: 9.0.
               Target elevation: 450.
               Target description: T-80 dug-in.
               Target location: NB 865 427.
               Type of mark: If beacon or laser, include the appropriate code. None.
               Location of friendly forces: South 1000, troops in contact.
               Egress: East to Chevy.
               Remarks: The final attack heading is 300-345.

                             Note: “de” means “this is.”

Type 3 Control. Type 3 control may be used when tactical risk assessment indicates that CAS
attacks pose a low risk of fratricide. When authorized in this control option, aircraft are given a
blanket weapons release clearance to attack targets that meet the prescribed restrictions set by the
terminal controller. The terminal controller continues to monitor the conduct of the attack by voice
or digital communications and retains abort authority at all times. The terminal controller may or
may not see the target area.
Time to Ordnance Impact
Immediately following the 9-line brief, the terminal controller gives the aircrew the desired time
for ordnance to impact on the target and any other required information.

                                                                   8-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                           Example: Type 3 control

               Transmission:

               Razor 11 de A3C, type 3 in effect, advise when ready for 9-line. Mazda,
               360 Right, 9.9, 450, mechanized company in the open, NB 922 556,
               laser 1111, southeast 3000, egress south to Mazda. You are cleared to
               engage from time 45-55. Execute attacks north of the river. Cobra team
               is currently 3000 S in position to lase. Contact on this TAD [tactical air
               direction]. Report attack complete.
               Description of Transmission:

               IP or BP: Mazda.
               Heading and offset: 360 right.
               Distance: 9.9.
               Target elevation: 450.
               Target description: Mechanized company in the open.
               Target location: NB 922 556.
               Type of mark: (If beacon or laser, include the appropriate code.)
               Laser 1111.
               Location of friendly forces: Southeast 3000.
               Egress: South to Mazda.
               Remarks: You are cleared to engage from time 45-55. Execute attacks
               north of the river. Cobra team is currently 3000 S in position to lase.
               Contact on this TAD [tactical air direction]. Report attack complete.

                             Note: “de” means “this is.”

Time on Target. If both the terminal controller and aircrew are on a synchronized clock, this time
should be expressed as a TOT. Using TOT eliminates the need for a hack and eases coordination
of target marking and SEAD. See appendix E for more information.

                                           Example: Time on target

               Terminal controller: Time on target 30.
               Flight leader: Roger, 30.

                                                                   8-13


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Time to Target. If a synchronized clock has not been established or is not maintained by either the
terminal controller or flight leader, TTT may be used to coordinate the timing of the mission.
The TTT—
• Is the number of minutes and seconds to elapse before the ordnance impacts the target.
• Is followed by the word hack.
• Must allow sufficient time for the aircrew’s response and flight time. Typically, a TTT of 6 to 8

   minutes is used.
The aircrew must determine whether they can comply with the desired TTT. The terminal
controller passes the TTT to the aircraft as shown in the following example:

                               Example: Time to target passed to aircraft
               Terminal controller: 6 plus 0, 0 . . . hack.
               Flight leader: Roger, 6 plus 0, 0.

When the flight leader reads back the hack time, this indicates a good copy of the brief, that the
hack was accomplished, and that the hack allows sufficient time for the aircraft’s response. If the
time is too short, the flight leader transmits negative and states a required TTT to allow aircraft
positioning for ordnance delivery. The countdown continues from the original hack.

                                Example: Countdown from original hack
               Negative, 8 plus 0, 0.

The terminal controller then re-examines the situation and determines whether the required TTT
indicated by the pilot is acceptable. If it is acceptable, the terminal controller restates the TTT to
the flight leader.

                            Example: Restate time of target to flight leader
               8 plus 0, 0.

If the flight leader fails to copy all of the brief but can comply with the hack, the flight leader—
• Notifies the terminal controller and indicates the specific lines of the brief that was not copied.
• Acknowledges the hack. The countdown continues from the hack.

                                                                   8-14


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                Example: Acknowledgment of compliance

               Flight leader: Tiger 14, Check 10-2, say again line 2, roger, 6 plus 0,0.
               Terminal controller: 045, left.

If the flight leader missed parts of the brief and missed the hack, then transmissions are made as
shown in the following example:

                       Example: Flight leader transmits correct brief and hack

               Flight leader: Tiger 14, Check 10-2, say again line 2, negative hack.

               Terminal controller: 045, left, 6 plus 0, 0 . . . hack.

               Flight leader: Roger, 6 plus 0, 0.

At the end of the mission brief, the flight leader repeats the TOT/TTT to notify the terminal
controller that the flight leader received and understood the entire mission brief.

Additional Information. Additional information to pass along with the time of attack includes
extended information on marks, threats to the aircraft, and final attack headings:

• Laser target line. Line from observer’s laser to target.
• Ordnance delivery. Special instructions concerning ordnance delivery.
• Threat, location, and type of suppression. Any type of air defense artillery (ADA) and if any

   SEAD missions are active.
• Any active GTLs. Given in degrees magnetic.
• Final attack heading. The aircraft will orient when dropping ordnance on the target. This may

   be a single heading different from the run-in heading or a pair of headings.
• Hazards to aviation. Any additional hazards, including terrain and man-made features.
• ACAs. Both formal and informal, ACAs include SEAD altitude deconfliction.
• Weather. Any weather conditions that may affect the aircraft, such as fog or significant

   precipitation.
• Restrictions. Any additional information that was not briefed in the special instructions.
• Additional target information. Increased target information that will allow the aircraft to better

   attack the target.
• Night vision capability. Conditions that may affect night vision capability, such as ambient

   light from cities or flares.
• Danger close. Lets aircraft know the terminal controller will be dropping within the minimum

   safe distance and will require the on-scene commander’s initials.

                                                                   8-15


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Mark, Adjust, and Control of Initial and Subsequent Aircraft

Initial Aircraft. A laser, IR, or munitions mark may be required to assist aircraft in identifying the
initial aircraft or target. The preferred method of marking a target is by laser if the aircraft has a
laser seeker or airborne passive laser spot tracker. A WP munitions mark, if required, will be
timed to impact 20 to 30 seconds before the established TOT or TTT. An illumination mark that is
fuzed to burn on the ground should impact 45 seconds before the TOT or TTT.

If the attacking aircraft is in a low-level approach, it will start to climb at a pull-up point to gain
sufficient height to observe the mark and execute the attack. The pilot will establish the pull-up
point somewhere between the IP and the target. Positive clearance from the terminal controller for
the aircraft to drop or fire is typically required before delivery of any ordnance. Before clearing
the aircraft, the terminal controller should have both the aircraft and target in sight and ensure that
the aircraft’s wings are level and pointed in the correct direction.

Laser Mark. A laser mark is the most effective means of improving accuracy of aircraft-delivered
ordnance. The combination of a laser mark and laser-guided weapon (LGW) provides the best
results. If the aircraft is equipped with a laser spot tracker, the pilot can also employ a laser mark
to visually locate the target and conduct an accurate attack with unguided ordnance.

In a preplanned mission—

• The laser-on time may be specified in the JTAR, and the terminal controller turns the laser on
   and off as scheduled.

• The pilot will typically initiate laser on in immediate and on-call missions.
• The pilot transmits a 10 second warning call (i.e., the aircraft will need laser on in 10 seconds)

   and then commands laser on when ready to acquire the laser.
• The pilot may also use this technique to initiate laser on in preplanned missions. Typical laser

   designation time is 20 seconds, although pilots may request longer laser-on time by specifying
   a time after transmitting laser on (e.g., laser on, 30 seconds). If communications are unreliable,
   the terminal controller should begin designating 30 seconds before TOT or TTT.
• The terminal controller alerts the pilot when the laser is turned on. If the aircraft’s wings are
   level and pointed in the right direction, the terminal controller clears the pilot to attack by
   saying cleared hot.
• The pilot confirms visual identification of the designated laser spot by transmitting spot and
   acknowledges the clearance by repeating cleared hot. Minimizing laser-on time is important in
   a laser countermeasures environment and when employing battery-operated laser designators.
• The terminal controller turns off the laser designator when the pilot transmits terminate, when
   the weapon hits the targets, or after 20 seconds of laser designating (longer if specifically
   requested or if communications are unreliable).

                                                                   8-16


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Offset designation procedures may also be used in a laser countermeasures environment and, if
required, are indicated by the terminal controller in the remarks section of the CAS briefing. At
the laser-on call—

• The terminal controller designates an offset position near the target to avoid alerting the target.
• The pilot then transmits shift to direct the terminal controller to shift laser energy from the

   offset position onto the target itself. The shift transmission can replace the spot transmission.

Refer to chapter 11 for more information on laser operations.

                                             Example: Laser mark

               Flight leader: 10 seconds. (No response is required.)

               Flight leader: Laser on, 30 seconds. (Terminal controller may elect to
               turn the laser on 10 seconds after the 10-second call without hearing the
               laser-on call if communications problems are expected. Flight leader
               may request longer laser-on time.)

               Terminal controller: Laser on, 10-2. The aircraft is wings level, on
               heading, pointed at the target, and cleared hot.

               Flight leader: Spot, 10-2, roger, cleared hot.

               Flight leader: Terminate.

Infrared Mark. Infrared pointers and other IR devices that can assist a terminal controller in marking
a target at night are now becoming available. Although these IR devices can be effective in
identifying targets to pilots who are wearing night vision devices, they cannot be used to guide or
improve the accuracy of aircraft ordnance. These IR devices should be used only in situations
where the enemy is unlikely to also have night vision devices. Infrared marks should be initiated 20
to 30 seconds before the TOT/TTT, or when requested by the pilot, and should continue until the
pilot transmits stop or the weapon hits the target.

                                          Example: Infrared mark

               Flight leader: Sparkle on. (Terminal controller may elect to turn IR
               pointer on 30 seconds before the TOT/TTT without hearing the sparkle-
               on call if communications problems are expected.)
               Terminal controller: Sparkle on, 10-2. The aircraft is wings level, on
               heading, pointed at the target, and cleared hot.
               Flight leader: Contact sparkle, 10-2, roger, cleared hot.
               Flight leader: Stop.

                                                                   8-17


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Munitions Marking. Marking with artillery, NSFS, or mortar fires is an effective means of assisting
pilots in visually acquiring the target, but unlike laser marks, these means do not improve the
accuracy of aircraft ordnance. Before choosing to mark by artillery, NSFS, or mortars, observers
should consider the following:

• The ability of these munitions by themselves to achieve the desired effects on the target.
• The prudence of diverting these supporting arms from other missions.
• The danger of additional exposure of these supporting arms to the enemy’s indirect-fire

   acquisition devices.
• The additional coordination between supporting arms required for this mission.

If the decision is made to mark the target with another supporting arm, WP marking rounds will be
timed to impact 20 to 30 seconds before the established TOT or TTT, and illumination marks
fuzed to burn on the ground should impact 45 seconds before the established TOT or TTT.

When marking rounds appear, the terminal controller transmits an adjustment, and if the aircraft’s
wings are level and pointed in the right direction, clears the aircraft to attack. An adjustment
orients the pilot’s eyes in the vicinity of the target. Speed in adjustment is more important than
accuracy, because this gives the pilot more time to identify the target. The terminal controller
makes the adjustment by using a cardinal direction and distance (in meters) from the marking
round to the target. The terminal controller may need to specify the marking round to help the
pilot differentiate it from other munitions exploding in the battlespace (e.g., from the mark WP,
west 200). If there is a prominent ground reference available, the terminal controller can use it in
the adjustment (e.g., from the mark, west 200 in ravine). The pilot acknowledges the adjustment
by transmitting tally mark and the clearance by transmitting cleared hot (see figure 8-1).

                      N

                                         Marking Round    West 200.

W                                                      E

              Target  Estimated 200 m

                                                                                                  FAC

                                 S

                      Figure 8-1. Adjustment From Marking Round.

                         8-18


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                        Example: Munitions marking

               Terminal controller: 10-2, from the mark, west 200. The aircraft is
               wings level, on heading, pointed at the target, and cleared hot.
               Flight leader: Tally mark, roger. The aircraft is wings level, on heading,
               pointed at the target, and cleared hot.

The following conversation applies if the pilot needs help identifying the mark.
                                       Example: Identifying the mark

               Terminal controller: 10-2, from the mark, illumination on deck, west
               200. The aircraft is wings level, on heading, pointed at the target, and
               cleared hot.
               Flight leader: Tally mark, roger. The aircraft is wings level, on heading,
               pointed at the target, and cleared hot.

The following conversation applies if a prominent ground reference is available.
                                  Example: Prominent ground reference

               Terminal controller: 10-2, from the mark, west 200 on north side of dirt
               road. The aircraft is wings level, on heading, pointed at the target, and
               cleared hot.
               Flight leader: Tally mark, roger. The aircraft is wings level, on heading,
               pointed at the target, and cleared hot.

             Note: When secure communications are not available, an abort code is
             given in the initial check-in brief from the aircraft to the terminal controller
             as a challenge-reply authentication. If it is necessary to discontinue an
             aircraft’s run on a target, the terminal controller will transmit the word
             abort and the authenticated code letter to initiate the abort procedure. If
             secure communications are available or no challenge-reply authentication
             is used, the terminal controller ceases the attack by announcing Abort,
             abort, abort.

                                         Example: Abort procedure

               Abort Tango.

                                                                   8-19


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Subsequent Aircraft. When the flight consists of multiple aircraft, each aircraft may be
individually adjusted onto the target. Subsequent aircraft typically trail preceding aircraft by
approximately 30 seconds. This lapse allows protection of aircraft from fragmentation.

The adjustment for subsequent aircraft (see figure 8-2) is made from the preceding aircraft’s hit or
from the impact of the marking round, whichever is closer to the target, by using the same
procedure (i.e., cardinal direction and distance). The terminal controller provides clearance to
attack in the same manner for both subsequent and initial aircraft.

                          Example: Subsequent/multiple aircraft adjustment

               Terminal controller: From lead’s hit, east 200, cleared hot.
               Wingman: Roger, cleared hot.
               (The following applies if the mark is closer than the lead’s hit.)
               Terminal controller: From the mark, west 200, cleared hot.
               Wingman: Tally mark, roger, cleared hot.

As a general rule, a goal of CAS is to complete a successful attack against all targets on the first
pass, but in a low or medium threat environment, reattacks may be an option if this does not occur.
The reattack should ensure the desired effect on the target, allow visual orientation by the pilot,
and increase responsiveness to the ground commander. If a reattack is conducted—

• The terminal controller will give the pilot an egress direction and may assign different
   attack headings.

• Additional marks or a narrative description of target location in relation to the last mark, last
   hit, landmarks, terrain features, or friendly positions may be given.

• The aircraft may also be returned to the IP for reattack.

Dash 2 N

                                “From Lead’s
                                Hit East 200.”

W                                                               E

                    Lead’s Hit                   Target

                                Estimated 200 m

                           S

Figure 8-2. Adjustment for Dash 2.

                  8-20


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Report of Battle Damage Assessment
Following completion of the attack, a BDA report is provided by the terminal controller. The
report may be transmitted to the pilot and/or the DASC. At a minimum, the SALUTE report
should include a BDA.

                      Example: Report on immediate close air support mission
               Transmission:
               Check 21 de A3C, inflight report, over.
               Check 21, T6G, SS619 456, 0905, 4 armored vehicles destroyed, 1 turning
               east, 10 killed in action, mission successful.
               Description of Transmission:
               Call sign: Check 21.
               Mission number: T6G.
               Location: SS 619 456.
               Time-on-target: 0905.
               Results: 4 armored vehicles destroyed, 1 turning east, 10 killed in
               action, mission successful.
               “de” means “this is.”

AIR-DELIVERED ORDNANCE NEAR FRIENDLY TROOPS

The accuracy of air-delivered ordnance is subject to many variables. Therefore, unlike indirect-
fire weapons, a danger close distance for the safe separation of air-delivered ordnance is difficult
to establish. When air-delivered ordnance is to be delivered near friendly forces (i.e., danger
close), a careful analysis must be made concerning the following:
• The air control agency must consider the situation when selecting and assigning aircraft.
• The pilot must incorporate this situation when selecting attack tactics.
• The terminal controller must provide data/instructions to assist the pilot in ensuring safe

   separation.

                                                                   8-21


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

As with all CAS missions, the supported commander must approve its delivery. Factors that may
be considered when delivering air-delivered ordnance in the vicinity of friendly troops include
the following:

• Type and size of ordnance. The accuracy of air-delivered ordnance varies with the type, size,
   and method of delivery of the ordnance; the type of aircraft; and the pilot’s proficiency.

• Type of aircraft and weapons system. The accuracy of air-delivered ordnance varies with the
   type of aircraft and weapons system being employed. Sophisticated weapons enhance delivery
   accuracy and reliability. Generally, high-angle delivery enhances accuracy.

• Attack heading. The most common inaccuracies in delivering ordnance occur in low dive
   angles, which result in errors in range along the attack heading of the aircraft. Therefore, in
   proximity to friendly forces, an attack heading that is parallel to friendly troops enhances safety
   and is preferred.

• Weather in the target area. Low ceiling restricts aircraft tactics and acquisition of the target.
• Terrain in the target area. The position of the target on terrain features (e.g., in a draw, on a

   hill) will influence the aircraft’s attack tactics and accuracy.
• Threat. The threat level and type of threat influence attack tactics. In a high threat environment,

   low-level, high-speed tactics may reduce accuracy. The characteristics of the threat weapons (e.g.,
   range, method of acquisition) will influence tactics as well.
• Target/mark availability and visibility. The availability of a mark and its acquisition by the pilot
   enhance the safe delivery of air-delivered ordnance.
• Terminal controller proficiency. The availability of an experienced JTAC with the ability to
   control the air strike will enhance the safe delivery of ordnance. The ability to control depends
   on the terminal controller’s ability to see the target, mark, and aircraft during its attack and
   communications.
• Type and degree of friendly cover. Risk estimate distances allow the commander to estimate
   risk in terms of friendly casualties that may result from fires near friendly troops along the
   forward line of the commander’s own troops. Friendly forces outside the 0.1 percent
   probability of incapacitation distance may still be at risk from fragments, but at a lower risk.
   Taking steps to reduce risk, such as placing troops behind cover in the prone, may mitigate
   the risk.

See MCRP 3-31.6 for the most current inventory of ordnance, munitions, and corresponding risk
estimate distances.

EMERGENCY PROCEDURES FOR REQUESTING CLOSE AIR SUPPORT

During a combat emergency situation, the need may arise for an untrained Marine to request and
adjust CAS. After providing identification, the Marine requester must specify on what type of
target CAS is desired, where the target is located, when the air strike is desired, and whether or not
the delivery can be controlled.

                                                                   8-22


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                Example: Emergency transmission of a request for close air support

               Transmission:

               I have 5 armored vehicles moving in column on the road. Coordinates
               SS623 456, elevation 55. Need support ASAP. I cannot control.
               Description of Transmission:

               What (target description): I have 5 armored vehicles moving in column
               on road.
               Where (target location): Coordinates SS623 456, elevation 55.
               When (time that the air strike is required) (e.g., ASAP, NLT, at a
               specific time): Need support ASAP.
               Whether (statement of whether the requester can control, observe, mark,
               correct): I cannot control.

AC-130 CALL FOR FIRE

The AC-130 is a CAS platform that provides unique capabilities to the terminal controller. Its
extended loiter time during the hours of darkness and precision fire control systems offer the
ground maneuver unit some distinct advantages. Due to the accuracy of the fire control system,
ordnance can be delivered very close to friendly positions. Additionally, sensor target acquisition
capabilities, coupled with ground beacon position marking give the AC-130 a limited adverse
weather capability. Before the AC-130 attack can commence, the terminal controller’s position
must be identified. Various methods to identify position include strobe lights, flares, GLINT [gated
laser intensifier] tape, and electronic beacons.

Format
The call for fire is a specific format for the AC-130 and is different from the 9-line briefing
format. In addition to standard briefing items, additional information, such as a detailed threat
description, marking of friendly locations, identifiable ground features, and the ground
commander’s willingness to accept danger close, must be included. Because the AC-130 is
capable of extended loiter, the aircrews can work a series of targets with a single ground party.
When this occurs—

• The format may be abbreviated, but must include the magnetic bearing and range to target in
   meters, from the friendly position to the target.

• A brief description of the target must be included.

                                                                   8-23


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Transmission of the fire mission is clearance to fire unless fires will impact danger close. Danger
close is 165 meters for the 105-mm cannon, 75 meters for the 40-mm cannon, and 65 meters for
the 25-mm gun. To authorize danger close, the terminal controller will announce danger close in
the remarks after the call for fire and provide the ground commander’s initials.

                                   Example: AC-130 call for fire format

               AC-130 C/S: Spooky-90, this is W3T, fire mission, over.
               Observer C/S: My position grid NB 411 252 (e.g., TRP, grid).
               Marked by_________________ (e.g., strobe, beacon, IR strobe) over.
               Direction 275, distance 200 (magnetic bearing & range in meters, TRP,
               grid).
               Marked by___________ (e.g., IR pointer, tracer), over.
               Insurgents with SA-7s 500 meters to the east, danger close BAL, over.
               (e.g., threats, danger close clearance, restrictions, at my command).
               Description of Transmission

               Observer ID/warning order: ___________, this is ___________ (e.g.,
               AC- 130 C/S), fire mission, over (observer C/S).
               Friendly location/marked: My position _________ (e.g., TRP, grid).
               Marked by ______________ (e.g. strobe, beacon, IR strobe).
               Target location: ________________, (magnetic bearing, range in
               meters, TRP, grid).
               Target description/mark: Marked by ______________, (e.g., IR pointer,
               tracer).
               Remarks: ____________________________, over (e.g. threats, danger
               close clearance, restrictions, at my command).
               Legend:

               BAL-initials required from the ground commander to drop ordnance
               danger close
               C/S-call sign
               ID-identification

                                                                   8-24


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Adjustments
Typically, adjustments are made using cardinal directions and distances. The AC-130 cannot
distinguish colors because the display is black and white; however, light and dark colors are
distinguishable. Additional considerations include the following:
• Do not reference clock positions for adjustments, as the aircraft flies a circular orbit.
• Do not give run-in headings.
• Do not give no fire headings. Only give no fire areas.

                                            Example: Adjustments
               Northeast 200, over.
General Rules
Although it is an accurate and reliable weapons system, there are certain limitations on the AC-130.
Firing down an incline can cause considerable miss distances. Due to the accuracy of the
weapons system and the proximity of fires to friendly troops, lethal areas of fragmentation
should be considered.

                                                                   8-25


---



---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                      CHAPTER 9
   SUPPRESSION OF ENEMY AIR DEFENSES

Suppression of enemy air defenses is the activity that neutralizes, destroys, or temporarily
degrades enemy air defenses in a specific area by physical attack or electronic attack. Suppression
of enemy air defenses should be considered when friendly or unmanned aircraft cannot complete
their missions without critically exposing themselves to enemy air defenses. Although typically
associated with offensive air support (OAS) missions, SEAD may be employed to protect any
type of aircraft or unmanned aerial vehicle mission, including air reconnaissance, assault support,
antiair warfare, and electronic warfare. When determined to be appropriate, SEAD fires are
delivered during the critically vulnerable portions of friendly flight profiles. Although SEAD can
be provided by various destructive and disruptive means from either ground or aviation forces,
this chapter will focus on the delivery and coordination of artillery and NSFS SEAD fire missions.

COORDINATION

Because aircrews may rely predominately on closely delivered SEAD fires for protection, all
details of SEAD missions must be closely coordinated between aircrews, terminal controllers,
spotters and observers, and fire support coordinators. When coordinating timing for SEAD
missions, everything is based on the timing of the air mission. Supporting indirect fires will be
scheduled based on a specific aircraft event time (e.g., CAS TOT/TTT, assault support L-hour,
unmanned aerial vehicle mission area arrival time). Coordination should ensure that aircraft are
protected not only from enemy fires, but also from friendly SEAD fires. Protection from friendly
fires is achieved by separating aircraft from SEAD fires by time and/or distance. Munition
marking rounds may be incorporated into SEAD fires to aid target identification by OAS
aircraft. Depending on the type of air mission supported, SEAD fire missions are either planned
or immediate.

Planned Missions
Planned SEAD missions are scheduled during fire planning to support preplanned or on-call air
missions. Units that request these types of air missions must consider the air defense threat and
submit requests for SEAD support as appropriate. Typically, known ADA targets will be
attacked immediately; however, support for suppressing undetected systems must be planned.
Planned SEAD missions are the result of coordinated planning by the air support request, the
appropriate FSCCs, and the unit providing aviation or unmanned aircraft support. See MCTP 3-10F
for more information on the fire support planning process.

                                                                    9-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Immediate Missions
Immediate SEAD missions are conducted to support immediate air missions. Because of the
urgency of immediate air requests, planning and coordination for immediate SEAD missions are
abbreviated. Critical SEAD decisions are made primarily by the requesting unit under the
supervision of the battalion FSCC. Requests for immediate artillery, mortar, or NSFS SEAD fires
are made as calls for fire.

DECISION MAKING

Validating Requirements
Suppression of enemy air defenses fire missions do not guarantee safety from enemy air
defenses. These fire missions divert indirect-fire weapons from other fire support tasks and may
expose those weapons to enemy counterfire. Therefore, terminal controllers should first attempt
to route aircraft away from enemy air defenses rather than automatically request SEAD fires. If
aircraft cannot be routed away from enemy air defenses, then fire support personnel must balance
aircraft vulnerability against the risk of exposing indirect-fire weapons to determine whether
SEAD fires are appropriate for that air mission. The temporary diversion of fire support and the
consumption of fire support resources must also be considered. Before requesting OAS that would
