require SEAD support, fire support personnel must first consider whether artillery or NSFS
could range the mission target and achieve the desired effects. If so, an artillery or NSFS attack
would be more efficient than an OAS attack supported by SEAD. If the mission target cannot be
ranged by indirect fire or is more efficiently attacked by aircraft, OAS supported by SEAD fires
may be appropriate.

Selecting Targets
Targets should be selected based on aircraft flight tactics, routing, and enemy air defense
capabilities. The prerequisite to selection is target location. Often, enemy ADA is not detected
until aviation enters the area; therefore, observers must be able to acquire ADA based on its
battlespace signature. Suppressive fires are placed as accurately as possible on enemy air defense
sites that can engage aircraft.

Selecting the Appropriate Weapons System and Firing Unit
An available weapons system and a firing unit that is capable of firing the appropriate ordnance
when and where required must be selected. Requirements include accuracy, range, ordnance
availability, and state of training. The selection of a particular weapons system and/or firing unit
should not adversely affect other fire support requirements. Although several indirect-fire systems
are capable of delivering SEAD fires and target marks, artillery is usually the most appropriate
system based on range, responsiveness, ordnance, and fire direction capabilities.

Determining Marking Requirements
Specific aircraft missions, such as CAS, may require a mark to orient the aircrew onto the target.

                                                                    9-2


---

            MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Selecting the Appropriate Ordnance
Ordnance must be selected based on its ability to most effectively suppress SEAD targets without
endangering the aircrew or hindering future maneuvers. When firing a munition marking round,
select the ordnance that will best identify the target to the aircrew. Refer to table 9-1 for more
information on the vulnerability of enemy air defense systems and suitability of specific ordnance.

Determining the Method of Separation
To prevent fratricide, aircraft must be separated from SEAD fires and trajectories. Separation may
be by distance (i.e., lateral, altitude, or a combination of altitude and lateral) or by time (i.e.,
interrupted or nonstandard). Separation techniques are discussed in further detail later in the chapter.

Table 9-1. Vulnerabilities of Selected Air Defense Weapons.

     Weapon                 Vulnerabilities
ZPU-3/ZU-23     Exposed personnel
                Optics
ZSU 57-2/S-60   Exposed personnel and gunners
                Thin armor plating

ZSU 23-4 2S6    Optics

SA-7/14, SA-16  Exposed radar and antennas
SA-9/13         Exposed gunners
                Exposed missiles
                Optics
                Thin armor plating

SA-6, SA-8      Exposed radar and antennas
                Exposed missiles

                Susceptibility of radar to jamming

Determining Duration and Volume of Fires
The duration and volume of fires required to protect friendly aircraft during vulnerable periods of
flight must be determined by consideration of the following:

• Vulnerability, resiliency, range, and method of acquisition of the targeted enemy air defense
   weapons.

• Number and type of friendly aircraft.
• Aircraft flight tactics and the separation required between aircraft and SEAD fires.
• Type of ordnance used for suppression.
• Vulnerability of the SEAD firing unit.

If aircraft must fly near SEAD trajectories or effects, SEAD fires must be separated to prevent
fratricide.

                9-3


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Timing the Delivery of Fires
Use of a synchronized clock to coordinate timing, such as CAS TOT 05, is the desired means of
timing fires. As an alternate method, elapsed time can be used (e.g., CAS TTT 6 plus 0, 0 . . . stand
by, hack). When selecting a synchronized or elapsed time to coordinate ground and air activities,
terminal controllers and observers must allow sufficient time for all units to prepare for the
mission and for FSCC coordination. Observers should send calls for fire as early as possible
during aircraft routing (e.g., before the aircraft departs the IP to ensure that firing units can
deliver SEAD fires on time).

Preparing the Call for Fire
When all SEAD decisions have been made, the appropriate call for fire should be prepared and
transmitted, ensuring that all required coordination is completed.

SEPARATION TECHNIQUES

Separation by distance permits the continuous attack of SEAD targets and usually requires less
coordination than separation by time. If distance separation is too restrictive on aircraft routing,
time separation may be used to protect aircraft from friendly fires.

Time separation is the least flexible and often precludes suppressive fires at critical moments. If an
aircraft must fly near the effects or trajectories of SEAD fires, SEAD fires may need to be
interrupted to ensure aircraft safety, which is a form of time separation. The technique that
requires the least coordination while still providing adequate flexibility and protection to the
aircraft should be selected.

Distance Separation

Lateral Separation. Lateral separation is effective for coordinating SEAD fires against targets that
are safely separated from flight routes and when aircraft can be routed away from SEAD
trajectories and targets (see figure 9-1). For low-level CAS attacks, lateral separation should be
used when aircraft and firing units engage safely-separated targets and when aircraft will not
cross GTLs (see figure 9-2).

The minimum separation distance between SEAD targets and aircraft routes varies based on SEAD
ordnance and its fragmentation pattern. Terminal controllers or the FSCC must know the GTL so
that they can restrict aircraft from crossing trajectories. Establishing a temporary informal ACA is
one method of maintaining lateral separation (e.g., stay out of quadrant northeast of grid 6215,
stay west of river).

Altitude Separation. Altitude separation is effective when aircraft can safely remain above or
below indirect-fire trajectories.

                                                                    9-4


---

Protected Flight  MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                                                                                                     Line 15
                                                                                                               Grid

                                                                                                                       Unprotected Flight
                                                                                                        Grid

                                                                                                               Line
                                                                                                                     62

Figure 9-1. Artillery/Assault Support Aircraft Lateral Separation.

                                                                          Unprotected Flight
                                     Protected Flight

Figure 9-2. Artillery/Close Air Support Aircraft Lateral Separation.
                                         9-5


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Establishing a temporary informal ACA is one method of maintaining altitude separation (e.g.,
stay above 3,000 ft MSL) (see figure 9-3). This technique can be used to support high-level CAS
attacks. Altitude separation can also be limited to a specific area to give aircraft more freedom to
maneuver (e.g., stay below 500 ft MSL in quadrant northeast of grid 6215) (see figure 9-4). See
MCTP 3-10F for more information on computation of altitudes.

Altitude and Lateral Separation. Altitude and lateral separation is the most restrictive technique for
aircraft routing but may be required when aircraft must cross the firing unit’s GTL. This technique
requires aircraft to remain above or below indirect-fire trajectories at certain points. For low-
level CAS attacks, this technique is effective—

• When aircraft and firing units engage safely separated targets, but the CAS target requires
   crossing the GTL (see figure 9-5).

• If helicopters can be protected by routing them to fly under SEAD indirect-fire trajectories and
   away from SEAD targets (see figure 9-6).

Stay above 3,000 ft MSL  Protected Flight

                                                                    Unprotected Flight

    Stay above 3,000 ft MSL in                                                        ft MSL

                         Figure 9-4. Artillery/Close Air Support Aircraft Altitude Separation.

Separation may be increased by firing a high-angle or reduced charge. Establishing a temporary
informal ACA is one method of maintaining altitude and lateral separation (e.g., stay below 1,000
feet MSL and between easting grid lines 58 and 62).

When CAS and indirect fires engage the same target, a stay-above altitude incorporating the
fragmentation effects can be determined (refer to MCTP 3-10F). A lateral offset in the form of a
final attack heading or cone prevents premature crossing of the GTL.

Time Separation
Time separation, interrupted or nonstandard, may be required when aircraft cannot be routed away
from indirect-fire trajectories or SEAD targets. This technique requires the timing of SEAD fires
to be coordinated with the routing of aircraft so that even though aircraft and SEAD fires may
occupy the same space, they do not do so at the same time (see figure 9-7). For low-level CAS
attacks, time separation can be used when aircraft are attacking targets just short of indirect
fires along the GTL (see figure 9-8). All timing for SEAD fires is based on a specific aircraft
event time (e.g., CAS TOT/TTT, assault support L-hour, unmanned aircraft mission area arrival
time). In immediate SEAD fire missions, the aircraft event time becomes the zero hour or H-hour
for scheduling. The preferred method for coordinating timing is the use of a previously established
synchronized clock. If a synchronized clock has not been established or is not universally
available, an elapsed time may be used to coordinate timing. See appendix E for instructions on
establishing and communicating synchronized and elapsed times.

Separation Techniques
Table 9-2 summarizes the appropriate separation techniques for typical CAS scenarios.

             Table 9-2. Separation Techniques.

     Attack   CAS Target Same     CAS Target Distance                 CAS Crosses GTL
High level   as/Near SEAD target  From SEAD Target
Low level                                                          Altitude or time separation

AIRSPACE COORDINATING MEASURES

Fixed-Wing and Rotary-Wing Close Air Support
When coordinating SEAD fires or target marking for FW or RW CAS aircraft, terminal controllers,
spotters, and observers must be familiar with the use of contact points, IPs, HAs, and BPs. See
chapter 4 and MCRP 3-20F.7, Marine Air Traffic Control Detachment Handbook, for more
information on these ACMs.

Other Mission Support
Aircraft and unmanned aircraft conducting deep air support, air reconnaissance, assault support,
offensive antiair warfare, and electronic warfare missions, may use contact points and IPs.

When air reconnaissance and assault support aircraft prepare to begin a run on a reconnaissance
target or landing zone, initial, en route, and orbit points are used:

• IP. The IP is where the aircrew establishes communication with the ground control agency (i.e.,
   DASC or TACP) for coordinating instructions.

• En route point. Aircrews use en route points to define routes of flight to and from specific
   areas. En route points allow specific routing of aircraft for command and control, airspace
   limitation, and rules of engagement requirements.

• Orbit point. The orbit points represent geographic or electronic positions and are used to
   station aircraft while they await further routing instructions.

                                                                   9-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

TARGET MARKING FOR CLOSE AIR SUPPORT AIRCRAFT

A mark should be provided for CAS aircraft whenever possible. When one of the following
marking methods is not possible, the CAS target may be identified by a narrative description.

Laser Marking
The preferred method of marking a target is by laser if the CAS aircraft has a laser spot tracker.
The laser marking ensures the accurate engagement of the target by LGW and assists the CAS
aircrew in more accurate delivery of unguided ordnance. Laser marks require coordinating the
approach of the aircraft to correspond with the direction of the reflected laser energy.

Infrared Marking
Infrared pointers and other IR devices can be used by terminal controllers to mark targets at night
for pilots who are wearing night vision devices. Unlike laser designators, these IR devices cannot
be used to guide or improve the accuracy of aircraft ordnance. Infrared pointers should be used
with caution because they may expose the terminal controller to an enemy with night vision
capability. Infrared marks should be initiated 20 to 30 seconds before the CAS TOT/TTT or when
requested by the pilot and continue until the pilot transmits terminate or the weapon hits the target.

Indirect-Fire Marking
Artillery, NSFS, and mortar munitions—specifically WP or illumination fuzed to impact on the
deck—are excellent means of visually marking targets for CAS aircraft. Munition marking rounds
should be delivered as close to the CAS target as possible with—

• WP marks timed to impact 20 to 30 seconds before the CAS TOT/TTT.
• Illumination marks timed to impact 45 seconds before the CAS TOT/TTT.

These lead times ensure the munition marking round is in position early enough and remains visible
long enough for the terminal controller to provide final control instructions and for the pilot of the
lead aircraft to acquire the target. Based on the CAS aircraft’s TOT/TTT given in the SEAD call for
fire, the firing unit will determine the time to fire to ensure the marking round impacts as required.
Munition marking rounds delivered within 300 meters of the CAS target are generally considered
effective enough to direct CAS aircraft. If the munition marking round is not timely or accurate,
terminal controllers should be prepared to use a backup marking technique or rely on verbal
instructions to identify the target to CAS pilots. If the situation requires precise marks, observers
or spotters can adjust marking rounds early to ensure accurate marks, as well as SEAD fires, are
delivered to meet the CAS schedule. This may, however, alert the enemy to an imminent attack.

Direct-Fire Marking
Direct-fire weapons, such as the MK-19, can be used to deliver a mark. While this method may provide
more responsiveness than indirect-fire marks, its use may be limited by range and the visibility of the
burst in the battlespace. The MlA1 tank has no WP round and cannot be used for marking.

Other Aircraft Marking
Another aircraft, such as an F/A-18D or AH-lW/Z, can be used to deliver a marking round.

                                                                   9-13


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Backup Marking
When firing marks, backup marks should also be planned. For example, artillery may be tasked to
deliver the primary mark while a mortar or other aircraft may be assigned the responsibility of
being the backup mark. One consideration for planning backup marks is the difference between
the TOFs of the primary and backup weapons systems. If a mortar is tasked as backup for an
artillery mark, it cannot have a longer TOF than the artillery.

Other Means of Target Marking
Other means that may be used to identify a target include the following:

• The requesting unit may give a narrative description of the target in relation to their own position.
• The target may be marked by devices such as strobe lights, mirrors, or air panels.
• The target may be marked in relation to prominent landmarks.

ARTILLERY SUPPRESSION OF ENEMY AIR DEFENSES

For artillery SEAD support of air missions, the terminal controller (e.g., FAC) coordinates aircraft
routing, required suppression and marking, and timing with the artillery FO. The observer
determines the artillery firing requirements to protect aircraft from both enemy air defenses and
friendly fires and passes this information to the artillery FDC in the form of a call for fire. The FSCC
monitors these radio transmissions and ensures that the timing and effects of these SEAD fires are
coordinated with other operations being conducted by the unit.

Call for Fire
The artillery SEAD call for fire is based on the standard artillery call for fire format (see figure
9-12, figure 9-13, figure 9-4, and figure 9-15). Calls for fire may include instructions for
marking rounds if required.

Warning Order. The SEAD fires requests that may be identified in the warning order include
SEAD, SEAD polar, SEAD laser polar, and SEAD shift from a known point. The observer
identification and warning order constitute the first transmission of the call for fire.

                                            Example: Laser polar

               Suppression direction 6300, distance 2413, vertical angle plus 05, over.

Target Location. When determining target location, the most accurate method of target location
that is appropriate for the situation should be used. The target location element of the call for fire
must include the suppression target locations and, if required, the location for a munition marking
round. Target locations are specified by stating grid to suppress, followed by the location, and then
stating grid to mark, followed by the location. If no mark or suppression is desired, the requisite
portions are omitted. The target location constitutes the second transmission of the call for fire.

                                                                   9-14


---

                                  MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                          Examples: Target location

Grid: Grid to suppress 456 123, grid to mark 462 129, over.
Shift: Suppression direction 6300, right 180, add 600, up 45, over.
Suppression direction 6300, distance 2400, up 40, mark direction 6340,
distance 2500, up 45, over.

unmanned aircraft arrival time 1430, over.

Method of Engagement. The munition for SEAD suppression will be standardized and fired unless
another shell/fuze combination is requested. Depending on the terrain and maneuver
requirements, either DPICM or HE/VT will be used. Both are effective against—

• Personnel in the open (e.g., air defense gunners).
• Air defense fire control and radar devices on AAA.
• Surface-to-air missiles (SAMs).

Concerning size and probability of area coverage, DPICM is more effective. However, HE/VT
may be preferable in future maneuver areas and HE/delay may be preferable in forested areas.
WP/PD (i.e., fuzed to impact on the ground) is the standard munition for marking and will be
fired unless another shell or fuze combination is requested. White phosphorous is a good
general-purpose marking munition, because it provides a reliable, quick-building burst; is
distinguishable from HE bursting in the battlespace; and remains visible for several seconds.
Other shell or fuze combinations that may be effective in SEAD and marking CAS targets include
those discussed in the following subparagraphs.

Illumination. Illumination fuzed to burn on the deck is an effective mark. The following should be
taken into consideration when employing illumination during SEAD missions. Illumination––

• Can affect forward-looking infrared (FLIR) systems.
• Is generally too intense for aircrews operating with night vision devices.
• Can also be employed for suppression to confuse the acquisition systems of heat-seeking

   missiles, such as the SA-7/14, SA-9/13, and SA-16.

                                                                   9-20


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Can produce some confusion for radar guidance systems. Illumination flares in the air during
   an aircraft’s ordnance delivery can confuse antiair radar and IR-seeking missiles. The optimum
   HOB of the illumination flare for suppression is 300 to 400 meters above ground level in
   between CAS and the suspected ADA position.

Smoke. Smoke can be used to hinder optical acquisition of CAS aircraft by the enemy, but must be
positioned so that it will not hinder the pilot’s ability to acquire the target or marking round. The
use of smoke will interfere with optical acquisition systems of small arms, machine guns, and
most AAA and tactical SAMs.

Method of Fire and Control. The method of fire and control includes instructions for timing and
duration of SEAD fires.

For immediate SEAD fire missions, the aircraft event time (e.g., CAS TOT/TTT, assault support L-
hour, unmanned aircraft mission area arrival time) serves as the basis or zero hour to schedule
artillery fires. Fire and control instructions include the start and end times for suppression fires
and the aircraft event to determine time of fires.

The standard suppression times are expressed as either interrupted or continuous. Nonstandard
suppression includes plus or minus minutes from zero hour (e.g., minus two through plus one).
More than one interval of suppression fires may be specified if SEAD fires are nonstandard (e.g.,
minus two through minus one, plus one through plus two). Aircraft event times should be
expressed as a time on the synchronized clock (e.g., CAS TOT 1305). If a synchronized clock is not
available, aircraft event time is expressed as an elapsed time. For example, “CAS TTT 6 plus 0,
0. Stand by, hack,” or in relation to another event, “H-hour plus 10 minutes.” Refer to appendix E
for instructions on establishing and communicating synchronized and elapsed times.

Message-to-Observer
Once the call for fire has been received by the artillery battery, the battery FDC transmits the MTO.
The MTO includes the following unless the observer requests otherwise:

• Unit to fire.
• Number and type of rounds in suppression.
• Number and type of rounds for mark.
• Target number.
• Fire request changes.

NAVAL SURFACE FIRE SUPPORT SUPPRESSION OF ENEMY AIR DEFENSES

For NSFS SEAD, the terminal controller (e.g., FAC/JTAC) coordinates aircraft routing, required
suppression and marking, and timing with the NSFS spotter. The spotter determines the NSFS
requirements to protect aircraft from both enemy air defenses and friendly fires and passes this
information to the NSFS ship in the form of a call for fire.

                                                                   9-21


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

The NSFS SEAD call for fire is based on the standard call for fire format (see figure 9-16 and figure
9-17). The spotter tailors it for each specific mission. Calls for fire may include instructions for
marking rounds if required. The FSCC monitors these radio transmissions and ensures that the
timing and effects of the SEAD fires are coordinated with other operations of the unit.

Call for Fire
Warning Order and Target Number. The SEAD fire requests are identified in the warning order
(e.g., SEAD fire mission). The spotter identification, warning order, and target number constitute
the first transmission of the call for fire (see figure 9-18).

                              Example: Warning order and target number

               T4R this is E3P, SEAD fire mission, target number NZ 3101, over.

Target Location. When determining target location, the most accurate method of target location
that is appropriate for the situation should be used. The target location element of the call for fire
must include the suppression target locations and, if required, the location for a munition marking
round. In the call for fire, all suppression target information, which includes target location, target
description, method of engagement, and method of control, should be stated before information is
given on a second suppression target or the mark target. Target locations are specified by stating
suppression, followed by the location, and mark, followed by the location, or negative mark if no
mark is desired.

                                 Example: Immediate SEAD decision 5
               Mission: CH-46 assault support inserted away from GTL.
               Targets: One suspected ZPU-4 along flight route; 1 suspected AAA
               site 800 meters from landing zone; location by grid method.
               System/unit: NSFS/USS O’Bannon DD-987 (V4G).
               Mark: None.
               Ordnance: HE/CVT. This is standard.
               Separation method: Lateral.
               Duration/volume: First target: 3 minutes before to 2 minutes before
               L-hour; second target: 1 minute before to 1 minute after L-hour.
               Timing: L-hour at 1510 on synchronized clock.

                                                                   9-22

                      Example: Immediate SEAD call for fire 7

    Spotter: Y2P this is C5E, SEAD fire mission, target number AF 0018, over.
    Ship: Reads back.
    Spotter: Suppression grid ND 516 368, altitude 478, suspected AAA site,
    continuous, mark grid ND 456 359, altitude 500, CAS TOT 1030,
    continuous, over.
    Ship: Reads back.
    Spotter: Suppression GTL 1600, summit 800 feet, ready 18, over.
    Ship: Reads back.
    Spotter: Mark GTL 1700, summit 900 feet, ready 19, over.
    Ship: Reads back.

Stay above
2000 feet MSL

                   Mark  Dash 1       Dash 2
                         Strike       Strike

                         Suppression

      Figure 9-18. Example of Suppression of Enemy Air Defenses
    Call for Fire Using Altitude Separation and Synchronized Clock.

                         9-26


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Target Description. Target description is provided as in the standard call for fire. Enemy air
defense positions should be identified as known or suspected to assist in developing enemy
intelligence information.

Method of Engagement. The standard method of engagement for SEAD suppression is HE/CVT, 1
gun, sustained fire, interval 10 seconds for suppression.

For NSFS marks, the standard is WP/PD fired to impact at 20 to 30 seconds before the aircraft
event time.

These standards will be fired unless another method of engagement is specifically requested. The
effects of NSFS HE/CVT are similar to those of artillery HE/VT. Because some ships may not
carry WP, illumination may need to be used as a mark. Illumination marks should be timed to
burst 45 seconds before the aircraft event time and fuzed to burn on the deck. The effects of NSFS
illumination are similar to those of artillery illumination.

Each method of engagement includes instructions for timing and duration of SEAD fires.
Engagement instructions include the starting and ending times for suppression fires relative to the
aircraft event time. The aircraft event time is stated in the method of control element of the call for
fire. Suppression times are expressed as plus or minus minutes from zero hour (e.g, minus two
through plus one). More than one sequence of suppression fires may be specified if fires on that
target must be broken up (e.g., minus two through minus one, plus one through plus two). To
abbreviate the SEAD call for fire to support CAS missions, two standard programs are established
for typical SEAD scenarios.

Continuous. The continuous SEAD program may be used when separating aircraft and SEAD fires
by distance. This program is usually effective when the SEAD target is in the vicinity of the CAS
target and SEAD fires will not endanger CAS aircraft. Continuous SEAD fires are delivered
during the critical portion of the CAS aircraft’s attack phase and egress; specifically, from one
minute before the CAS TOT/TTT until 1 minute after the CAS TOT/TTT. This program is
requested by stating continuous instead of desired suppression times.

Interrupted. The interrupted SEAD program may be used when separating aircraft and SEAD fires by
time. This program is usually effective against SEAD targets that are the same as or close to the CAS
target and where SEAD effects or trajectories may endanger CAS aircraft. Interrupted SEAD fires
are delivered during the critical portion of the CAS aircraft’s attack phase; specifically, from 1
minute before the CAS TOT/TTT until 30 seconds before the CAS TOT/TTT. This program is
requested by stating interrupted instead of the desired suppression times.

Method of Control. For immediate NSFS SEAD missions, control instructions must include the
aircraft event time (e.g., CAS TOT/TTT, assault support L-hour, unmanned aircraft mission
area arrival time), which serves as the basis or zero hour from which NSFS is scheduled.
Aircraft event times should be expressed as a time on the synchronized clock (e.g., CAS TOT
1305). If a synchronized clock is not available, express aircraft event time as an elapsed time;
for example, CAS TTT 6 plus 0, 0 . . . stand by, hack, or in relation to another event, H-hour plus
10 minutes.

                                                                   9-27


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

The target location, target description, method of engagement, and method of control constitute
the second transmission of the call for fire. Once instructions for a target have begun in this
transmission, spotters should complete the location, description, and method of engagement for
that target before providing instructions for another suppression target or a mark target.
For example, stating suppression for the first target location, description, and method of
engagement; and mark for the location and method of engagement. Because of the length of this
transmission, information should be transmitted as it is determined, and elapsed times (i.e., TTT
and hacks) should be sent as a third transmission.

                                            Example: Suppression
               Suppression grid 765 567, altitude 80, suspected SA-6, nonstandard,
               minus 2 through plus 2, mark grid 762 575, altitude 50, CAS TOT
               1305, over.
Prefiring Report
The NSFS ship will transmit a normal prefiring report that includes the GTL, summit, and TOF. In
addition, shot and splash should be transmitted to assure spotters that fires will impact as desired.

                                                                   9-28


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                     CHAPTER 10
               AERIAL OBSERVATION

This chapter concerning aerial observation will detail the unique aerial observer procedures for
calling for and adjusting artillery and NSFS.

AERIAL OBSERVER

Aerial observers—

• Are trained to observe the battlespace from aircraft in order to conduct reconnaissance and
   adjust artillery and NSFS.

• May also perform the duties of a FAC(A) or TAC(A).
• Are also referred to as air spotters when adjusting NSFS.
• Use procedures and terminology for adjusting fires similar to those employed by the ground

   observers or spotters. Some variations are imposed by the observers’ perspective from the
   aircraft. Marine aerial observers operate primarily from the F/A-18D FW aircraft and the
   AH-1W/Z or UH-1N/Y helicopter.

PRELAUNCH CONSIDERATIONS

The aerial observer and pilot are given a thorough briefing that addresses the current friendly
situation, including infantry objectives, locations, front lines, zones of action, patrol plans, and
coordinating measures. In addition, the following information should be also obtained during
mission preparation:

• Enemy locations, organization, and equipment.
• Battery positions.
• Known points.
• Targets/suspected target areas.
• Reference lines available for making corrections.
• Areas to be searched.
• Ordnance available.
• Maps and photographs of obstacles and checkpoints.
• Communications details (e.g., frequencies, call signs, prearranged signals, authentication sheets).

                                                                   10-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Established operational procedures or SOPs, such as the method of fire for immediate
   suppression, special munitions, and SEAD.

• Fire support areas/fire support stations.
• NSFS ship disposition, availability, and radar beacon positions.
• Specific mission preparation, which includes time of takeoff and return, routes, mission, check-

   in times, control measures, known fires and air strikes, restrictive fire plans, weather, and relief
   on station.
• Message drop-off and pickup stations.
• Panel code.
• Location of ground radios.
• Pulse repetition frequency (PRF) code words.

REPORT IN AND OUT

The pilot reports in and out to air control agencies when airborne or over an assigned orbit point
by using the UHF radio. The aerial observer reports in and out with ground agencies by using
either the UHF or VHF radio.

Communication Nets
In addition to the division artillery air spot net or the NSFS air spot net (see chapter l), the
following nets and frequencies may be used.

Ship Air Spot Reporting In and Out Net. When arriving on station, the aerial observer must check in
with the SACC or the appropriate FFCC or FSCC. For NSFS, the ship air spot reporting in and out
net (UHF) is employed for the following:

• To assign air spotters to specific gunfire support ships and shore fire control parties, as
   directed.

• To direct NSFS air spotters to guard other air spot frequencies, if required.
• To brief spotters on the location of targets, friendly front lines, and zones of action, as directed.
• To release air spot planes when relieved and return them to the tactical air traffic control net

   controller or senior air observer for departure from the area.

The stations on this net include parent carriers, land bases, tactical air commanders, the
commander of the landing force, and all airborne NSFS spotting aircraft that report in and out.

Marine Air-Ground Task Force Air Observation Net. This UHF/VHF net provides a means for—

• Controlling air observation.
• Transmitting information from landing force aerial observers to landing force headquarters and

   other landing force units.
• Adjusting artillery or NSFS on an emergency basis.

                                                                   10-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Multiple air observation nets may be required, depending on the scope of the operation. The
stations on this net include the SACC, the FFCC, the FSCCs, and the senior artillery FDC.

Marine Air-Ground Task Force Artillery Air Spot Net. This VHF/UHF net provides the landing force
headquarters, FFCC or senior FSCC, artillery regiment, artillery battalions, and fire support
officers with an air spot net for conducting fire on deep and difficult targets. The stations on this
net include landing force aerial observers, the force command element, ground combat element
artillery aerial observers, and landing force artillery units, as required.

Division Air Observation Net. This VHF/UHF net provides a means for controlling air observation,
for the transmission of information from division aerial observers to division headquarters and
other division units, and for adjusting artillery or NSFS on an emergency basis. Stations on this
net include the division headquarters, aerial observers, and subordinate division units, as required.

Tactical Air Traffic Control Net. This UHF/VHF net provides a means for the Navy TACC/tactical
air direction center, tactical air operations center, and DASC to exercise airspace control of all
tactical aircraft and itinerant aircraft in the objective area. The stations on this net include the
Navy TACC/tactical air direction center, the tactical air operations centers, the DASC, FW
aircraft, and helicopters.

Tactical Air Request/Helicopter Request Net. This HF/VHF net provides a means for requesting
immediate air support from the DASC. The stations on this net include the DASC, TACPs, FFCC/
FSCCs, the helicopter direction center, the FAC(A), the TAC(A), and the assault support coordinator
(airborne), as required. In amphibious operations, a dedicated net (i.e., helicopter request [HF/
VHF]) may be activated during ship-to-shore movement for immediate helicopter requests.

Tactical Air Direction Net. This UHF/VHF net provides a means to direct aircraft in the conduct
of CAS missions, for the DASC to brief support aircraft on target information, or for the DASC to
assign missions to aircraft.

There are multiple tactical air direction nets available for the DASC to assign to terminal control
agencies (e.g., TACPs, FAC[A]s). The tactical air direction net is not a planning net and should
not be used for extraneous traffic.

Available Radios
Table 10-1 displays the radios that are available for communication by aerial observers.

                                                                   10-3


---

                                          MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                         Table 10-1. Radios Available to the Aerial Observer.

   Aircraft                                                      Communications Means
F/A-18D      Two ARC-182 transmitters/receivers with the capability to monitor any 2 frequencies in UHF/VHF/AM/FM

AH-1W/Z      Capable of operating when covered
             Two ARC-182 transmitters/receivers with the capability to monitor any 2 frequencies in UHF/VHF/AM/FM

UH-1N/Y      Capable of operating when covered
