LEGEND:

AM amplitude modulation

CALL FOR FIRE

The call for fire is used by the aerial observer for both artillery and NSFS in the same manner as
discussed in chapters 3 and 4. The following are special considerations for the aerial observer who
is making a call for fire.

Target Marking
The aerial observer may mark the target for a firing ship and use the ship adjust method of control.
This technique should be used only in a low-threat environment.

Method of Control
The command at my command allows the pilot time to position the aircraft for observation.

Target Location
Target location is determined by either the grid or shift from a known point method. Although the
methods are the same as discussed in chapter 2, obtaining an accurate target location is difficult
because targets are normally acquired by the naked eye. The use of gyro-stabilized, low-light
capable binoculars (i.e., the Fujinon Stabiscope S-40) facilitates accurate target location and
adjustment of fire and increases aircraft survivability. Non-gyro-stabilized binoculars are difficult
to use because of the distortion that is caused by the windscreen and vibration of the aircraft.

Hand measurements or estimations can be used to determine angular deviation. The aerial
observer should seek an object to use as a yardstick for assistance in estimation. For example, a
bridge span that is measured on the map to be 200 meters can be used as a yardstick in target
location and adjustment of fire. The aerial observer’s capability to determine an accurate target
location may be limited by the enemy situation. Because of the enemy acquisition capability, the
aerial observer may be afforded only a short exposure time. The aerial observer can offset the
enemy’s acquisition capability by using an FLIR receiver.

                                          10-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Direction
The aerial observer selects one of the following types of direction based on the battlespace
environment, terrain, type of aircraft, experience, and training:

• Gun-target line. The GTL provides an effective type of direction for the adjustment of fire. In
   artillery, if the aerial observer has knowledge of the firing unit’s location, the GTL can be used
   for adjustment. The FDC will assume that the GTL is being used unless otherwise specified by
   the observer. In NSFS, if the aerial observer can see both the ship and the target or can clearly
   visualize the GTL on the ground, GTL direction can be used. The aerial observer must specify
   direction GTL if this type of direction is used.

• Arbitrary reference line. The aerial observer—
    Can use visible terrain or a man-made feature as a reference line and use this line or a
        corresponding parallel line and project it through the target.
    Must be able to see both the target and the reference line feature simultaneously from all
        angles of approach. Roads, rivers, power lines, and railroads are examples of good
        reference lines.

• Observer-target line. The OT direction is an excellent reference for an aerial observer who is
   observing from a helicopter. The bearing-distance heading indicator on the aircraft instrument
   panel can be used to determine OT direction in degrees magnetic. The aerial observer may need
   to transmit a new OT direction for subsequent adjustments. Aerial observers have a difficult
   time using the OT direction while performing the observation from an FW aircraft. The FW
   aircraft will not normally head directly for the target on every pass; therefore, the aerial
   observer may have to estimate OT direction from the sides of the aircraft. The clock method
   using the nose of the aircraft can facilitate this estimation.

• Cardinal direction. Cardinal direction, although less accurate, can be used for direction.

Summit or Maximum Ordinate
The aerial observer may wish to know the summit or maximum ordinate for use in aircraft
positioning and flight path selection. In artillery, the aerial observer may request the maximum
ordinate from the firing unit. In NSFS, the summit is automatically reported in the prefiring
report (see chapter 4).

Time of Flight
The aerial observer automatically receives TOF in the artillery MTO or NSFS prefiring report.
This allows the pilot time to position the aircraft for observation.

ADJUSTMENT OF FIRE

Adjustment of fire involves spottings, corrections, and adjustment techniques from a helicopter:

• Deviation and range spottings. The aerial observer determines spottings by map spot,
   estimation, or hand measurement; depending on the aircraft positioning, the threat, and the type
   of aircraft.

                                                                   10-5


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• HOB spottings. The aerial observer cannot readily determine HOB; consequently, adjustments
   to HOB for HE with fuze time are difficult. The aerial observer must rely on the fragmentation
   pattern on the ground as a means for determining spots. The use of VT or CVT eliminates this
   problem.

• Corrections. Because of the aerial observer’s elevated view, the one-round adjustment method
   is preferred; however, the ground observer may use other methods as discussed (see chapter 5).

• Helicopter adjustment techniques. When the aerial observer is adjusting from a helicopter,
   either the stationary hover or pop-up technique may be used for adjustment:

• Stationary hover technique. In a stationary hover, the pilot positions the aircraft behind trees or
   other terrain features that conceal the aircraft and still permit observation of the target.

• Pop-up technique. In the pop-up technique, the pilot will unmask the aircraft 2 to 3 seconds
   before impact of the round. After the aerial observer observes the burst, the aircraft returns to
   the original hide position or moves to another hide position. The aerial observer sends
   corrections as the pilot remasks the aircraft.

FORWARD-LOOKING INFRARED RECEIVER

The FLIR receiver is a passive device used to detect targets by their IR radiation. The FLIR
receiver views the terrain in the vicinity of the aircraft. Infrared energy is converted into
electrical signals and displayed on a terminal in the cockpits of the F/A-18, AV-8B (night attack
version), AH-1W, AH-64A, AC-130, F-15E, F-16, and OH-58D aircraft. The FLIR—
• Allows for the recognition, identification, and classification of targets that would otherwise go

   undetected as a result of darkness or camouflage.
• Provides the aircrew with an excellent target during either day or night, although usually better

   at night.
The FLIR receiver, in conjunction with the laser, makes night adjustment of artillery, mortars,
or NSFS possible without illumination and the inherent time delays associated with illumination
missions. The call for fire using the FLIR receiver is the same as discussed in chapters 3 and 4.

                                                                   10-6


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                     CHAPTER 11
      LASERS, LASER DESIGNATORS, AND
      LASER-GUIDED WEAPONS SYSTEMS

Laser-guided weapons systems consist of ground and/or airborne designators used with surface-
or air-delivered laser-guided munitions. These systems include laser designators or range finders,
laser acquisition devices or seekers, laser-guided munitions, and delivery platforms. Laser-guided
weapons systems are used by artillery observers, NSFS spotters, reconnaissance personnel, FACs,
aerial observers, and aircraft pilots. This chapter focuses on the LGW systems employed by the
ground observer, spotter, or terminal controller in support of the MAGTF. See MCRP 3-31.6 for
the most current inventory of aircraft, ordnance, munitions and LGW systems.

PURPOSE OF LASER-GUIDED WEAPONS SYSTEMS

Laser-guided weapons systems—

• Provide combat units with a means of accurately locating and engaging high-priority stationary
   and moving point targets with first-round hit probability.

• Allow for faster location and attack of the target.
• Provide artillery, ships, and aircraft with accurate target data to attack effectively with

   conventional munitions.
• Provide positive control between the air and the ground with the coded laser beam.

BASIC REQUIREMENTS FOR USING LASER DESIGNATORS

The five basic requirements for using laser designators with laser acquisition devices or laser-
guided munitions are as follows:

• Line of sight. A line of sight must exist between the designator and the target and between the
   target and the laser acquisition device.

• PRF codes. A PRF code is used for the laser designator, the laser seeker, and the laser-guided
   munition. Each must use the same code when operating together.

• Direction of attack. An agreement on a direction of attack is necessary. The laser seeker or
   laser-guided munition must be able to sense the reflected energy from the laser designator.

                                                                   11-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Target designation. The laser designator must be designating the target at the correct time.
• Munition delivery envelope. The delivery system must release the munition within the specific

   munition delivery envelope.

CAPABILITIES OF LASER EMISSION SYSTEMS

Laser target ranging and designation systems provide accurate range, azimuth, and elevation
information for use in locating enemy targets or other positions. These systems vary from hand-
held range finders and pointers to aircraft-mounted designators. Laser designators provide the
energy source that is reflected from a designated target to provide terminal guidance for laser-
guided munitions. These systems emit discreet pulses of IR energy that are invisible to the naked
eye. The characteristics of these pulses are determined by a PRF code of laser energy, which can
be set by a series of switches on the equipment. The following are characteristics of common laser
emission systems:

• Vector 21-B. A hand-held, battery-operated, eye-safe CLRF, the Vector 21-B is an updated
   version of the PTS-180 VIPER that includes a DAGR GPS and an AN/PVS-14 night vision
   device that—
    Can provide range, azimuth, and vertical angle to a target.
    Can measure horizontal and vertical shift from two objects in addition to angular shift.
    Cannot designate targets for laser-guided munitions.
    Has a 7-power binocular sighting system and an included 10-power binocular enhancer that
        attaches to the system.
    Can provide approximately 3,000 range readouts per 6-volt lithium battery.
    Is capable of detecting multiple target reflections. The operator can apply a minimum range
        setting and a declination constant setting.
    Has multiple units of measure for azimuth (i.e., degrees, mils, gons) and range (i.e., feet,
        yards, meters).

             Note: Accuracy for the Vector 21-B is +/- 5 meters for distances of 5 to
             12,048 meters. Accuracy for azimuth measurements is +/- 10 mils for
             deviation and +/- 3 mils for inclination.

• Laser target designator/ranger (LTD/R) pod. The F/A-18C/D is capable of carrying the AN/
   AAS-38 LTD/R pod, which is effective during either the day or night and at high altitudes and
   provides the following functions:
    Terminal weapons guidance for all laser-guided munitions.
    Designation of targets for airborne coded laser acquisition/spot trackers.
    Night targeting system. The AH-1W/Z attack helicopter contains the night targeting
        system, which provides LTD/R capability, as well as FLIR visual capability and video
        capture. It performs the same functions as the LTD/R pod does for FW platforms.

                                                                   11-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Illumination devices. Hand-held lasers emit IR light for compatibility with night vision goggles
   (NVGs). They can either flood IR light within 1,000 meters to enhance general NVG
   performance or focus a small beam up to 4 kilometers to pinpoint a specific target with an IR
   light beam for any weapons system employing NVGs. Hand-held lasers are not capable of
   designating for a laser-guided munition.

LASER ACQUISITION DEVICES

Laser acquisition devices (e.g., seekers, spot trackers)—

• Are used to acquire reflected laser energy from laser-designated targets.
• Are installed in aircraft for target acquisition or in laser-guided munitions for terminal guidance.
• Allow visual acquisition of a coded laser-designated target.
• Must be set to the same PRF code as the laser designator for the user to see the target being

   lased. The AH-1W/Z has the capability to use on-board helicopterborne fire and forget
   missile (HELLFIRE) munitions as an improvised laser spot tracker before firing.

See appendix H for all laser spot tracker-equipped aircraft. There are two common laser
acquisition devices:

• Laser acquisition/spot tracker pod. The coded laser acquisition/spot tracker can be carried on
   the F/A-18A/C/D. Once it acquires the laser spot (i.e., target), it passes necessary ballistic
   information to allow FLIR or radar acquisition of the target and visual display. Desired PRF
   codes are in-flight selectable. It then employs LGWs or executes visual deliveries of nonlaser
   ordnance.

• Angle rate bombing system. The angle rate bombing system is used on the AV-8B. It consists of
   a 3-axis gimbaled television/laser spot tracker, which enables a view of the laser spot. The
   angle rate bombing system provides day or night attack and reattack information for either LGWs
   or nonguided bombs and allows in-flight selection of PRF codes, though it is affected by smoke
   or obscurants.

LASER-GUIDED MUNITIONS

These munitions concentrate on reflected laser energy during the terminal portion of the attack.
Such munitions are part of the precision-guided munitions family, which includes Maverick and
HELLFIRE missiles and laser-guided bombs.

• Maverick. An air-launched, air-to-ground missile that is available in a laser-guided variant and
   an IR-seeker variant. It can be employed on the AV-8B, A-10, F-15, F-16, and F/A-18 aircraft
   and is used against field fortifications, SAM sites, and armored vehicles. This missile requires

                                                                   11-3


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

   lock-on before launch and continual designation during flight. Once the Maverick is launched,
   the aircraft can break away or launch another missile. The PRF code is cockpit selectable.
• HELLFIRE. An air-launched, laser-guided, antiarmor missile launched from the AH-lW/Z,
   AH-64, and OH-58D aircraft. The HELLFIRE can be employed in indirect (i.e, lock-on after
   launch) or direct (i.e., lock-on before launch) fire methods and launched in four firing modes:
    Single. One missile.
    Rapid. Two or more missiles launched on the same code.
    Ripple. Two or more missiles launched on different codes using multiple laser designators.
    Multiple. Codes and designators used in a combination of rapid and ripple fire. The PRF

        code is cockpit selectable.
• Laser-guided bombs. The MK-82, MK-83, and MK-84 are designations for the 500-lb, 1,000-lb,

   and 2,000-lb bombs that can be converted to the GBU-12, GBU-16, GBU-10, GBU-22, GBU-23,
   and GBU-24 laser-guided bombs. These bombs use common laser guidance and control
   subassemblies with only the aerodynamic surfaces changed to match the particular size of the
   warhead. They can be employed in a standoff capacity. The PRF codes must be set before
   aircraft launch.

LASER ENERGY REFLECTIVITY

Laser energy is reflected in various ways that depend on the nature of the reflecting surface, the
angle at which the laser energy strikes the target surface, and the laser beam divergence. This
information is important in determining where the laser acquisition, tracking device, or seeker
needs to be to acquired and concentrated on the reflected energy spot.

Laser Spot Size
The laser energy or beam increases in size at a constant rate from its source. This spreading is a
function of the designator itself and the distance to the target being designated. This is called beam
divergence. For the CLRF, the laser energy spot size can be estimated for any distance by using
the following rule of thumb––the spot size is equal to 3 inches plus 8 times the OT distance,
presented in thousands of meters.

Target Surface Reflectivity
Targets contain a combination of mirror-like (e.g., windshields, windows, shiny metal) and low-
reflectivity surfaces. Mirror-like surfaces should not generally be designated because the
reflective energy does not spread sufficiently to allow the seeker to acquire it. Certain materials
are better reflectors of laser energy than others (e.g., brick, dirty metal). Targets with greater laser
energy reflectivity increase the probability of a laser seeker picking up the laser spot.

Attack headings for air-delivered munitions should be outside the safety zone, which is an area
10 degrees (i.e., 180 mils) on either side of the laser designator. Laser-guided weapons that are
launched within the 20-degree (i.e., 360 mils) safety zone could receive false target indications
and acquire the designator. The safest acquisition area for an LGW is more than 10 degrees
(i.e., 180 mils) but less than 60 degrees (i.e., 1100 mils) off the laser-target line. This provides

                                                                   11-4


---

             MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

a 50-degree (i.e., 900 mil) cone to either side of the laser-target line for an ideal attack heading
(see figure 11-1). Terminal controllers employing laser designators must provide air crews
with an attack heading that is within that cone.

    Best                          Target      Attack
Acquisition                                  Heading
Area (50 )                          Laser
                                 Designator       Best
                                             Acquisition
                       Safety                 Area (50 )
                       Zone
                        (20 )

              Attack
             Position

                                             Offset

                                              Run-in
                                             Heading

                       IP

                                                                    Best IP Area
                                  Figure 11-1. Safety Zone and Optimal Acquisition Area

                                             for Delivery of Laser-Guided Weapons.

The best results are obtained when designated targets reflect the beam upward and toward the
incoming seeker or munition. The amount of laser energy that is reflected from a target is
difficult to determine and is material dependent (see table 11-1).

                       11-5


---

            MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

          Table 11-1. Energy Reflection.

         Material               Percentage of
                                 Reflectivity

Brick                     55–90

Vegetation                30–70

Unpolished aluminum       55

Olive drab metal (dirty)  2–30

Asphalt                   10–25

Concrete                  10–15

Water                     2

Energy Reflection on Various Surfaces
A brief description of how the energy is reflected from various types of surfaces follows:

• Mirror-like reflections. The laser energy reflects in a narrow path from flat, bare, shiny metal,
   and glass surfaces as shown in figure 11-2. Any seeker looking for this laser energy must be in
   this narrow area of reflection to acquire the spot. These surfaces are not suitable for laser
   designation.

            Angular Reflection

                 Perpendicular Reflection
       Figure 11-2. Mirror-Like Reflections.

                          11-6


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Scattered reflections. Laser energy scatters in a large spherical arc from flat, low-reflectivity
   surfaces (see figure 11-3). These surfaces are suitable for laser designation.

                                                Figure 11-3. Scattered Reflections.

• Spillover. Laser energy spillover occurs when the laser spot is larger than the target. It is
   usually caused by beam divergence but may also be caused by a careless designator operator.
   Reflections from objects near the target may also occur (see figure 11-4). When this happens,
   the seeker may acquire the wrong spot.

                                                     Figure 11-4. Laser Spillover.

• Blocked. If the seeker is not in the proper position, laser energy reflections from the target can
   be blocked from the seeker by intervening objects or by the target itself (see figure 11-5).

                                                  Figure 11-5. Blocked Reflection.

• Absorbed. Aiming the designator into tunnels and other targets causes laser energy to be
   absorbed. To avoid this, the operator must aim the designator slightly above a tunnel opening.
   This will allow a munition to impact at that critical point. For munitions like laser-guided
   bombs that tend to impact short of the point being designated, this type of designation could
   guide the bomb into the tunnel opening (see figure 11-6).

                                                                   11-7


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                                                Proper Designation
                                                                              No Reflection

                                Figure 11-6. Absorbed Laser Energy on Concave Targets.

LASER-GUIDED MUNITIONS PLANNING

Laser Designator Planning Factors
Based on the commander’s guidance, the observer must consider the following factors when
employing a laser designator:

• Environmental conditions.
• Designator location relative to the target and delivery system.
• Laser designator aiming points.
• Communications.
• Mobility.

Mission Execution Employment Factors
In addition to the five basic requirements, the observer must also consider the following key
factors when employing laser-guided munitions:

• Enemy countermeasures. Judicious use of laser target designators will limit the enemy’s
   countermeasures capability.

• Battlespace obscuration. The effects of smoke, dust, and debris can impair the use of laser-
   guided munitions. The reflective scattering of laser light by smoke particles may present false
   targets. The operator can use a night sight, alternate positions on higher ground, and alternate
   designators to reduce smoke effects.

• Terrain and vegetation. Irregular terrain and vegetation will affect line of sight.
• Weather factors. Types of weather factors are as follows:

    Extreme temperatures (e.g., below 32 degrees) can affect the laser designator battery life
        and may require the use of an external power adapter. Placing the battery close to the body
        under heavy clothing will help to preserve its life.

    Rain, snow, fog, and low clouds can prevent the effective use of laser-guided munitions.
        Heavy precipitation can limit the use of laser designators by affecting line of sight.

                                                                   11-8


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

    Snow on the ground can produce a negative effect on the accuracy of laser-guided
        munitions.

    Fog and low clouds will block the laser-guided munition seeker’s field of view, reducing
        the guidance time and hit probability.

• Darkness. Targets are more difficult to locate, range, and designate during periods of poor
   visibility.

• Target shape. Poorly defined targets, such as caves and tunnels, may absorb the laser spots.
• Seeker and laser designator alignment. The direction of attack and/or GTL must be within the

   allowable mission attack angle. Laser-guided or air-launched munitions should attack the target
   within 60 degrees (i.e., 1,100 mils) to either side of the designator target azimuth. Some
   munitions may require a smaller angle. The closer the weapon approach angle is to the
   designator target azimuth, provided it is at least 10 degrees (i.e., 180 mils), the greater the
   probability of target acquisition and hit.
• Communications. Positive communications between the terminal controller and observer and
   the delivery aircraft and unit are required to coordinate the proper PRF code, seeker or laser
   designator alignment, and target designation timing.
• Safety. The invisible laser beam is intense IR radiation, which can cause serious eye damage
   and blindness. During peacetime, the use of laser-guided munitions imposes strict safety
   requirements during training exercises. Operators must adhere to range and unit safety SOPs
   when employing laser-guided munitions. During combat, the operator must attempt to avoid
   friendly casualties that may be caused by indiscriminate laser designation. Caution should also
   be taken when designating reflective surfaces, which can cause dangerous reflected beams. The
   use of binoculars and similar optical devices during airborne or ground lasing greatly increases
   the possibility of eye damage. Ground troops located in the target area should use safety
   goggles when working with aircraft that can laser designate targets. The terminal controllers
   must be aware of where friendly front lines are located when planning the attack heading for
   aircraft ordnance delivery.

Seeker Lock-on to Designator
Laser seekers may occasionally lock on to the designator instead of the target. For example,
intervening grass or leaves could reflect sufficient laser energy to cause seeker lock-on. To
prevent seeker lock-on to the designator position, the designator should be masked from the
seeker’s field of view by terrain, vegetation, or a temporary screen, such as blankets or tarps.
When the seeker’s progress indicates a seeker lock-on to the designator, it may be possible to
prevent a mishap by aborting the aircraft’s bombing run or by turning off the designator.

Pulse Repetition Frequency Codes
Laser coding permits the simultaneous use of multiple laser designators and laser-guided seekers
or weapons. Laser designators and seekers use a PRF coding system to ensure that a specific
seeker and designator combination works in harmony. By setting the same code in both the
designator and the seeker, the seeker will track only the target that is designated with that code.

Code Description. Dependent on the type of laser equipment, the system uses either a three- or
four-digit PRF code. Three-digit settings range from 111 to 788, and four-digit settings range
from 1111 to 1788. All three- and four-digit designators or seekers are compatible. When

                                                                   11-9


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

employing three- and four-digit designators or seekers, the first digit of the four-digit code must
always be set to one. The remaining digits of the two systems must match. For example, a four-
digit code of 1657 would be compatible with a three-digit code of 657.

Code Allocation and Assignment. Laser-guided weapons system codes are controlled and
coordinated. At the MAGTF level, different blocks of codes are assigned to artillery, air, and NSFS
to prevent interference between supporting arms activities. Each supporting arm then assigns
codes to its subordinate units for individual missions and changes codes periodically as required
by the situation. Subordinate FSCCs provide positive coordination of the code settings through the
various fire support representatives. For individual missions, the munition delivery system (i.e.,
artillery, ships, and aircraft) employs the codes. For CAS, codes are ultimately assigned to each
flight so that no two flights are in the air at the same time with the same code. When an aircraft is
on station, the pilot passes the code to the terminal controller. The terminal controller then
coordinates with the laser designator operator to ensure that the designator is set to the same code
as the aircraft. Individual aircraft may carry LGWs with different preset codes to accommodate
multiple aircraft attacks or multiple weapon releases or to allow variation in codes used on
consecutive attacks.

Security. The PRF codes are handled in the same manner as other classified material. Secure
means should be used, if available, when the codes are passed between laser designators and the
munition delivery unit or aircraft. However, the absence of compatible security means should not
normally dictate the termination of a laser-guided munition attack. In certain situations, codes will
be prebriefed.

Effect on Mission
Laser-guided munitions can be employed against high-priority targets early in the battle before
smoke and dust obscure the battlespace. Targets downwind can be attacked first if priorities
permit. Use of preplanned aiming points and laser-guided munition footprints can help to
overcome effects of obscuration, weather, vegetation, and terrain. Use of a night sight on the
designator will help to overcome the effects of darkness and can assist during periods of poor
visibility and inclement weather. The adverse effects of the mission execution and employment
factors can often be overcome by skillful employment of the designators. Enemy countermeasures
can be minimized by aiming the laser designator at an object near the target instead of directly on
target (i.e., offset designation).

METHODS OF EMPLOYMENT

Laser-guided munitions are employed in the ground and airborne modes.

Ground Mode
Ground laser designators identify targets for artillery, NSFS, and aircraft-delivered munitions.
Standard calls for fire are used, but the laser code must be exchanged between the ground
designator and the firing unit or aircraft.

                                                                  11-10


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Airborne Mode
Airborne laser designators identify targets for artillery, NSFS, and aircraft-delivered munitions.
Airborne designator systems operating in support of ground maneuver forces have the ability to
employ all types of laser-guided munitions. Standard calls for fire or requests for air support are
used. The code being used must be exchanged.

EMPLOYMENT CONSIDERATIONS

Operator employment considerations include the designator aiming point, spot tracking
techniques, and designator operator positioning.

Designator Aiming Point Considerations
The range, size, shape, reflectivity characteristics, location, and motion of a target all have an
influence on the technique of employing a laser designator. When lasing a target by using a night
sight device, the operator must be trained to use the device properly. For example, the operator
should know that the IR signature of a target will be at the highest concentration of heat. In the
case of a moving vehicle, this heat concentration will be slightly behind the designated lasing
point of the target.

Typically, a flat surface is the most favorable surface for designation because it is perpendicular to
the direction of the incoming ordnance. If there is not a flat surface, then a curved or irregular
surface facing the direction of the incoming ordnance should be a satisfactory aiming point.

Spot Tracking Techniques
The distance from the laser to the target determines the size of the laser spot. The following
techniques should be used to optimize spot tracking and weapon guidance:

• Target and laser spot size. If the target is larger in size than the laser spot, a point on the target
   should be selected to place the spot. The point selected should optimize conditions for spot
   tracking and weapon guidance. The direction from which the attack will be made, the shape,
   angular relationship, and reflectivity of the target surface, must be considered. In situations
   where the laser spot is quite small, the laser must not be aimed into an opening because the
   opening will absorb the reflected energy.

• Target masking or obscurity. If the operator suspects that the target may be partially masked
   from the view of the incoming seeker, he should aim the laser at a point on the target that will
   be within the seeker’s line of sight. In situations where the target is concealed, it may be
   necessary to aim the laser spot at an overhead or nearby object. For example, when designating
   a vehicle under a tree, the laser spot can be positioned in the foliage overhead.

• Target movement. In tracking a moving target, the operator may need to designate a point near
   the target, and wait until the target moves into the designated spot. If the target moves out of
   sight during lasing, the laser spot may be shifted to another target in the vicinity. If the laser
   spot tracker or laser-guided ordnance has already locked on, the spot should be moved slowly
   to the new location. Moving the spot too quickly could interrupt the laser output.

                                                                  11-11


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• Observer survivability. For observer survivability, designation time should be minimized to
   reduce the time available for the enemy to detect, locate, and suppress the designator. The
   operator may lase a spot a designated distance away from the target and walk the spot to the
   target for the terminal portion of the attack (i.e., offset designation). It minimizes laser on-
   target time and requires that the operator be advised of the progress of the attack to ensure that
   the spot is on the target at the proper time. The operator must not move the spot through any
   location that is masked from view by the attacking aircraft or guided ordnance.

• Power sources. Designation time should be minimized to conserve battery life. The life span of
   recharged batteries is variable and often short. A fresh battery should be installed when
   preparing to designate targets.

             Note: Even under vehicular power, it is possible to damage laser
             components by continuous lasing. The duration of continuous designation
             should not exceed one minute.

Designator Operator Positioning Considerations
The laser operator selects a position based on line of sight, cover and concealment, maximum
coverage of operations, and communications requirements. Next, the laser operator determines the
standoff distance. Standoff distance must be properly used when observing enemy avenues of
approach and chokepoints. The vulnerability of the designators must be considered when
designating point targets (e.g., tanks, guns). Although standoff may increase survivability of the
designator, the operator must be aware that the beam divergence at long standoff ranges could
preclude effective point-target designation. The operator should select positions that are near
expected locations of high-priority targets while minimizing risks to friendly forces. The sites
must be coordinated with the positioning of other antiarmor weapons, such as the TOW [tube-
launched, optically tracked, wire-command link guided missile] and Dragon. All antiarmor
weapons must be deployed to ensure mutual support and coordination.

SURVEY CONTROL

The ideal source for all survey information, used to provide observers with accurate grid location and
direction, is the supporting artillery battalion sensor section. The self-location procedures are based
primarily on using known points or the observer’s own directional control. If a known point is a burst
or located by map spot, or if direction is obtained by compass, errors exist and will be passed on to
the observer’s grid and direction. If the elements of survey (i.e., grid location, direction, and
elevation) are not available, laser designators and range finders can be used in conjunction with
the pocket-sized forward entry device, StrikeLink, or AFATDS. The same procedures can be
used during position improvement to improve firing accuracy or extend accuracy into the target area.

Self-Location
The procedures for self-location use different elements of survey to orient the observer. Self-
location can also be employed during position improvement to improve target location accuracy.
Once established, these accuracies can be passed to other observers in the area of operations. The
different procedures have different requirements and provide different elements of survey.

             Note: Both the StrikeLink and AFATDS can perform computations.

                                                                  11-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Trilateration. Trilateration requires the distances and vertical angles to 2 known points that are at
least 300 mils apart and accurately located. Trilateration provides grid location and direction to the
observer. It can be performed with a CLRF. The observer self-locates by determining accurate
distances to two known points. The observer can report these distances to the FDC or enter them
into the StrikeLink to compute location. The observer always specifies the point on the observer’s
left (i.e., lase and announce first).

                              Example: Self-location by using trilateration

     Observer: A16 this is A23, perform trilateration, known points 1 and 2.

     FDC: Reads back.

     Observer: Known point 2 on left: distance 3180, vertical angle +10. Known point 1:
     distance 1230, vertical angle -10.

     FDC: Reads back.

     FDC: Grid 45867 32339, altitude 422. Direction to known point 2:3095 mils.

     Observer: Reads back.

Triangulation. Triangulation requires directions to 2 known points that should be at least 300 mils
apart and provides a grid location without the altitude. If vertical angle is included in the data, the
determined altitude must be verified by at least a map spot. In this case, the observer sends the
directions and vertical angles (optional) for two known points to the FDC or enters them into a
pocket-sized forward entry device. The FDC determines the observer location and provides an
altitude (see figure 11-7).

                Known    Known
                Point 2  Point 1

Direction 1470

                         Direction 2082

                               Operator (Observer)
                                     Figure 11-7. Self-Location by Using Triangulation.

Resection. Resection requires the direction, distance, and vertical angle to one known point. A
back azimuth is determined from the polar information to provide a grid location to the observer.

                                                                  11-13


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Because the observer is only using one point, there must be accurate directional control and
common survey with the FDC for the known point. If not, the errors will be translated into
location errors (see figure 11-8).

                                                                                                                       Known
                                                                                                                       Point 1

                                                                                                        Direction 1328
                                                                                                        Distance 3020
                                                                                                        Vertical Angle +12

                             Operator (Observer)
                                       Figure 11-8. Self-Location by Using Resection.

Using One Known Point and One Burst. If only one known point is available, the second
prearranged point may be established by a planned burst of an HE or WP round. The natural
dispersion in terms of PER and deflection for the projectile will transfer survey errors to the
observer’s location. The operator should plan the location of the burst so that it is separated from
the known point by at least 300 mils. Graze bursts should be used. By using the laser, the operator
ranges the known point and the burst to determine the required direction, distance, or vertical
angle for each of the two points and reports these points to the FDC. The FDC computes the laser
location and corrected azimuth to the known point and sends the information to the operator. The
accuracy of the computed laser location and reference azimuth are affected by the accuracy of the
firing data and must include accurate altitude to the burst point. The FDC should use the most
accurate data available.

Using Known Points. There will be times when an observer will have no pre-established known
points and no readily identifiable terrain feature that can be measured from a map. A second
observer with an accurately located and oriented laser designator can use it to establish known
points for the other observer. To accomplish this, both observers must be able to see a common
area well enough to identify and locate objects to serve as known points for self-location. The
observed angle from the observer being located to the two points being measured must be greater
than 300 mils. Once mutual points have been identified and agreed upon, known points can be as
outlined in the example below. See figure 11-9.

                                                                  11-14


---

                           MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller
                                 Illumination Rounds

Simultaneous                              Simultaneous
Observation                               Observation
Second                                    First
Round                                     Round

                              Greater
                           than 300 mils

              Surveyed OP                 OP Location Unknown

Figure 11-9. Locating Second Observation Point by Simultaneous Observation.

Second Observer Assistance
An observer who has been accurately located and oriented through either survey or self-location
can assist other observers with self-location. The second laser operator can establish known points
for another operator to use in self-location or can perform a simultaneous observation with the
other laser operator on two illumination rounds. To coordinate assistance, the FDC(s) can refer
observers requiring self-location to observers with survey control.

Observer Actions After Being Located
As soon as the observer has obtained an accurate location, it should be recorded on the map. If
provided with direction, the azimuth should be adjusted to the reference point. The polar plot
should then be determined to several prominent points surrounding the position for future use (see
figure 11-10). The StrikeLink or AFATDS can determine the grids to these points for the observer,
who can then establish them as known points. The laser can also assist the observer in preparing a
visibility diagram by ranging objects along selected azimuths. Defilade areas can then be marked
on the map. Once the observer’s position is known to the FDC and accurate directional control has
been established, the observer can fire for effect on targets from that location. This assumes that
the firing unit FDC is firing accurately (see figure 11-10).

                           11-15


---

                                      MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                        Example: Establishing known points

Example 1

FDC: A47, this is A16, establish known points for A23, over.

Observer: Known point 4: tower at grid 45627 78953, direction 0832, distance
5740, vertical angle minus 9. Known point 5: burned tank at grid 45752 74452,
direction 0947, distance 6370, vertical angle minus 11, over.

With two known points established, the observer can now perform self-location
techniques. Any error in the oriented observer’s survey will be transferred to
the obtaining observer’s survey.

Example 2

Observer: A16 this is A47, simultaneous observation with A23, over.

1 round, grid 391 516, one round, grid 374 522, over.

Illumination by round, at my command, over.

Second observer: A47 this is A23, ready to observe, over.

FDC: A47 this is A16, ready, over.

Observer: A16 this is A47, fire, over.

As the illumination round descends, the observer coordinates simultaneous
lasing on the flare and begins tracking the descending flare. The observer
instructs the radio operator to transmit tracking, tracking, tracking . . . lase.
Once the command lase is given, both observers lase/range the flare’s
parachute simultaneously, record the data, then repeat the process with the
second illumination round. The observers must use their judgment to determine
whether they have received an accurate return from the flare’s parachute. If one
of the observers feels that there is an inaccurate return, the tracking phase
should be immediately repeated before any data is sent to the FDC. The
controlling observer sends data to the FDC either as polar data or a converted
grid. The FDC establishes the data as grid locations. The obtaining observer
then sends data to the FDC by using one of the self-location techniques or a
digital message system once the grid locations from the controlling observer’s
data are known.

LASER POLAR MISSIONS

An accurately located and oriented azimuth-determining device and laser range finding capability
provide an accurate target location for first-round fire for effect missions. However, some of the
requirements for accurate first-round fire for effect may be lacking at the firing unit. If the
observer is not sure that first-round fire for effect can be achieved on the target, fire should be
adjusted by using laser polar data.

Fire Direction Center Computed Shift
The following occurs when performing an FDC-computed shift:

             Step 1. The operator sends the laser polar plot data of the burst, which is equivalent to
             a spotting, to the FDC.
             Step 2. The FDC computes the shift, which is equivalent to a correction.
             Step 3. The observer’s data is transmitted as direction, distance, and vertical angle or
             is converted to grid coordinates. If the burst dissipates before it can be lased, the
             observer transmits lost burst to the FDC.

                          11-17


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                          Example: Computed shift

               Burst direction 5872, distance 4350, vertical angle minus 11, fire for
               effect, over.

Observer Computed Shift
If the supporting FDC is not automated, then the observer can compute a subsequent correction or
have the FDC process it manually (see table 11-2).

                                Example: Calculation of shift by observer

               H24 this is H58; new target, direction 0220, distance 3680, vertical
               angle plus 2, over.

               Battalion assembly area, ICM in effect, over.

TARGET SHIFTS

Laser polar missions have the capability to conduct large shifts that are similar to an NSFS fresh
target. If a new target is located, or a shift from an auxiliary adjusting point is employed, the
observer prevents the FDC from applying the new location as a correction to the previous round
by announcing new target. If this is not announced, the FDC will apply the difference between the
previous burst and new location in the opposite direction.

COMMON LASER RANGE FINDER EMPLOYMENT

The CLRF is a lightweight, hand-held laser range finder that can determine the range, azimuth,
and vertical angle of a target quickly and accurately. The device emits a laser burst and detects its
return when the burst is reflected from a distant object. The time lapse between the emission of the
beam and its return is converted to meters and displayed in the left side of the eye piece. The
internal magnetic compass determines the azimuth and vertical angle to the target and is displayed
in the right side of the eye piece. When accurately aimed, the CLRF provides a range that is
accurate to within 5 meters of the target, an azimuth that is accurate to within 10 mils, and a
vertical angle accurate to within 3 mils. However, clutter in front of or behind the target may cause
false readings in the CLRF. To ensure accurate data, the observer should associate the displayed
range with a terrain map analysis and the observer’s own range estimate to decide whether the
reading is accurate. When all of these figures do not correlate, the observer should consider the
information addressed in the following subparagraphs.

                                                                  11-18


---

                   MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                   Table 11-2. Calculation of Shift by Observer.

                                  Procedure                                            Example
Compute the OB distance factor and express it to the nearest          OB distance 3480
1,000 meters                                                          OB factor 3
                                                                      25 x 3 = 75 (left 80)
Determine the deviation shift by multiplying the angular deviation
by the OB factor                                                      Distance to target - 3680
Determine the range shift: OT range–OB range, and express it to       Distance to burst - 3480
the nearest 10 meters

                                                                      Difference             200

Compute a vertical shift if it exceeds 30 meters                      Vertical angle to target +2

Determine the vertical angle difference between the burst and         Vertical angle to burst (-) -1
the target, multiply by the OB factor, and express it to the nearest  Vertical angle difference +3
10 meters                                                             3 x 3 = 9 meters (10 meters)

LEGEND

OB observer-burst

Note: The observer’s correction would be stated as left 80, add 200, fire for effect, over.

Multiple Firings
Three consistent CLRF readings indicate that the observer has aimed in the same place each time.

Minimum Range Set or Multiple Range Measurements
Although the emitted laser beam is relatively narrow, it is wide enough to reflect from more than
one target or object. To mitigate the effects of a cluttered battlefield, the operator can set a
minimum range for the CLRF to read or set the CLRF on multiple range readings.

To prevent false readings from an intermediate object between the observer and the target, the
CLRF is equipped with a minimum range set. The range set indicates the range within which
the CLRF will not register a return. This option eliminates receiving false readings from
intermediate objects and enables the operator to lase through chain-link fences, tall grass, or at
objects partially obscured by an object (e.g., buildings).

Declination Constant Set
The CLRF uses an internal magnetic compass to determine its azimuth measurements to a target.
This allows the operator to set a declination constant that can be stored so that subsequent
readings from the magnetic compass will be displayed in mils grid. Declination constants can be
either positive or negative and are set to the nearest mil.

Target Location
The distance and azimuth provided by the CLRF should always be checked with a quick, but
thorough, map analysis. The observer should remember that the CLRF is designed to help refine
direction and distance. Therefore, the direction and distance determined by the device should
always be correlated with known information before target location is produced.

                                                  11-19


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Distance Measurement. The CLRF provides slant range to a target. This means that if the observer
is on a significantly higher piece of terrain than the target, the range displayed is for the straight
line from the observer to the target, not the distance horizontally along the ground. To compensate
for this, the CLRF has additional capabilities to display not only range, but also the vertical
difference in height between two objects.

Horizontal and Height Difference. The CLRF displays both horizontal distance and the height difference
between the observer and the target simultaneously, which allows the observer to compensate for
vertical shifts that are 35 meters or greater, if necessary.

Distance Between Two Objects. The CLRF also displays the distance between two objects, which
allows the observer to measure a 2D [two-dimensional] object. The observer simply lases the start
and endpoints of the target or area and the reading displayed is the distance between the two
objects. This allows the observer to determine the size of a target and if a different distribution (i.e.,
sheaf) is necessary to cover the target area properly. This can also be useful in determining trigger
distances for moving targets. The observer must be aware that this distance measurement is a
straight line distance and does not compensate for curves in a road or other irregularities.

Horizontal and Vertical Measurement. The CLRF measures the horizontal and vertical measurement of
an object, which allows the observer to measure the dimensions of a 3D object, such as a building
or vehicle. The observer lases one corner of the object and moves up diagonally to the opposite
corner. The readout displays the height and width of the object.

Azimuth and Inclination Measurement. In addition to acting as a laser range finder, the CLRF also
has the ability to provide azimuth and inclination measurements, often referred to as direction and
vertical angle. The CLRF has a digital compass that works similarly to a magnetic compass. Metal
objects and magnetic fields can cause errors in directional readings. Factors influencing azimuth
accuracy include minimum safe distances from objects: 55 meters from power lines; 10 meters
from tanks or vehicles; 2 meters from crew-served weapons; and 0.5 meters from rifles, knives,
watches, and steel helmets. In addition, nonmagnetic batteries should always be used, and the
compass should be recalibrated after every battery change to ensure accuracy.

Azimuth. Azimuth measurements are accomplished by use of the azimuth button and can be
displayed in degrees, mils, and gons. The digital compass may be declinated by inputting the
declination constant for that grid zone. This allows the CLRF to display readings in grid north
versus magnetic north.

Azimuth and Inclinations. Combined azimuth and inclination measurements display both the
direction (i.e., grid or magnetic) and the vertical angle to the object being measured. This data is
displayed simultaneously in the CLRF display. When both direction and vertical angle are
displayed, distance cannot be displayed. If an observer is conducting a laser polar mission, once a
distance measurement has been obtained, another separate measurement for direction and vertical
angle will need to be taken, or vice versa.

Defense Advanced Global Positioning System Receiver. Each CLRF comes with a DAGR. In
addition to its intended use as a GPS receiver, this DAGR has +96 Software loaded onto it. This
allows it to compute a 10-digit grid and elevation to the target lased by the observer. The DAGR

                                                                  11-20


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

must be configured to communicate with the CLRF via its configuration menu before operation
can begin. Once the CLRF is configured and the DAGR is set up properly, the two are connected
by a cable. When the CLRF lases a target (ideally from a tripod or other stable platform) the DAGR
automatically computes and displays a 10-digit grid and elevation to that target. Further display
screens will also display the laser polar data (i.e., direction, distance, and vertical angle) originally
lased by the CLRF. This data can be saved as a planned target in the DAGR. A total of 100
planned targets can be recalled and referenced.

             Note: When connected together, ensure CLRF declination is set to zero and
             DAGR set to grid north to have correct declination output readings during
             operations.
Adjustment of Fire
Lateral and vertical shifts in the adjustment of fire are computed by using the mil-relation formula.
Deviation adjustments are made by using the difference between the azimuths to the burst and
target and applying the mil-relation formula, making the correction in the appropriate direction.
Range adjustments are made by using the difference in range between the target and the burst,
making the correction in the appropriate direction.

                                                                  11-21


---



---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                     APPENDIX A
                OBSERVATION POST

An OP is a position from which military observations are made or from which fire is directed and
adjusted. An OP may be airborne. The selection of an OP is critical to the survivability and the
ability of the observer or spotter to effectively call for and adjust fire.

Observation Post Selection
A good OP must permit observation of the area of operation of the supported unit. The observer
must select an OP with other observers’ OPs and maneuver OPs in mind so as to prevent and/or
minimize gaps or dead spaces. Visibility diagrams should be constructed as time permits. An
OP should:

• Prevent identification by aerial observers or in aerial photographs by loose dirt, wire lines,
   paths and approaches to the position, and communications (e.g., excessive radio traffic,
   detection of antennas).

• Accommodate the establishment and maintenance of communications.
• Facilitate the adjustment of fire when practical (e.g., angle T).
• Enhance survivability through concealment. Concealment is the only protection available in

   the early stages of occupation. As time permits, the observer should improve the OP.
• Have routes of entry and exit without arousing the suspicion of the enemy. The edges of woods

   and villages can be used as entries and exits.
• Make use of elevation provided by crests or trees.
• Avoid landmarks and prominent terrain features because these may be targeted.

When selecting an OP, the observer must consider the characteristics of the forward slope versus
those of the reverse slope:

• Forward slope position:
    Should afford a better view of the front and flanks.
    Cannot be neutralized by fires impacting on the topographical crest.
    Should aid in concealment because the hillside provides background.
    Should not be easily approached during daylight.
    Should have radio communications, but it may be necessary to remote antennas to the
        reverse slope to avoid exposure.
    Does not provide cover from direct fire.

• Reverse slope position:
    Allows greater freedom of movement.
    Facilitates installation, maintenance, and concealment of communications equipment.

                                                                   A-1


---

                          MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

    Affords protection from direct fire.
    Affords only a limited field of view to the front.
    Causes the OP to be neutralized when enemy fire is adjusted on the topographical crest.

Observation Post Preparation
The preparation and improvement of the OP must allow sufficient space for the observer, team,
and equipment (e.g., the laser designator). If the OP is on the ground—

• A trench is usually the first means of protection, but it may be improved by emplacing a cover
   of logs and earth.

• It may be fortified with sandbags as time permits.
• It may be concealed by using both the natural cover afforded by the ground and camouflage.

   The observer must remove the signs of fresh digging. Special precautions must be taken to
   camouflage the cover and openings.

Figure A-1, figure A-2, and figure A-3 provide illustrations of improved OPs.

Tactical Occupation of an Observation Post
When conducting a deliberate occupation of an OP, the observer’s defensive posture may become
permanent and allow the use of many procedures that would normally be omitted during offensive
operations. Many of the procedures used are time intensive techniques that are only possible
during static operations, such as establishing a deliberate OP. If static operations exist, the
observer should focus on security, location, communications, targeting, observation, and position
improvement (SLoCTOP).

Security. A security patrol covering a 6400-mil area with a 500-meter radius should be established
around the surrounding area before occupying the OP. The patrol should consist of one portion of
the C-FST that is task-oriented, with the remainder of the team remaining in a holding position to
monitor radio nets. The observers must ensure that they do not silhouette themselves during the
patrol. They should exercise camouflage, noise, and light discipline during all phases of the OP
occupation.

                          A-3


---

                    MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Ground Concealment  Open for Observation
                               Original Ground Line

                       Logs

                                       Protection
                               from Artillery Time Fire

                          Figure A-3. Observation Post Using a Two-Person Fighting Hole.

Location. The observer should be finalizing the exact observation site while the security patrol is
being conducted. The degree of accuracy for self-location is 100 meters. The position should not
be skylined or easily identifiable as an OP (e.g., topographical crest, 2/3 up, on nondescript high
ground). The location should offer sufficient observation into the enemy operational area or likely
enemy avenues of approach. The observer should prepare an OF fan for use, begin a terrain sketch
by using reference points that are easily identifiable, and develop a visibility diagram in
accordance with appendix B of this publication. Additionally, the observer should ensure that the
current situation map is accurate and contains all current friendly units, known or suspected
enemy positions, and graphical control measures.

Communications. Communications are the number one priority for the C-FST, as they are the most
valuable resource for developing situational awareness during occupation. They must be
established with all required stations as early as possible. It is critical for the radio and/or
telephone operator to record all information that is transmitted (e.g., other missions, position
updates, SALUTE reports) to assist in the smooth transition from a mobile to a static observation
posture. If communications cannot be established or if they are distorted, measures must be taken
to establish and/or improve the situation (e.g., change location, check batteries, improve antenna).

                                                                   A-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Because communications are a necessity, the observer should have access to procedures for field-
expedient antennas.

             Note: HHQ at both maneuver and supporting arms agencies must be
             updated with the most current observer location and other relevant
             information (e.g., enemy situation).
Targeting. The observer should always locate targets by using the most accurate and expedient
means available and include these target locations in the terrain sketch. Laser designators
should be well concealed. All laser designators and range finders should be operating within
safety parameters.
Observation. The team members should be proficient in the recognition of friendly and enemy
forces. The observer should constantly refine company targets and those assigned by HHQ,
identify trigger points and TRPs, review engagement criteria, and ensure that this information is
understood by all team members. The observer should ensure that there is a well-implemented
plan to sustain 24-hour operations.
Position Improvement. The observer should dig-in, including forming a parapet, as time permits.
To ensure that the position is sufficiently concealed, it should be viewed from the enemy’s point of
view. Camouflaging should be continually improved by using items that are organic to the area.
Additionally, units should establish SOPs for the conduct of an OP, including a rotation schedule,
placement of the latrine, and mess procedures.
Position improvement is continuous. Specific areas for concentration are security,
communications, noise and light discipline, camouflage, and weapons and equipment
maintenance.

                     APPENDIX B
                                      100CONSTRUCTION OF THE VISIBILITY DIAGRAM
                                                          200
The observer constructs the visibility diagram in overlay form and drawn to the map’s scale. The
completed visibility diagram is formed by the systematic alignment of terrain profiles. A terrain
profile is an exaggerated view of a portion of the Earth’s surface along a line between two points,
such as the line between the OP and point A in figure B-1. The observer can construct a terrain
profile from any contour-lined map.

                                             100
                         A

                                                                               OP

                            Figure B-1. Profile (Line OP-A) Constructed on a Contour Map.

STEP 1

Draw radial lines from the observer’s location:
• Locate and plot own position on the map.
• Construct radial lines from the OP to the limits of the observer’s visibility.

    Each adjacent pair of radial lines should form an angle of no more than 100 mils (see
        figure B-2).

    Each radial line from the OP represents a line of vision. The OF fan fulfills this function
        when placed on the map and oriented.

                                                                   B-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                                                                                           100 mils

                                                                                                               100 mils

                                                 OP
                                                                                                               100 mils

                                                                                                           100 mils

                   Figure B-2. Radial Lines of Vision Ranging Out From the Observation Post.

STEP 2

Construct the profile. The basic element of the visibility diagram is a terrain profile. Each of the
radial lines represents a terrain profile, and each profile must be constructed. The most common
way of indicating elevation and relief on a map is by contour lines. A contour line is a line
representing an imaginary line on the ground where all points are at the same elevation. Starting at
sea level, normally at zero contour, each contour line is the contour interval, and the value of this
interval is listed in the marginal data on a map.
Starting at zero elevation, every fifth contour line is drawn so that it is heavier than the other
contour lines. The heavier lines are index contours. At some place along each of the index
contours, the line is broken and the elevation is listed. The contour lines falling between index
contours are called intermediate contours. The elevation of any point is determined by doing
the following:
• Determine the contour interval from the map or marginal data.
• Find the numbered contour line nearest to the desired elevation point.
• Determine the direction of slope from the numbered contour line to the desired point.
• Count the number of contour lines that must be crossed to progress from the numbered line to

   the desired point and noting the direction (i.e., either up or down). The number of lines that are
   crossed, multiplied by the contour interval, is the distance above or below the starting value.

                                                                   B-2

                100
Along a radial line, determine both the value of the highest and the lowest contour line (see
                                      100figure B-3) as follows:
                                                          200
• Draw equally spaced horizontal lines on a blank piece of paper so that there will be one line
   representing each contour value crossed by the profile line from the OP.

• Number each line on the paper by assigning the top line a value equal to one contour interval
   higher than the highest contour line determined along the radial line.

• Number the remaining lines in a downward sequence, by contour interval, to the bottom line.
   Assign the bottom line a value of one contour interval lower than the lowest contour line
   determined.

• Draw a vertical line from each contour line intersecting the radial line (i.e., profile line). Extend
   the vertical line to the horizontal line corresponding to the elevation of a contour line.

• Continue this procedure until all contour lines have been connected with their corresponding
   elevations. The result will appear as a series of perpendicular lines connecting points of a
   contour line with a horizontal line of a specified elevation.

• Once all vertical lines have been dropped for contour lines, drop the vertical lines from hilltops,
   ridge tops, streamlines, and valleys (see the broken lines in figure B-4).

• Determine the endpoints of these perpendicular lines by interpolation. If the diagram is being
   drawn at the OP, make allowances for buildings, new construction, newly grown vegetation,
   and other changes to the map.

• Connect the endpoints of all perpendicular lines with a smooth, curved line. When the points
   are connected, the relief of the terrain along the profile line becomes apparent. To determine
   visibility from the terrain profile, draw straight lines from the OP to the lowest points of

                     APPENDIX C
                 CRATER ANALYSIS

Whenever and wherever hostile cannon, missile, or mortar shelling is detected, it must be reported
without delay to the appropriate intelligence officer. The intelligence officer can then evaluate and
act on the information. When counterfire agencies are provided with sufficient information, they
can implement immediate operational objectives to ensure the successful attack of hostile
weapons. Shelling reports (SHELREPs) form the basis for effective counterfire action. The most
reliable, accurate, and informative SHELREPs are visual or electronic observation supplemented
by crater analysis and fragment identification. Observers and liaison personnel should be capable
of conducting crater analysis.

Crater Analysis Procedures
Observers can determine the direction of fire of an enemy projectile with reasonable accuracy by
analyzing its crater or ricochet furrow. Observers can obtain a weapon’s location by plotting the
intersection of the back-azimuth rays from two or more widely separated craters caused by
projectiles fired from the same weapon. Essentially, crater analysis involves determining the axis
of the crater, marking the axis, and measuring the azimuth of the axis to the weapon.

The initial step in crater analysis is to locate a suitable crater for use in determining the direction
to the hostile weapon. The crater should have occurred recently and be clearly defined on
the ground.

Because the crater is the beginning point for plotting direction to the enemy weapon, observers
should determine grid coordinates of the crater as precisely as the time and the method used will
allow. Observers must collect shell fragments to identify the type and caliber of the weapon and
determine direction to the firing weapon by using one of the methods described in this appendix.

Artillery Shell Craters
Fuze Quick Craters. The detonation of a projectile causes an inner crater. The burst and momentum
of the shell carry the effect forward and to the sides, forming an arrow that points to the rear (toward
the weapon from which the round was fired). The fuze continues along the line of flight, creating
a fuze furrow (groove in the ground). There are two methods of obtaining a direction to a hostile
weapon from this type of crater. Observers can obtain the best results by determining a mean or
average of several directions from both methods.

Fuze Tunnel and Center of Crater Method. In this method, one stake is placed in the center of the crater
and another is placed in the furrow at the point where the fuze was blown, forward to the front of
the crater (see figure C-1). A direction-measuring instrument is set up in line with the two stakes,
and the direction is measured to the hostile weapon. There are five steps of the fuze tunnel and
center of crater method:

                                                                   C-1


---

                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Step 1. Place a stake in the center of the crater.
Step 2. Place a second stake in the fuze furrow.
Step 3. Set up a direction-measuring instrument (e.g., compass, CLRF) in line with
the stakes and away from fragments.
Step 4. Orient the instrument.
Step 5. Measure the direction to the hostile weapon.

To Gun       Stake
 Stake              Instrument

                                   Figure C-1. Fuze Tunnel and Center of Crater Method.

A variation of this method is to place a stake where the shell entered the ground instead of in the
center of the crater and determine the direction in the same manner. However, this is rarely possible
because indications of the point of entry are usually destroyed by the explosion of the shell.

Side Spray Method. The side spray method involves bisecting the angle formed by the lines of the
side spray by striking arcs (see figure C-2). The seven steps in measuring the direction of a fuze
quick crater by the side spray method are as follows:

        C-2


---

                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Step 1. Place a stake in the center of the crater.
Step 2. Place two stakes, one at the end of each line of side spray, at equal distances
from the center stake.
Step 3. Hold a length of WD-1 wire to each side spray stake, and strike an arc forward
of the fuze furrow.
Step 4. Place a stake where these arcs intersect.
Step 5. Set up a direction-measuring instrument in line with the stake at the
intersection of the arcs and the center stake.
Step 6. Orient the instrument.
Step 7. Measure the direction to the firing weapon.

To Gun        Stake

                     Measuring
