                                                  Figure C-2. Side Spray Method.

Fuze Delay Craters
There are two types of fuze delay craters: ricochet and mine action.
Ricochet. The projectile enters the ground in a line following the trajectory and continues in a
straight line for a few feet, causing a ricochet furrow. The projectile normally deflects upward
and at the same time changes direction. The change in direction usually is to the right as a result
of the spin or rotation of the projectile. The effect of the air burst can be noted on the ground (see

                                                                   C-3


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

figure C-3). Directions obtained from ricochet craters are considered to be most reliable. The five
steps to determine direction from a ricochet furrow are as follows:

             Step 1. Clean out the furrow.
             Step 2. Place stakes at each end of a usable, straight section of the furrow.
             Step 3. Set up an instrument in line with the stakes and away from the fragments.
             Step 4. Orient the instrument.
             Step 5. Measure the direction to the weapon.

   Stake          Shell Cut Through Grass  Stake

Initial Straight
Portion Used

                                                              Instrument

To Gun                                                Furrow

                  Shell Skid Along Ground,
                     Change of Direction

                   Effect of
                   Air Burst

                                              Figure C-3. Ricochet Furrow Method.

Mine Action. Mine action occurs when a shell bursts beneath the ground. Occasionally, such a burst
will leave a furrow that can be analyzed in the same manner as the ricochet furrow. A mine
action crater that does not have a furrow cannot be used to determine the direction to the weapon.

Mortar Shell Craters
In a typical mortar crater, the turf at the forward edge (the direction away from the hostile mortar)
is undercut (cut beneath with a portion left overhanging). The rear edge of the crater is rid of
vegetation and grooved with splinters (see figure C-4). When fresh, the crater is covered with
loose earth that must be carefully removed to disclose the burned inner crater. The ground
surrounding the crater is streaked by splinter grooves that radiate from the point of detonation.
The ends of the splinter grooves on the rearward side are on an approximately straight line. This
line is perpendicular to the line of flight when on level ground or on slopes with contours
perpendicular to the plane of fire (see figure C-5). A fuze tunnel is caused by the fuze burying

                                                                   C-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

itself at the bottom of the crater in front of the point of detonation. Three methods may be used to
determine direction from a mortar shell crater: main axis, splinter groove, and fuze tunnel.

To Mortar

             Stake
           Instrument

                                                   Figure C-4. Main Axis Method.

Main Axis Method. There are four steps that are taken to determine direction by the main axis
method when a definite and regular crater is formed:

             Step 1. Lay a stake along the main axis of the crater, dividing the crater into
             symmetrical
             halves. The stake points in the direction of the mortar.
             Step 2. Set up an instrument in line with the stake and away from fragments.
             Step 3. Orient the instrument.
             Step 4. Measure the direction to the weapon.

Splinter Groove Method. The five steps to determining direction by the splinter groove method are
as follows:

             Step 1. Lay a stake along the ends of the splinter grooves that extend from the crater.
             Step 2. Lay a second stake perpendicular to the first stake through the axis of the fuze
             tunnel.
             Step 3. Set up an instrument in line with the second stake and away from any
             fragments.
             Step 4. Orient the instrument.
             Step 5. Measure the direction to the weapon.

                                                                   C-5


---

           MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

To Mortar

              Stakes
           Instrument

                                               Figure C-5. Splinter Groove Method.

Fuze Tunnel Method. The four steps that are taken to determine direction by the fuze tunnel method
are as follows (see figure C-6):

             Step 1. Place a stake in the fuze tunnel.
             Step 2. Set up an instrument in line with the stake and away from any fragments.
             Step 3. Orient the instrument.
             Step 4. Measure the direction to the weapon.

Rocket Craters
A crater resulting from a rocket impacting with a low or medium angle of fall is analyzed in the
same manner as an artillery crater resulting from a projectile armed with fuze quick. However, if
the rocket impacts with a high angle of fall, the crater is analyzed in the same manner as a crater
resulting from a mortar round. The tail fins, rocket motor, body, and other parts of the rocket may
be used to determine type and caliber of rocket fired.

Shell Fragment Analysis
The type and caliber of a hostile weapon can be determined by analysis of shell fragments.
Dimensions of the parts, as well as of the complete shell, vary according to the type and caliber of
shell (see figure C-7). The fragments must contain one or more feature that can be matched with
technical specifications of the shell. Such features are mortar fin assemblies, rotating bands, and
fuze wells. Curvature and thickness of large shell fragments can also aid in identification. The
observer should ensure that the data on any munitions that are suspected to be in the area are
disseminated through intelligence channels. Fragments from hostile shells that cannot be

                                                                   C-6


---

           MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

To Mortar

           Stake
             Instrument

                                                 Figure C-6. Fuze Tunnel Method.

identified should be tagged and sent to the intelligence officer concerned. To be of maximum
value, the tag for the fragment should show the following:

• The date and time that the shell impacted.
• The accurate location of the crater.
• The direction that the shell came from and the method used in determining this direction (e.g.,

   survey of crater, sound, flash).
• The name and organization of the person reporting.
• A reference to a previously submitted SHELREP, if appropriate.

Duds and Low-Order Bursts. Logically, a dud would provide the best identification of the caliber of
a weapon. However, because a dud may not always be available (or may be considered too
dangerous to handle) a low-order burst would provide the next best means of identification. When
the explosive filler is incompletely detonated, a low-order burst occurs and large shell fragments
result. The observer can use these large pieces to identify thread count, curvature, and wall thickness.

High-Order Bursts. A high-order burst will normally form into small, deformed fragments. These
fragments are useless for identification purposes unless they include a section of either the
rotating band or the rotating band seat. Fragments of either of these sections will positively
identify the shell because each shell has its own distinctive rotating markings (see figure C-8).

Rotating Bands and Band Seats. The observer may identify a shell by caliber, type, and nation or
other place of origin from the following:

• Pattern or rifling imprints.
• Width, number, and size of rotating bands.
• Dimensions and pattern of keying or knurling (i.e., ridges) on the band seat.
• Dimensions and pattern of keying and knurling impressed on the rotating bands.

Tail Fins. The observer may identify a mortar from the mortar shell fragments and tail fins. The tail
fins are the best indication of type and caliber (see figure C-9). A mortar that is not fin stabilized
may be identified from pieces of the projectile that are imprinted with rifling. Tail fins are often
found in the fuze hole of the crater.

Note: With the exception of rotating bands and band seats on the tail fins,
different types of shells may be identical in one dimension, such as wall
thickness, but seldom will be alike in two or more dimensions. Therefore, it
is possible to make positive identification from two or more dimensions.

Fuzes. Because the same type of fuze may be used with several types of projectiles, it is
impossible to establish the type and caliber of a weapon by examination of the fuze.

Determination of Caliber by Fragment Geometric Analysis
The geometric method of determining projectile caliber is based on the problem of circumscribing
a circle about a triangle. For accurate results, the following considerations apply:

• The fragment selected for use should be the largest available. It should include an arc of the
   projectile circumference, ranging in size from a minimum of 1/2 inch (37mm) for a small-
   caliber weapon to a minimum of 2 inches for a large-caliber weapon.

                                C-12


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

• The fragment must be from that portion of the projectile located between the rotating band and
   the bourrelet. In the case of a nonboat tail (square base) projectile, the fragment may be from
   any portion located between the bourrelet and the base.

• The fragment should not be distorted. However, if the fragment is only slightly distorted, the
   results will be fairly accurate and will give a close approximation of the caliber.

• The approximate caliber of a suitable fragment that meets the above conditions can be
   determined by using the following procedure (see figure C-14):

• Select two points, such as A and B as illustrated in the “I” portion of figure C-14, and place
   them as far apart as possible on the circumference of the projectile fragment.

• Measure the chord (straight line segment) distance between points A and B with dividers, or
   any other suitable instrument, and plot this measurement on paper (I and II, figure C-14).

• Select a third point C, on the arc fixed by A and B, and measure the distance from point A to
   point C. Using this distance as the radius, strike an arc at point A on the paper (II, figure C-14).

• Measure the distance on the fragment from point B to point C. Using this distance as the radius,
   strike an arc at point B on the paper. Mark the intersection of the two arcs. This is the plot of
   point C.

• Draw a triangle using points A, B, and C as the vertices. Erect perpendicular bisectors to the
   sides of the triangle (IV, figure C-14). To construct a perpendicular bisector of a line, set off a
   radius greater than one-half the length of the line. Using this radius, strike arcs (one on each
   side of the line) from each end of the line, and draw a line connecting the two points of
   intersection of the arcs. This line is the perpendicular bisector (III, figure C-14).

• Measure the distance from the point of intersection of the perpendicular bisectors to one of the
   vertexes of the triangle, and multiply this distance by two. The result is the diameter of the
   projectile that produced the fragment.

                                                                   C-13


---

Shelling Reports
A SHELREP is any report of enemy shelling containing information on caliber, direction, time,
density, and area shelled. The information obtained from a crater should be forwarded by the most
rapid means available to the appropriate personnel. The information is transmitted by using the
standard SHELREP, which corresponds to lines a through k of DA [Department of the Army]
Form 2185-R, Artillery Counterfire Information. The information, no matter how incomplete,
should be forwarded. Fragmentary or incomplete information is often of value in supplementing
or confirming existing information.

                                            C-14


---

                                                 MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Items a, b, and k—unit of origin, position of the observer, and friendly damage incurred—are
encoded for security reasons. The current call sign or code name for the unit is in item a, and
codes used to transmit the information are included in items b and k. Item b is not applicable when
this form is used for crater analysis.

Because three elements—direction, dimensions, and curvature—must be measured for crater
analysis, the following equipment is required:

• Measuring instrument (e.g., a compass).

• Dividers and a ruler.

• Curvature template, which is used to measure the curvature of the fragment and so determine
   the caliber of the shell (see table C-1). The template can be constructed of heavy cardboard,
   acetate, wood, or other appropriate material.

• Ammunition data sheets on commonly encountered munitions.

                    APPENDIX D
TARGET ANALYSIS AND MUNITIONS EFFECTS

To ensure that the firing unit employs effective munitions, the observer must provide an accurate
target analysis.

Observer Responsibilities
As the eyes of indirect-fire supporting arms, artillery observers, mortar FOs, and NSFS spotters
must properly describe the target to the firing unit. In addition, the observers must recommend
(i.e., fire request) and NSFS spotters must decide (i.e., fire order) on the best method of attack
based on the size, type, and posture of the target.

Target Analysis
A target must be analyzed to determine its weak points. This determination is based upon where
the target is most vulnerable, which fires will best exploit the target’s weaknesses, and the degree
of damage desired (see table D-1 and table D-2). When analyzing a target, the observer should
consider what effects are sought based upon suppression, neutralization, and destruction.

Suppression. Suppression of a target limits the ability of enemy personnel to perform their
mission. This type of fire is used against likely, suspected, or inaccurately located enemy units
where time is essential. Suppression can be delivered by small delivery units. Various munitions
can be used to cause suppressive results, such as HE or smoke. The effect of suppressive fires lasts
only as long as the duration of fires and does not damage the target.

                Table D-1. Target Characteristics and Considerations.

Target Characteristics                                 Considerations

Composition             Indicates achievable destructive or incendiary effects

Size and shape          May require special sheaf, multiple aim points

Vulnerability           Affects weapon selection

Mobility                May affect weapon/ammunition selection because of required response time

Resiliency              May affect decision to engage

Location                May affect weapon selection because of proximity to friendly forces Accuracy
                        will affect volume of fire to achieve desired effects

                        D-1


---

                   MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

  Environmental     Table D-2. Environmental Characteristics and Considerations.

                   Overhead vegetation reduces effectiveness of some munitions and requires delay fuze action
Jungle areas       Orientation of terrain features in the target area may require repositioning of firing units or high-angle fires
Desert areas       Reserve ICM and HE/VT for firing at personnel on rooftops and targets in the open
Arctic areas       Employ high-angle fires for targets in defilade
                   Use HE/Q or CP for creating rubble and against covered targets
                   Note that HE/delay is not effective for mortar or brick penetration (use HE/Q or CP)
                   Are conducive to illumination and incendiary munitions

                   Require high-angle fires to attack defilade targets
                   Increase lethality of HE in hard, rocky surfaces
                   Reduce effectiveness of WP, ICM, and FASCAM in deep snow and wooded areas
                   Make unobserved fires less accurate in changing atmospheric conditions
                   Enhance air bursts on reverse slopes
                   Allow use of FASCAM to compartmentalize or close routes
                   Are conducive to rock slides
                   Offer opportunity for CAS to attack targets on reverse slopes and steep slopes

                   Restrict employment of bomblet submunitions (e.g., ICM, cluster bomb units) because of overhead cover
                   Promote fires in proximity to friendly forces

                   Increase use of smoke for screening; however, atmospheric conditions reduce duration of smoke screens
                   Slow rates of fire
                   Cause ICM, FASCAM, and HE/Q to be less effective because of deep snow and mud
                   Allow the best results for air bursts
                   Reduce smoke effectiveness

                   D-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Neutralization. Neutralization of a target knocks the target out of the battle temporarily. Personnel
casualties or weapons system damage of 10 percent or more are considered necessary to neutralize
a unit. A damaged weapons system will have significantly degraded performance until repairs are
made. Examples of a neutralized target include a firepower kill, such as a destroyed main sight or
gun, or a mobility kill from a damaged engine or tread. The unit or target can become effective
again when the casualties are replaced and the damage is repaired. The assets required to neutralize a
target vary according to the type and size of the target, the weapon, and/or the ammunition used.

Destruction. Destruction requires a target to be completely reconstituted or replaced. It is defined
differently for units and individual weapons systems. Casualties or system damages of 30 percent
are considered to render a unit destroyed. Destruction of an individual weapons system occurs
when it suffers both a firepower and mobility kill, a crew kill, or a catastrophic kill. With precision
destruction missions, direct hits are used to destroy hard materiel targets.

Ammunition and its Effects
Ammunition and its effects for indirect-fire weapons should consider both projectiles and fuzes.

Projectiles
High Explosives. The HE projectile is a hollow shell filled with TNT [trinitrotoluene] or
Composition B. When detonated by a PD, delay, VT, or mechanical time fuze, the bursts will
cause fragmentation and blast over a wide area. It is most often used by the observer in
adjustment. Shell HE is effective against personnel, vehicles, and material and is fired by mortars,
artillery, and NSFS. The HE/VT, effective against stationary armored vehicles, is delivered from
artillery and NSFS.

White Phosphorous. The WP shell is a bursting, chemical-type projectile. Its hollow shell bursts on
impact by an internal charge that expels burning WP over a limited area. The WP shell is—

• Used for marking, screening, obscuring, and incendiary effects.
• Used against vehicles; petroleum, oils, and lubricants and ammunition storage areas; and

   enemy observers.
• Fired with an impact fuze. However, it may be fired with a fuze timed to obtain an air burst. It

   cannot be fired with a fuze VT. The WP shell can be fired by mortars, artillery, and NSFS.

Illumination. This is a base-ejection projectile. A canister containing burning illuminant is ignited
and expelled in a small parachute. This projectile is used for either battlespace illumination or
marking. Illumination can be fired by mortars, artillery, or NSFS.

Infrared Illumination. This is a base-ejection projectile. A canister containing a burning illuminant is
ignited and expelled in a small parachute. This projectile is used for either battlespace illumination
or marking. Infrared illumination can be fired by mortars, artillery, and aircraft. A night vision
device must be used to see the battlespace when using this projectile.

                                                                   D-3


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Felt-Wedge Smoke. The M825 improved smoke round—

• Is a base-ejection projectile that uses felt wedges impregnated with WP to create uniform
   dispersion of smoke over an area. The M825 provides 5 to 10 minutes of smoke over a large
   area. Unburned wedges can cause a hazard to friendly forces; therefore, caution must be
   exercised when operating in these areas.

• Is delivered by a 155-mm artillery weapon.

Family of Scatterable Mines. The FASCAM artillery shells are used to deliver ADAM or RAAMS
against an enemy force and are delivered by a 155-mm artillery weapon.

Improved Conventional Munitions. The ICM shell is a base-ejection projectile that consists of a
mechanical time fuze and a body assembly containing grenades. When the fuze functions, the
grenades are dispersed over a large area. The current type of ICM in use is the DPICM, which is
produced in two artillery munitions––M483A1, called ICM, and M864, called base-burn ICM.
The DPICM is very effective against exposed troops, vehicles, and armored vehicles. The ICM
projectiles can be fired by artillery and rocket systems.

High Capacity. The high-capacity projectile is a fragmenting round that can also produce penetration
effects. The high-capacity projectile is fired by the 5-inch naval gun. The projectile may have
multiple fuzes, including a nose fuze. The fuzes are—

• A nose plug.
• A PD or mechanical time fuze.
• An auxiliary detonating fuze, which is an intermediate detonating device to magnify the

   explosion of the nose fuze.
• A base detonating fuze with a delayed arming action.

Excalibur. The Excalibur round is a coordinate-seeking projectile that uses a GPS signal to guide
itself onto the target. It is considered a precision-guided munition and is much more accurate than
the standard HE round. It can only be fired from the M777 howitzer.

Fuzes
Fuze Quick. Fuze quick is a PD fuze that functions on impact. It is fired from all indirect-fire
weapons (e.g., artillery, mortars, NSFS) with the HE projectile or WP projectile. Fuze quick loses
its effect if troops are in trenches, on uneven ground, or on earthworks. It is used against personnel
who are either standing or prone on the ground, armored vehicles, and light material.

Fuze Delay. A fuze delay is a 0.05-second delay that can be set on the quick fuze to allow either
ricochet fire or penetration. The observer should use fuze delay for penetration if firing into dense
woods, against light earthworks, or non-masonry buildings. Fuze delay—

• Is not effective against concrete or brick.
• Can be used in conjunction with a ricochet. A ricochet causes a low air burst when there is a

   small angle of impact on a hard surface. Ricochets are particularly common when firing high
   charges in artillery or when firing NSFS.

                                                                   D-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

Mechanical Time. The mechanical time fuze functions in the air at a given time along the trajectory. It
requires adjustment to achieve the proper 20-meter HOB when used with HE or WP. Therefore,
another shell and/or fuze combination should be considered if time is critical and air bursts are
desired. Time fuzes—

• Should never be used for high-angle missions.
• Are used with HE and WP against troops in the open, in trenches, or in fighting holes; vehicles;

   and materiel.
• Can be fired by all artillery weapons and NSFS.
• Are used for payload munitions, such as illumination, smoke, M825 smoke, and ICM projectiles.

Proximity Variable Time. Proximity VT fuzes are radio activated for detonation at a 7-meter HOB.
Various proximity fuzes exist in the NSFS inventory. A VT fuze provides a more effective air
burst than fuze time and does not have to be adjusted. A proximity VT fuze—

• Is an excellent fuze to fire with shell HE for fire for effect missions, unobserved fires, or high-
   angle fires.

• Should be used in missions conducted by an aerial observer when an air burst is desired.
• Is used against all targets that can be attacked with fuze time.
• Can be fired with HE projectiles by all artillery weapons, mortars, and the 5-inch naval gun.

Controlled Variable Time. Controlled VT fuzes have an adjustable arming feature that prevents the fuze
from arming until the set time has elapsed; therefore, it provides more safety when firing over
friendly troops. The CVT fuze is available for NSFS HE projectiles. It is the preferred proximity
fuze for NSFS. The CVT fuze has a 7-meter HOB.

Other Variable Time. Other versions of the VT fuze exist in the NSFS inventory (e.g., VT-RF [variable
time-radio frequency], VT-IR [variable time-infrared]).

Concrete Piercing. Concrete piercing (CP) fuzes are used with artillery and NSFS HE projectiles for
attack of concrete structures, earth, and log-reinforced emplacements. The two types of CP fuzes
are non-delay and delay. Non-delay fuzes are used primarily for spotting or for clearing rubble and
shattering concrete. Delay fuzes are used to destroy a concrete target. Table D-3 provides a guide
for selecting weapons to attack various targets.

                                                                   D-5

                     APPENDIX E
   TIMING THE DELIVERY OF FIRE SUPPORT

An important aspect when requesting and coordinating fire support is the ability to time the
delivery of fires. Techniques such as synchronization, elapsed time, and event timing are used in
Marine Corps operations.

             Note: Other techniques and terminology for timing the delivery of fire
             support (e.g., the running clock) should be avoided because they are
             difficult to establish and often create confusion.

Synchronization
Synchronization is a technique that places all units on a common time. The two types of
synchronization are AN/PSN-11 precise lightweight global positioning system receiver (PLGR)
or DAGR and synchronized clock.

AN/PSN-ll PLGR or DAGR. The preferable device for synchronizing is the AN/PSN-11 PLGR or
DAGR, because it is the easiest and most accurate electronic device available to establish common
time. The PLGR or DAGR must be able to track at least one satellite. An unencrypted PLGR or
DAGR, encrypted PLGR or DAGR, and time cube will all display the exact same time, expressed
in seconds.

Synchronized Clock. The synchronized clock uses a specific hour/minute based on either the
local or universal (i.e., ZULU) time zone, as dictated by the OPORD or unit SOP. The
synchronized clock—

• Requires all units to be placed on an established time and requires periodic time checks.
• Streamlines the coordination of timing once it has been established.

The synchronized clock is established by the senior command element or headquarters and is
disseminated to all units. Dissemination can be accomplished electronically from unit to unit by
voice time checks or acquired as needed from the Naval Observatory. The following example
provides voice transmissions for establishing a synchronized clock.

                                       Example: Voice transmissions

               Sender: T7Y (unit or collective call sign) this is V8P, stand by for time
               check in 2 minutes, time will be 1505, over.

               Receiver: V8P this is T7Y, roger, out. If collective call sign, each unit
               responds.

                                                                   E-1


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

               Sender: T7Y this is V8P, stand by for time check . . . 5-4-3-2-1-hack,
               time is 1505, over.

               Receiver: V8P, this is T7Y, time 1505, out.

                             Note: Specify the time zone if it is different from
                             the unit SOP or OPORD. For example, time 1505
                             SIERRA.

Local Time. A local time zone (e.g., ROMEO time zone) may be used to establish the synchronized
clock. The use of the local time zone on the synchronized clock may cause confusion if the unit or
supporting forces are operating over more than one time zone.

Universal Time. Universal time is based on the ZULU time zone. Use of universal time facilitates
timing of actions across time zones and is the preferred basis for establishing a synchronized
clock. If required, units and agencies can independently access the Naval Observatory, which
provides an automated, continuous broadcast of time. The units and agencies can acquire universal
time from the Naval Observatory on HF radio frequencies of 5.000, 10.000, 15.000, 20.000, or
25.000 MHz. Universal time is particularly advantageous when operations involve joint forces
and/or aircraft operating from remotely located airfields.

             Note: Universal time is determined from observations of the stars, radio
             sources, and also from ranging observations of the moon and artificial
             Earth satellites. The scale determined directly from such observations is
             designated universal time observed (UTO); it is slightly dependent on the
             place of observation. When UTO is corrected for the shift in longitude of
             the observing station caused by polar motion, the time scale UT1 is
             obtained. When an accuracy better than one second is not required,
             universal time can be used to mean coordinated universal time.

Elapsed Time
The delivery of fires may be timed by specifying a number of minutes and/or seconds that are
required to elapse from a stated countdown reference or “hack.” Elapsed time is most desirable
when timing the delivery of fires in an immediate or time-critical situation, when a synchronized
clock has not been established or its accuracy is doubtful. Elapsed time is difficult to disseminate
when several units or agencies are involved (e.g., during a coordinated attack of a target by
aircraft, artillery, or NSFS).

Elapsed time is expressed in relation to the transmission of hack. When starting an elapsed time,
hack is transmitted by specifying the number of minutes and seconds to elapse before ordnance
will impact the target. The hack is used in transmitting the TTT for immediate or on-call CAS.

                                                 Example: Hack

               Eight (minutes understood) plus zero, zero (seconds understood) . . . hack.

                                                                   E-2


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

The term hack is used for all supporting arms and replaces the term mark for use in initiating or
establishing time.
Event Timing
The timing of fires may be in relation to a specific event (e.g, H-hour, H-5, L-hour, L-3).
However, precautions should be taken to ensure that all agencies that are involved in the event are
aware of the correct H- or L-hour.

                                                                   E-3


---



---

                                MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

BUSTER             ordnance release                                                   A/S
(target/object)                                                                       A/A, A/S
CAPTURED           See also CLEARED TO ENGAGE                                         A/S
CHATTERMARK        Fuel state needed for recovery*                                    A/A
CLEARED HOT        No visual contact with FRIENDLY aircraft/ground position
                                                                                      A/S
CLEARED TO ENGAGE  Opposite of VISUAL
                   Fly at maximum continuous speed (military power)
CONTACT            Specified surface target or object has been acquired and is being
                   tracked with an on-board sensor *
CONTINUE           Begin using briefed radio procedures to counter communications
CONTINUE DRY       jamming
                   1. Type 1 and 2 CAS terminal control clearance to release
                   ordnance on this pass

                   2. Training range operations: the range control officer or range
                   safety officer authorizes ordnance release
                   1. Type 3 CAS terminal attack control clearance. Attack aircraft
                   or flight may initiate attacks within the parameters imposed by
                   the JTAC **

                   2. Clearance to fire on designated GROUP/target **
                   1. Sensor information at the stated position

                   2. Acknowledges sighting of a specified reference point (either
                   visually or via sensor)

                   3. Individual radar return within a GROUP or ARM
                   Continue present maneuver, does not imply a change in clearance
                   to engage or expend ordnance
                   Continue present maneuver, ordnance release not authorized

                   Used to provide approval to aircraft to continue the pass without
                   expending ordnance during Type 1, 2, or 3 control (JTAC must use
                   “Type 3, CONTINUE DRY” for dry Type 3 control)

                   Note: Simulated weapons deliveries may be performed.

                   F-1

             Code                            Air Brevity Codes                                 Type of Communication
CYCLOPS
DASH (#)                                              Definition                             A/A, AIR-MAR
                    Any unmanned aircraft
DEADEYE             Aircraft position within a flight                                        A/S, S/S
HOLD FIRE                                                                                    A/S, S/S
HOSTILE             Use if specific call sign is unknown                                     A/S, E/W
                    Laser designator system inoperative
JOKER               An emergency fire control order to stop firing on a designated
LASER ON            target, to include destruction of any missiles in flight *
LASING              A contact identified as enemy upon which clearance to fire is
MAGNUM              authorized in accordance with theater rules of engagement*
(system/location)
NEGATIVE LASER      WARNING: This use of HOSTILE is as a brevity term for air-to-air
NO JOY              and maritime air engagements and should not be confused with
                    the same term in tactical data link identification and rules of
OFFSET (direction)  engagement.
PIG(S)              Fuel state above BINGO at which separation or BUGOUT or event
PLAYTIME            termination should begin
(freq) POGO (freq)  Start/acknowledge laser designation
                    The speaker is firing the laser
PULSE               Launch of FRIENDLY anti-radiation missile
PUSHING
                    Laser energy has not been acquired                                       A/S
RIFLE               Aircrew does not have visual contact with the TARGET/BANDIT              A/A, A/S, S/A
ROPE
                    Opposite of TALLY                                                        A/S
SAUNTER             Maneuver in a specified direction with reference to the target
                    FRIENDLY glide weapon(s) (e.g., joint stand-off weapon [JSOW])           A/A
                    Amount of time aircraft can remain on station, given in hours plus       A/A
                    minutes (e.g., ONE PLUS THIRTY equals 1 hour and 30 minutes)             A/S
                    Switch to communication channel number preceding POGO                    A/S

                    If unable to establish communications, switch to channel number
                    following POGO

                    If no channel number follows POGO, return to this channel
                    Illuminate/illuminating a position with flashing IR energy
                    1. Departing designated point

                    2. (GROUP/descriptions) GROUP has turned cold and will
                    continue to be monitored
                    FRIENDLY A/S missile launch *
                    Circling an IR pointer around an aircraft to help the aircraft identify
                    the FRIENDLY ground position

                    CAUTION: This technique may damage night vision devices.
                    Fly at best endurance

                      GLOSSARY

                               Section I. Acronyms and Abbreviations

AAA................................................................................................................... antiaircraft artillery
ACA .........................................................................................................airspace coordination area
ACE ........................................................................................................... aviation combat element
ACM ................................................................................................. airspace coordinating measure
ADA................................................................................................................... air defense artillery
ADAM ................................................................................................ area denial artillery munition
AFATDS ................................................................ Advanced Field Artillery Tactical Data System
APICM....................................................................antipersonnel improved conventional munition
ASAP .................................................................................................................. as soon as possible
ATO ........................................................................................................................ air tasking order

BBDPICM ............................................ base burn dual-purpose improved conventional munitions
BDA ........................................................................................................ battle damage assessment
BP................................................................................................................................battle position

CAS......................................................................................................................... close air support
C-FST......................................................................................................company fire support team

                                                              Glossary-4


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

                                           Section II. Definitions

add—In artillery and naval fire support, a correction used by an observer/spotter to indicate that
an increase in range along a spotting line is desired. (MCRP 1-10.2)

adjust fire—In artillery and naval fire support: 1. A method of control transmitted in the call for
fire by the observer or spotter to indicate that he will control the adjustment. 2. An order or
request to initiate an adjustment of fire. See also call for fire. (As contained in this glossary, this
term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

adjustment of fire (NATO)—Process used in artillery and naval gunfire to obtain correct
bearing, range, and height of burst (if time fuzes are used) when engaging a target by observed
fire. (Army) — Pertains to mortar fire also. (See also call for fire.) See FM 6-20. (As contained in
this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

air—In artillery and naval gunfire support, a spotting, or an observation, by a spotter or an
observer to indicate that a burst or group of bursts occurred before impact. (As contained in this
glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

airburst—An explosion of a bomb or projectile above the surface as distinguished from an
explosion on contact with the surface or after penetration. (As contained in this glossary, this
term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

altitude—The vertical distance of a level, a point or an object considered as a point, measured
from mean sea level. See also drop altitude; elevation; minimum safe. (DOD Dictionary)

battle damage assessment—The estimate of damage composed of physical and functional
damage assessment, as well as target system assessment, resulting from the application of lethal
or nonlethal military force. Also called BDA. See also combat assessment. (DOD Dictionary)

battle damage assessment—(See DOD Dictionary for core definition. Marine Corps
amplification follows.) The timely and accurate estimate of the damage resulting from the
application of military force. Battle damage assessment estimates physical damage to a particular
target, functional damage to that target, and the capability of the entire target system to continue
its operations. (MCRP 1-10.2)

bracketing—(DOD, NATO) A method of adjusting fire in which a bracket is established by
obtaining an over and a short along the spotting line, and then successively splitting the bracket
in half until a target hit or desired bracket is obtained. See FM 6-30. (As contained in this
glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

close air support—Air action by fixed-wing and rotary-wing aircraft against hostile targets that
are in close proximity to friendly forces and that require detailed integration of each air mission
with the fire and movement of those forces. Also called CAS. See also air interdiction;
immediate mission request; preplanned mission request. (DOD Dictionary)

                                                              Glossary-5


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

correction—Any change in firing data to bring the mean point of impact or burst closer to the
target. A communications procedure word to indicate that an error in data has been announced
and the corrected data will follow. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are
applicable to this publication only.)

danger close—In close air support, artillery, mortar, and naval gunfire support fires, it is the term
included in the method of engagement segment of a call for fire which indicates that friendly
forces are within close proximity of the target. The close proximity distance is determined by the
weapon and munition fired. See also final protective fire. (DOD Dictionary)

designate—A command given to lase a target for a laser-guided weapon. (As contained in this
glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

direct air support center—The principal air control agency of the Marine air command and
control system responsible for the direction and control of air operations directly supporting the
ground combat element. Also called DASC. (DOD Dictionary)

direct fire—Fire delivered on a target using the target itself as a point of aim for either the
weapon or the director. (DOD Dictionary)

down (NATO)—In artillery and naval gunfire support: 1. A term used in a call for fire to
indicate that the target is at a lower altitude than the reference point used in identifying the
target. 2. A correction used by an observer/spotter to indicate that a decrease in height of burst
is desired. (Army) — Includes mortars and direct fire. See FMs 6-20, 7-90, 7-91, 17-12, and 23-1.
(As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

drop—In artillery and naval gunfire support, a correction used by an observer/spotter to indicate
that a decrease in range along a spotting line is desired. (AAP-6) Denotes a shift correction, a
decrease in the observer-target distance. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition
are applicable to this publication only.)

final protective line—A line of fire selected where an enemy assault is to be checked by
interlocking fire from all available weapons. A final protective line may be parallel with, or
oblique to, the front of the position. Also called FPL. (As contained in this glossary, this term
and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

fire direction center—That element of a command post, consisting of gunnery and
communications personnel and equipment, by means of which the commander exercises fire
direction and/or fire control. Also called FDC. (DOD Dictionary)

fire for effect—(DOD) That volume of fires delivered on a target to achieve the desired
effect. (NATO) 1. Fire which is delivered after the mean point of impact or burst is within the
desired distance of the target or adjusting/ranging point. 2. Term in a call for fire to indicate the
adjustment/ranging is satisfactory and fire for effect is desired. Also called FFE. See also call for
fire; destroy; final protective fire; fire mission; neutralize; suppression. (As contained in this
glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

                                                              Glossary-6


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

fire support area—An appropriate maneuver area assigned to fire support ships by the naval
force commander from which they deliver gunfire support to an amphibious operation.
(DOD Dictionary)

fire support station—An exact location at sea within a fire support area from which a fire
support ship delivers fire. (DOD Dictionary)

fuze—A device which initiates an explosive train. (As contained in this glossary, this term and
definition are applicable to this publication only.)

fuze (specify)—In artillery and naval gunfire support, a command or request to indicate the type
of fuze action desired; i.e., delay, quick, time, proximity. (As contained in this glossary, this term
and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

gon—A measurement of plane angles, corresponding to 1/400 of a full circle, thus dividing a
right angle in 100. The unit is also known as the grad, grade or gradian. One gon is equal to 9/10
of a degree or π/200 of a radian. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are
applicable to this publication only.)

graze—In artillery and naval gunfire support, a spotting, or an observation, by a spotter or an
observer to indicate that all bursts occurred on impact. (As contained in this glossary, this term
and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

gun-target line—An imaginary straight line from gun to target. Also called GTL.
(DOD Dictionary)

high-angle fire—Fire delivered at angles of elevation greater than the elevation that corresponds
to the maximum range of the gun and ammunition concerned; fire, the range of which decreases
as the angle of elevation is increased. (Army) Fire delivered to clear an obstacle (such as a hill) that
low-angle fire cannot, or fire delivered to attack targets on the reverse side of an obstacle (such as a
hill) that cannot be attacked with low-angle or direct fire. (As contained in this glossary, this term
and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

immediate smoke—A mission fired, normally by a platoon, to immediately screen or obscure
the enemy and/or friendly positions or movement. (As contained in this glossary, this term and
definition are applicable to this publication only.)

immediate suppression—A fire for effect-type mission, fired normally by battery standing
operating procedures, in which a platoon fires to force the enemy to take cover. (As contained in
this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

indirect fire—Fire delivered on a target that is not itself used as a point of aim for the weapons
or the director. See also direct laying. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are
applicable to this publication only.)

                                                              Glossary-7


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

lateral spread—A technique used to place the mean point of impact of two or more units 100
meters apart on a line perpendicular to the gun-target line. (As contained in this glossary, this
term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

line—In artillery and naval gunfire support, a spotting, or an observation, used by a spotter or an
observer to indicate that a burst(s) occurred along the observer target line. (As contained in this
glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

list of targets—A tabulation of confirmed or suspect targets maintained by any echelon for
informational and fire support planning purposes. (As contained in this glossary, this term and
definition are applicable to this publication only.)

low-angle fire—Fire delivered at angles of elevation below the elevation that corresponds to the
maximum range of the gun and ammunition concerned. (As contained in this glossary, this term
and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

maximum ordinate—In artillery and naval gunfire support, the height of the highest point in the
trajectory of a projectile above the horizontal plane passing through its origin. Also called vertex
height. (DOD Dictionary)

mean point of impact—The point whose coordinates are the arithmetic means of the coordinates
of the separate points of impact/burst of a finite number of projectiles fired or released at the
same aiming point under a given set of conditions. (As contained in this glossary, this term and
definition are applicable to this publication only.)

naval surface fire support— Fire provided by Navy surface gun and missile systems in support
of a unit or units. Also called NSFS. (DOD Dictionary)

observation post—A position from which military observations are made, or fire directed and
adjusted, and which possesses appropriate communications; may be airborne. Also called OP. (As
contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

observed fire fan—A device used to refine an observer’s estimation of distance in the target
area. (Field Manual 6-30)

observer-target factor—The distance from the observer to the target in meters, expressed to the
nearest thousand then divided by one thousand. (for example, the observer-target factor for 6,300
meters is 6). (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this
publication only.)

observer-target line—An imaginary straight line from the observer/spotter to and through
the target. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this
publication only.)

point target—A target of such small dimension that it requires the accurate placement of
ordnance in order to neutralize or destroy it. It is generally a target that is less than 200 meters in

                                                              Glossary-8


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

length and width. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this
publication only.)

projectile—An object projected by an applied exterior force and continuing in motion by virtue
of its own inertia, as a bullet, shell, or grenade. Also applied to rockets and to guided missiles. (As
contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

range spread—The technique used to place the mean point of impact of two or more units 100
meters apart on the gun-target line. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are
applicable to this publication only.)

reduced charge—1. The smaller of the two propelling charges available for naval guns. 2.
Charge employing a reduced amount of propellant to fire a gun at short ranges as compared to a
normal charge. (DOD Dictionary)

reference line—A convenient and readily identifiable line used by the observer or spotter as the
line to which spots will be related. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are
applicable to this publication only.)

reference point—A prominent, easily identified terrain feature to which an observer knows the
direction. (Field Manual 6-30)

refinement—A final correction sent to the fire direction center to move the center of impact to
the adjusted point; always given for any mission. (Field Manual 6-30)

registration fire—Fire delivered to obtain accurate data for subsequent effective engagement
of targets. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this
publication only.)

round—One shot fired by a weapon. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are
applicable to this publication only.)

rounds complete—(NATO) In artillery and naval fire support, the procedural term used to
indicate that the number of rounds ordered at “fire for effect” have been fired. [Note: the Army
definition also applies to mortar fire.] (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are
applicable to this publication only.)

salvo—1. In naval gunfire support, a method of fire in which a number of weapons are fired at
the same target simultaneously. 2. In close air support or air interdiction operations, a method of
delivery in which the release mechanisms are operated to release or fire all ordnance of a specific
type simultaneously. (DOD Dictionary)

sheaf—In artillery, mortar and naval gunfire support, planned planes (lines) of fire that produce a
desired pattern of bursts with rounds fired in two or more weapons. (As contained in this
glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

                                                              Glossary-9


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

shell (specify)—A command or request indicating the type of projectile to be used. (As contained
in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

shelling report—Any report of enemy shelling containing information on caliber, direction,
time, density and area shelled. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are
applicable to this publication only.)

ship adjust—In naval gunfire, a method of control in which the ship can see the target and
adjust the fire. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this
publication only.)

shot—A report that indicates that a gun or guns have been fired. (As contained in this glossary,
this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

splash—In artillery and naval gunfire support, word transmitted to an observer or spotter five
seconds before the estimated time of the impact of a salvo or round. (As contained in this
glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

spotting—A process of determining by visual or electronic observation, deviations of artillery or
naval gunfire from the target in relation to a spotting line for the purpose of supplying necessary
information for the adjustment or analysis of fire. (As contained in this glossary, this term and
definition are applicable to this publication only.)

spotting line—Any straight line to which the fall of shot of projectiles is related or fire is
adjusted by an observer or a spotter. (DOD Dictionary)

spreading fire—A notification by the spotter or the naval gunfire ship, depending on who is
controlling the fire, to indicate that fire is about to be distributed over an area. (As contained in
this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

summit—The highest altitude above mean sea level that a projectile reaches in its flight from
the gun to the target; the algebraic sum of the maximum ordinate and the altitude of the gun.
(DOD Dictionary)

supporting arms—Weapons and weapons systems of all types employed to support forces by
indirect or direct fire. (DOD Dictionary)

suppression of enemy air defenses—Activity that neutralizes, destroys, or temporarily degrades
surface-based enemy air defenses by destructive and/or disruptive means. Also called SEAD.
(DOD Dictionary)

sustained rate of fire—Actual rate of fire that a weapon can continue to deliver for an indefinite
length of time without seriously overheating. (DOD Dictionary)

target number—The reference number given to a target. (As contained in this glossary, this
term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)

                                                             Glossary-10


---

                                                     MCRP 3-10F.2 Supporting Arms Observer, Spotter, and Controller

time of flight—In artillery, mortar, and naval gunfire support, the time in seconds from the
instant a weapon is fired, launched, or released from the delivery vehicle or weapons system to
the instant it strikes or detonates. (MCRP 3-12A)
time on target—The actual time at which munitions impact the target. (DOD Dictionary) The
local time, expressed in minutes after the hour, when the forward air controller desires ordnance
impact on target. Also called TOT. (MCRP 1-10.2)
trajectory—The path of a projectile, missile, or bomb in flight. (As contained in this glossary,
this term and definition are applicable to this publication only.)
Universal Time— A measure of time that conforms, within a close approximation, to the mean
diurnal rotation of the Earth and serves as the basis of civil timekeeping. Also called ZULU time.
(Formerly called Greenwich Mean Time.) (DOD Dictionary)
up—(NATO) In artillery and naval fire support: a. A term used in a call for fire to indicate that
the target is higher in altitude than the point which has been used as a reference point for the
target location. b. A correction used by an observer or a spotter in time fire to indicate that an
increase in height of burst is desired. [Note: the Army definition also applies to mortar fire.] (Army)
Indirect fire, a term used to move the point of aim higher than the previous point of aim, usually
expressed in mils. (As contained in this glossary, this term and definition are applicable to this
publication only.)

                                                             Glossary-11
